\section{From a nonlinear map to a finite-dimensional recurrence}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:algebra}

\subsection{What is taken from Part~I}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:imported}

The manuscript opens by reproving four facts that Part~I proves by the same
arguments; they are used here in Part~I's form.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\arabic*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item A continuous solution $f:\R\to\R$ of $P[f]=0$ with $P(0)\ne0$ is a
strictly monotone homeomorphism (Lemma~\ref{lem:homeo}).
\item $\Ann(f)$ is an ideal of $\R[z]$, generated by $\mu_f$ when it is
nonzero, because $(zP)[f](x)=P[f](f(x))$ uses \emph{right composition}
(Lemma~\ref{lem:ideal}, \eqref{eq:rightcomp}). One must not distribute $f$
over a linear combination of its values.
\item Every bilateral sequence annihilated by $A(E)$, $A(0)\ne0$, has a unique
exponential-polynomial expression $u(n)=\sum_\lambda p_\lambda(n)\lambda^n$
with $\deg p_\lambda<m_A(\lambda)$, and its minimal recurrence polynomial is
$\prod_{p_\lambda\ne0}(z-\lambda)^{1+\deg p_\lambda}$
(Lemma~\ref{lem:recurrence}).
\item $\mu_f$ is the least common multiple of the minimal recurrence
polynomials of the orbits $u_x(n)=\itn{f}{n}(x)$, $n\in\Z$, and finitely many
orbits attain every multiplicity (Lemma~\ref{lem:lcm}).
\end{enumerate}
The manuscript adds two observations. For a homeomorphism, a factor $z$ could
be cancelled from an annihilator by surjectivity; consequently
$\mu_f(0)\ne0$. And if $E$ acts on functions by $Ev=v\circ f$, then on the
finite-dimensional cyclic space generated by $\id$ it is an invertible linear
operator whose cyclic-vector minimal polynomial is $\mu_f$ (compare the remark
after Lemma~\ref{lem:lcm}); the same letter $E$ denotes the shift
$n\mapsto n+1$ on sequences, because evaluation intertwines the two actions.
The nonlinear nature of $f$ as a real map does not interfere with this linear
action on functions.

\subsection{Why exactly one factor at one disappears}

\begin{lemma}[Difference-spectrum identity]
\label{nrr:sc:lem:difference}
For a homeomorphism with minimal polynomial $\mu$, the function
$f-\id$ has minimal polynomial $\scD\mu$ under right composition by $f$.
Equivalently, $\scD\mu$ is the least common multiple of the minimal
recurrence polynomials of
\begin{equation}
 d_x(n)=\itn{f}{n+1}(x)-\itn{f}{n}(x).
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:increments}
\end{equation}
If $f=\id$, the difference function is zero and its monic annihilator
is the constant polynomial~$1$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The difference function is $(E-1)\id$. A polynomial $Q$ annihilates it
if and only if $\mu\mid(z-1)Q$. The least monic such $Q$ is
$\mu/\gcd(\mu,z-1)$, which is precisely $\scD\mu$.
Evaluation at every point gives the scalar formulation.
\end{proof}

For increasing $f$, each nonzero sequence $d_x$ has one strict sign for
all $n\in\Z$. Apply $\itn{f}{n}$ to the strict inequality
$f(x)>x$ or $f(x)<x$. This sign property is the bridge between dynamics
and positive recurrence spectra. (Part~I uses the same increments
\eqref{eq:increments} on the unit circle only.)

\section{Positive bilateral recurrences: a complete spectral test}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:positive}

\subsection{The elementary oscillation argument}

A finite trigonometric sequence is a finite sum
$T(n)=\sum_{\zeta\in S}c_\zeta\zeta^n$ with distinct $|\zeta|=1$.
Two elementary facts will be used.

\begin{lemma}
\label{nrr:sc:lem:trig}
There exist integers $N_k\to+\infty$ such that
$\zeta^{N_k}\to1$ for every $\zeta\in S$.
If a real trigonometric sequence satisfies
$\liminf_{n\to+\infty}T(n)\ge0$, then $T(n)\ge0$ for all $n\in\Z$.
If such a sequence is nonzero and nonnegative, its constant-frequency
coefficient is strictly positive.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The first assertion, and the returns $T(N_k+\ell)\to T(\ell)$ and
$T(-N_k+\ell)\to T(\ell)$ in both time directions, are
Lemma~\ref{lem:returns}; the second assertion follows from them exactly as in
the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:positive}; the third is
Lemma~\ref{lem:mean}. The manuscript proves all three by the same
compactness and Ces\`aro-mean arguments.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[Necessary extreme-circle conditions]
\label{nrr:sc:prop:positive-necessary}
The minimal polynomial of a nonzero nonnegative real bilateral
recurrence sequence is positive-admissible.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Let $r_+$ be the greatest active root modulus and $d_+$ the greatest
polynomial degree on that circle. As $n\to+\infty$,
\[
 u(n)=r_+^n n^{d_+}\bigl(T_+(n)+o(1)\bigr),
\]
where $T_+$ is the nonzero trigonometric sequence made from the leading
coefficients on that circle. Positivity and Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:trig} show
that $T_+\ge0$ and its constant frequency is positive. Thus the root
$r_+$ occurs with degree $d_+$, giving maximal multiplicity.

At $n\to-\infty$, the least active modulus $r_-$ dominates instead.
Apply the same argument to $u(-m)$ as $m\to+\infty$. The positive root
$r_-$ must again attain the greatest multiplicity on its circle.

If there is only one circle, let $d$ be its maximum degree and $T$ its
leading trigonometric sequence. Positive time gives $T(\ell)\ge0$;
negative time, using the same simultaneous returns, gives
$(-1)^dT(\ell)\ge0$ for all integers $\ell$. Since $T\ne0$, $d$ is even.
The positive-root multiplicity $d+1$ is therefore odd. (This paragraph is
the argument of Theorem~\ref{thm:positive}.)
\end{proof}

The two distinct extreme circles use different leading coefficients,
so there is no parity relation between them. A parity condition imposed
separately on every extreme circle would be false.

\subsection{A positive analytic interpolation with any allowed spectrum}

\begin{lemma}[Two-ended positive envelope]
\label{nrr:sc:lem:envelope}
Let $a<b$ and let $d,e$ be arbitrary nonnegative integers.
For sufficiently large $\Lambda>0$, the function
\begin{equation}
 G(t)=e^{at}(\Lambda-t)^d+e^{bt}(\Lambda+t)^e
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:envelope}
\end{equation}
is strictly positive on $\R$ and satisfies
\begin{equation}
 cW(t)\le G(t)\le CW(t),\qquad
 W(t)=e^{at}(1+|t|)^d+e^{bt}(1+|t|)^e,
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:W}
\end{equation}
for some constants $c,C>0$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
On $[-\Lambda,\Lambda]$ both summands are nonnegative and they do not vanish
simultaneously. For $t\ge\Lambda$, the second summand is positive.
The first can be negative only when $d$ is odd, and then the ratio of
its absolute value to the second is bounded by
\[
 e^{-(b-a)t}t^{d-e}.
\]
Choose $\Lambda\ge1$ large enough that this bound is at most $1/2$ for all
$t\ge\Lambda$. At $t\le-\Lambda$, the first summand is positive, and a
potentially negative second summand is controlled by
$e^{-(b-a)|t|}|t|^{e-d}$. Enlarge $\Lambda$ to make that bound at most $1/2$.
This proves positivity everywhere.
At $-\infty$, $G/W\to1$; the same is true at $+\infty$.
A strictly positive continuous quotient has positive minimum and finite
maximum on the remaining compact interval, giving \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:W}.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Positive recurrence realization]
\label{nrr:sc:thm:positive-realization}
A nonconstant monic real polynomial $A$ with $A(0)\ne0$ is
positive-admissible if and only if it is the minimal polynomial of a
strictly positive bilateral recurrence sequence. Moreover, every such
$A$ is the minimal shift polynomial of a strictly positive real-analytic
exponential polynomial $g:\R\to(0,\infty)$ such that
\begin{equation}
 0<c\le\frac{g(t+1)}{g(t)}\le C<\infty\quad(t\in\R).
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:ratio}
\end{equation}
Here a shift polynomial annihilates a function of $t$ under $t\mapsto t+1$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Necessity is Proposition~\ref{nrr:sc:prop:positive-necessary}.
For sufficiency, first suppose there are at least two root moduli.
Put $a=\log r_-$, $b=\log r_+$,
$d=m_A(r_-)-1$, and $e=m_A(r_+)-1$.
Use $G$ from Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:envelope} as the two positive-real-root
parts.

For every remaining real root or conjugate pair, choose the representative
$\lambda=\rho e^{i\theta}$ with $0\le\theta\le\pi$ and insert the real term
\begin{equation}
 H_\lambda(t)=e^{(\log\rho)t}t^{m_A(\lambda)-1}\cos(\theta t).
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:mode}
\end{equation}
For positive real roots $\theta=0$; for negative ones $\theta=\pi$.
On an extreme circle, the degree condition bounds $|H_\lambda|/W$.
For a strictly interior radius, exponential separation at both ends
bounds the same quotient, regardless of polynomial degree.
Thus, with $H=\sum H_\lambda$, there is $M<\infty$ such that $|H|\le MW$.
Choose a nonzero positive $\varepsilon$ small enough that
$g=G+\varepsilon H\ge cW/2$.
All intended leading mode coefficients are nonzero, and no new mode
has been introduced.

If there is only one radius $r$, write $a=\log r$ and
$d=m_A(r)-1$. The admissibility condition makes $d$ even. Start instead
with
\[
 G(t)=e^{at}(1+t^2)^{d/2}.
\]
Each remaining term \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:mode} has polynomial degree at most $d$,
so a sufficiently small nonzero perturbation again stays between two
positive multiples of $G$.

To justify exact minimality under a real shift, write cosine terms as
pairs of complex exponentials. On $e^{st}p(t)$, $E-e^s$ lowers polynomial
degree by one. Distinct continuous exponents give independent functions.
The exponents $s=\log\rho\pm i\theta$ have $e^s=\lambda,\overline\lambda$;
when $\theta=\pi$ both give the same negative root, but their leading
coefficient functions cannot cancel identically. The maximal degree
in that primary component is still $m_A(\lambda)-1$.
It follows that the minimal shift polynomial is exactly $A$.

The weights $W(t+1)/W(t)$ are bounded above and away from zero:
$1+|t+1|$ and $1+|t|$ differ by factors between $1/2$ and $2$, and the
exponential factors acquire fixed multipliers. The same assertion holds
for the single-circle weight. Comparability gives \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:ratio}.

Finally, for each root the leading coefficient of the sampled sequence
$g(t_0+n)$ is a nonzero real-analytic function of $t_0$ (or a nonzero
complex-valued analytic function). Avoid the finitely many zero sets.
Then $g(t_0+n)>0$ has minimal polynomial $A$.
\end{proof}

The last generic-sampling step matters for negative roots. An
antiderivative can contain $\sin(\pi t)$, which vanishes at every integer
although it is not the zero function. Exact minimality must be proved
as a function of the continuous coordinate, or at a nonsingular sampling
phase, not inferred carelessly from phase zero.

\section{Proof of the increasing-spectrum classification}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:classification}

\subsection{Necessity for a family of orbit increments}

\begin{lemma}
\label{nrr:sc:lem:lcm-positive}
The least common multiple of finitely many positive-admissible
polynomials is positive-admissible.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Take any root at an extreme circle of the least common multiple, with
its attained maximal multiplicity. In a factor attaining it, this circle
is also extreme; that factor supplies the positive root with at least
that multiplicity. If the union has only one circle, every factor has
that circle, and the maximum of its positive-root multiplicities is the
maximum of finitely many odd integers, hence is odd.
\end{proof}

Suppose $f\ne\id$ is increasing with minimal polynomial $\mu$.
By Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:difference}, $\scD\mu$ is the least common multiple
of the nonzero increment-orbit minimal polynomials, using finitely many
orbits. Multiply each increment sequence by its constant sign to make
it positive. Proposition~\ref{nrr:sc:prop:positive-necessary} and
Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:lcm-positive} show that $\scD\mu$ is
positive-admissible. For $f=\id$ the minimal polynomial is $z-1$.
This proves the necessity in Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:main}.

\subsection{Integrating the positive coordinate}

Assume $A=\scD\mu$ is positive-admissible. Construct $g>0$ as in
Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:positive-realization}. We choose a real-analytic
antiderivative $h$ whose minimal shift polynomial is exactly $\mu$.

For a nonzero continuous exponent $s$, a canonical antiderivative is
\begin{equation}
 \int e^{st}p(t)\,dt
 =e^{st}\sum_{j=0}^{\deg p}\frac{(-1)^jp^{(j)}(t)}{s^{j+1}}.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:primitive}
\end{equation}
It preserves polynomial degree and hence the shift-root multiplicity.
For the frequency $s=0$, ordinary integration increases polynomial
degree by one. Apply this term by term, respecting complex conjugation.
If $1$ is a root of $A$, the multiplicity at $1$ increases by one, as
required. If $1$ is absent from $A$ but present in $\mu$, add a nonzero
constant, say $1$. If $1$ is absent from $\mu$, add no constant.
Thus $h'=g>0$ and its exact shift polynomial is $\mu$.

Let $(\alpha,\beta)=h(\R)$, with infinite endpoints permitted.
Define on this interval
\begin{equation}
 F(h(t))=h(t+1).
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:conjugacy}
\end{equation}
The analytic inverse function theorem makes $F$ real analytic there,
and
\begin{equation}
 F'(h(t))=\frac{g(t+1)}{g(t)}.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:Fprime}
\end{equation}
Consequently it is bi-Lipschitz with constants uniform on the interval.
Also $P[F](h(t))=P(E)h(t)$ for every polynomial $P$, proving exact
minimality on that interval.

\subsection{The range and extension through a finite endpoint}

Write $r_-$ and $r_+$ for the extreme root moduli of $A$.
The envelope asymptotics give the following exhaustive possibilities:
\begin{equation}
 h(\R)=
 \begin{cases}
 \R,&r_-\le1\le r_+,\\
 (c,\infty),&1<r_-,\\
 (-\infty,c),&r_+<1.
 \end{cases}
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:range}
\end{equation}
Indeed, at an endpoint with exponential rate zero, $g$ has a nondecaying
polynomial envelope and is not integrable. A positive rate is integrable
only at $-\infty$, and a negative rate only at $+\infty$.
The extreme rates are ordered, so both endpoints cannot be finite.

When the range is $\R$, take $f=F$. It has no fixed point because
$h(t+1)>h(t)$. In a half-line case, set $f(c)=c$ and extend by reflection:
\begin{equation}
 f(2c-x)=2c-F(x)
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:reflection}
\end{equation}
for $x$ in the original half-line. The limiting value of $F$ at $c$ is
$c$, since both $h(t)$ and $h(t+1)$ tend there.
This extension is an increasing homeomorphism. Its bi-Lipschitz bounds
hold on each side and pass across $c$ by addition.
It is analytic away from $c$.

If $\mu(1)\ne0$, our choice of primitive gives $c=0$.
If $\mu(1)=0$, the constant contribution $c$ is annihilated.
Thus in both cases $\mu(1)c=0$. Iterating \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:reflection} gives
\[
 \mu[f](2c-x)=2c\mu(1)-\mu[F](x)=0.
\]
The value at $c$ satisfies the same equation. Minimality on the original
half-line forces minimality globally. This proves sufficiency and all
regularity claims of Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:main}.

\begin{remark}[What is not being claimed about analyticity]
The construction is globally real analytic when its coordinate covers
$\R$. In a half-line case the reflected map need not be differentiable
at $c$. No complex-entire inverse is used, and no global complex
analyticity is asserted. These distinctions avoid an otherwise incorrect
regularity upgrade at an accumulating fixed point.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Added in the merge: one circle recovers Part~I]
\label{nrr:sc:rem:circular}
When all roots of $\mu$ lie on one circle $|z|=r$,
Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:main} is Theorem~\ref{thm:classification}. If
$r\ne1$, then $\scD\mu=\mu$ has one circle, and positive admissibility says
that $r$ is a root of odd multiplicity at least every other multiplicity:
condition~(i) of Theorem~\ref{thm:classification}. If $r=1$ and
$\mu\ne z-1$, then $\scD\mu$ is nonconstant and lies on the unit circle; it
is positive-admissible exactly when $1$ is a root of $\scD\mu$ of odd
multiplicity $m_\mu(1)-1\ge1$ that is at least every other multiplicity,
that is, when $m_\mu(1)$ is even, at least $2$, and exceeds every other
multiplicity: condition~(ii). The realizations agree in kind as well:
by Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:fixedfree} below, for $\mu\ne z-1$ on one circle
a fixed-point-free realization exists exactly when $r=1$, in accordance with
Section~\ref{sec:classification} (every realization fixes $0$ when $r\ne1$
and has no fixed point when $r=1$). As a finite check of the implementation, the manuscript's
\texttt{realizable} test (shipped as
\nolinkurl{code/02-spectral-core-spectral_core.py}) was compared in the write
phase with the conditions of Theorem~\ref{thm:classification} on 638
single-circle spectra: radii $2$ and $1$; the roots $\pm r$, the pair
$\pm ri$ and the pair $r(3\pm4i)/5$; the positive root with multiplicity
$0$ to $4$ and the other three with multiplicity $0$ to $3$, the empty
spectrum excluded ($2\times319$ cases). There were no disagreements. The
comparison script is not shipped; the grid above specifies it completely.
\end{remark}

\section{The exact universal spectral core}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:core}

\subsection{A root-by-root formula}

Normalize $P$ to be monic, which does not change its solution set.
First define a positive-recurrence core $\pcore(B)$ for any monic real
$B$ with $B(0)\ne0$.

If $B$ has no positive real root, set $\pcore(B)=1$.
If its only distinct positive root is $r$, put
\begin{equation}
 \kappa=2\left\lfloor\frac{m_B(r)-1}{2}\right\rfloor+1,
 \qquad
 \pcore(B)=\prod_{|\lambda|=r}
       (z-\lambda)^{\min(m_B(\lambda),\kappa)}.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:onecore}
\end{equation}
Thus $\kappa$ is the largest odd integer not exceeding $m_B(r)$.

If there are at least two distinct positive roots, let $b_-<b_+$ be the
least and greatest such roots. Define
\begin{equation}
 k_B(\lambda)=
 \begin{cases}
 0,&|\lambda|<b_-\text{ or }|\lambda|>b_+,\\
 \min(m_B(\lambda),m_B(b_-)),&|\lambda|=b_-,\\
 m_B(\lambda),&b_-<|\lambda|<b_+,\\
 \min(m_B(\lambda),m_B(b_+)),&|\lambda|=b_+,
 \end{cases}
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:annularcore}
\end{equation}
and set $\pcore(B)=\prod_\lambda(z-\lambda)^{k_B(\lambda)}$.
Conjugate roots receive identical multiplicities, so these are real
polynomials.

\begin{definition}[Increasing spectral core]
\label{nrr:sc:def:core}
For monic $P$ with $P(0)\ne0$, define
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\quad\core(P)=(z-1)^{\delta(P)}\pcore(\scD P).\quad}
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:core}
\end{equation}
The value $\core(P)=1$ means that no increasing solution exists.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}
\label{nrr:sc:lem:largest-positive}
Every positive-admissible divisor of $B$ divides $\pcore(B)$.
When $\pcore(B)\ne1$, it is itself positive-admissible.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
For an admissible divisor, its extreme root moduli are positive roots
of $B$. Thus all its roots lie between the least and greatest positive
roots of $B$. At a boundary of that full interval, its multiplicities
cannot exceed the positive-root multiplicity there. This gives all bounds
in \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:annularcore}.
If $B$ has just one positive root, the divisor has only that radius,
and its positive-root multiplicity is odd. Formula \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:onecore}
is exactly the largest permitted cap.
Conversely the displayed cores satisfy the defining extreme and parity
conditions directly.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Sharp universal order reduction]
\label{nrr:sc:thm:core}
For every real $P$ with $P(0)\ne0$, the polynomial $\core(P)$ satisfies:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)},leftmargin=2.4em]
\item $\core(P)\mid P$, and for continuous increasing maps on $\R$,
$P[f]=0$ if and only if $\core(P)[f]=0$.
\item Increasing solutions exist if and only if $\core(P)\ne1$;
equivalently, if and only if $P$ has a positive real root.
\item When solutions exist, there is a globally bi-Lipschitz increasing
map with minimal polynomial exactly $\core(P)$.
\item For every real polynomial $Q$, the universal implication
\eqref{nrr:sc:eq:universal} holds exactly when $\core(P)\mid Q$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Divisibility in (i) follows from the construction. Suppose $P[f]=0$
and let $\mu=\mu_f$, so $\mu\mid P$. The operation $\scD$ preserves
divisibility: at the root $1$ it replaces multiplicity $m$ by
$\max(m-1,0)$, a monotone function of $m$.
If $f\ne\id$, the classification makes $\scD\mu$ admissible.
Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:largest-positive} gives
$\scD\mu\mid\pcore(\scD P)$. Restoring the possible one copy of $z-1$
yields $\mu\mid\core(P)$, hence $\core(P)[f]=0$.
For $f=\id$, the factor $z-1$ in $P$ and in $\core(P)$ gives the same result.
The reverse implication follows because $\core(P)$ divides $P$.

If $\core(P)\ne1$ but $\pcore(\scD P)=1$, then $\core(P)=z-1$,
realized by the identity. Otherwise
$\scD\core(P)=\pcore(\scD P)$: when $P$ has no root at one, neither does
its positive core. Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:main} gives (iii).
If $\core(P)=1$, (i) would force $\id=0$, impossible on $\R$.
A positive root $r$ supplies the solution $f(x)=rx$, and the formula has
no nontrivial value without a positive root. This proves (ii).

If $\core(P)\mid Q$, every solution of the reduced equation satisfies
$Q[f]=0$. Conversely, apply any proposed universal relation to the map
in (iii); its minimal polynomial forces $\core(P)\mid Q$.
When there are no solutions, the implication is vacuous and $1$ divides
every $Q$, so (iv) remains valid.
\end{proof}

Equivalently, the ideal of common iterative relations of the increasing
solution class is
\begin{equation}
 \bigcap_{\substack{f:\,P[f]=0\\ f\text{ continuous increasing}}}\Ann(f)
       =(\core(P)).
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:commonideal}
\end{equation}
The intersection over an empty class is the full polynomial ring.
This is stronger than merely exhibiting some eliminable roots: it
identifies the exact obstruction to \emph{every} further universal reduction.

\subsection{Consequences for boundary and interior roots}

When $\scD P$ has two distinct positive roots, every root strictly between
the smallest and largest positive radii survives at its full multiplicity
in the universal core. At a boundary, a nonreal or negative root survives
up to the multiplicity of the positive root there. It need not disappear.
When only one positive root remains, the additional odd cap is exact.
The unit root affects these conclusions only through the preliminary
one-step multiplicity reduction, but omitting that step changes answers.

The core is idempotent:
\begin{equation}
 \core(\core(P))=\core(P).
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:idempotent}
\end{equation}
It also respects divisibility: $P\mid Q$ implies
$\core(P)\mid\core(Q)$. This follows either from the sharp universal
property or by observing that realizable divisors of $P$ are realizable
divisors of $Q$.

\section{An exact answer to increasing affine rigidity}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:rigidity}

The distinction between a polynomial being the minimal polynomial of
\emph{an affine map} and forcing \emph{all maps} to be affine cannot be
ignored. A small piecewise-linear example is decisive.

\begin{theorem}[Affine and homogeneous-linear rigidity]
\label{nrr:sc:thm:rigidity}
Assume that $P$ has at least one increasing continuous solution on $\R$.
Then all such solutions are affine if and only if
\begin{equation}
 \core(P)=z-r\quad\text{for some }r>0,
 \qquad\text{or}\qquad \core(P)=(z-1)^2.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:affine}
\end{equation}
All such solutions are homogeneous linear if and only if
$\core(P)=z-r$ for some $r>0$.
In that case the unique increasing solution is $f(x)=rx$.
When $\core(P)=(z-1)^2$, the solutions are exactly the translations
$f(x)=x+c$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
A reduced polynomial $z-r$ forces $f=r\id$.
If $(z-1)^2[f]=0$, the argument in the proof of
Corollary~\ref{cor:unitrigidity} (each bilateral orbit is
$\itn{f}{n}(x)=x+n(f(x)-x)$, and order preservation for every $n\in\Z$
forces the coefficient of $n$ in $(y-x)+n\bigl((f(y)-y)-(f(x)-x)\bigr)>0$ to
vanish) shows that $f(x)-x$ is constant, and every translation does satisfy
the equation. The manuscript gives the same argument.

For necessity, let $R=\core(P)$ and use a realization with $\mu_f=R$.
An increasing affine map $ax+b$ has minimal polynomial
$z-a$ if $b=0$, $(z-1)^2$ if $a=1,b\ne0$, and
$(z-1)(z-a)$ if $a\ne1,b\ne0$.
Thus unless $R$ has one of these forms, the realization is nonaffine.
The remaining case $R=(z-1)(z-r)$ with $r>0$, $r\ne1$, also fails
universal affine rigidity. Indeed
\begin{equation}
 f(x)=\begin{cases}x,&x\le0,\\rx,&x\ge0\end{cases}
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:piecewise-rigidity}
\end{equation}
is increasing and continuous, satisfies $R[f]=0$, and is not affine.
This completes the affine classification.
Finally nonzero translations are not homogeneous linear, proving the
last assertions.
\end{proof}

The piecewise-linear mechanism is already visible in Bogdanov's comment
on Petrov's question \cite{Petrov2016}; it is not claimed as a new
construction. What the present result adds is an exact test for arbitrary
input spectra, including multiplicities and nonreal roots, through
$\core(P)$. The unrestricted version of Petrov's question includes decreasing
maps and is not declared solved here.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch~42: decreasing maps are decided in Part~III,
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:affine} and Remark~\ref{nrr:dc:rem:petrov}; the
version with $P(0)=0$ remains open.]

\begin{remark}[Added in the merge: the circular rigidity criteria]
\label{nrr:sc:rem:rigidity-recovers}
For $P$ supported on one circle, Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:rigidity} gives
Corollaries~\ref{cor:nonunitrigidity} and~\ref{cor:unitrigidity}. If
$r\ne1$ and $r$ is a root, then $\core(P)=\pcore(P)$ is given by
\eqref{nrr:sc:eq:onecore}, and it equals $z-r$ exactly when $\kappa=1$ and
$P$ has no other root, that is, $P=(z-r)^m$ with $m\in\{1,2\}$. If $r=1$
and $M=m_P(1)\ge1$, then $\core(P)=z-1$ exactly when $\scD P$ has no
positive root, that is, $M=1$; and $\core(P)=(z-1)^2$ exactly when
$\pcore(\scD P)=z-1$, that is, $P=(z-1)^M$ with $M-1\in\{1,2\}$. By
Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:core}(ii), $M=0$ (or $r$ not a root) leaves no
increasing solution. These are the statements of the two corollaries.
\end{remark}

\section{Which spectra admit fixed points?}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:fixed}

\subsection{Finite limits eliminate incompatible modes}

\begin{lemma}
\label{nrr:sc:lem:limits}
Suppose a real recurrence sequence $u(n)$ has a finite limit as
$n\to+\infty$. Its active roots are either of modulus less than $1$,
or a simple root at $1$. Its $1$-component equals the limit.
With a finite limit as $n\to-\infty$, replace ``less'' by ``greater''.
A recurrence sequence with finite limits at both ends is constant.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
A greatest-modulus, greatest-degree normalization, as in
Proposition~\ref{nrr:sc:prop:positive-necessary}, shows that a nonzero
component with modulus greater than one cannot remain bounded: a nonzero
finite trigonometric leading term returns away from zero along a subsequence.
Apply this successively to remove the finitely many growing levels.
On the unit circle, positive polynomial degree is likewise impossible
under boundedness. A degree-zero trigonometric sequence having a limit
must be constant: its difference from that limit tends to zero, and the
simultaneous returns in Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:trig} make it identically zero.
The negative-time claim follows by replacing $n$ by $-n$.
The intersection of the two permitted supports is the simple constant mode.
\end{proof}

\begin{proposition}[The fixed set is an interval]
\label{nrr:sc:prop:fixedinterval}
For an increasing polynomial-iterative homeomorphism, $\Fix(f)$ is
empty or a closed interval, with points, rays, and the whole line allowed.
In particular, a nontrivial bounded component of $\R\setminus\Fix(f)$
cannot occur.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The fixed set is closed. If $a<b$ are fixed, every orbit of a point
$x\in[a,b]$ stays in $[a,b]$ for all integer times and is monotone.
It has finite limits at both ends. Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:limits} makes the
orbit constant, so $x$ is fixed.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Fixed-point-free realization}

\begin{theorem}
\label{nrr:sc:thm:fixedfree}
Let $\mu\ne z-1$ satisfy Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:main}, and let $r_-,r_+$ be
the extreme root moduli of $\scD\mu$. A realization with no fixed point
exists if and only if
\begin{equation}
 r_-\le1\le r_+.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:fixedfree}
\end{equation}
When it exists it can be globally real analytic and bi-Lipschitz.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Sufficiency is the full-line case of \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:range}.
For necessity, if all roots of $\scD\mu$ have modulus greater than one,
every increment sequence is absolutely summable toward $-\infty$.
Thus every orbit has a finite backward limit, necessarily fixed by
continuity. If all roots have modulus less than one, the forward limit
is finite instead. Both contradict the absence of fixed points.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Fixed-point realization}

For a polynomial $\mu$, let $\mu_{<}$ and $\mu_{>}$ be its monic factors
formed from roots with modulus respectively less and greater than one.
An empty factor is $1$.

\begin{theorem}[Fixed-point spectra]
\label{nrr:sc:thm:fixedpoint}
A nonconstant monic real $\mu$, with $\mu(0)\ne0$, is the exact minimal
polynomial of an increasing homeomorphism having a fixed point if and
only if either $\mu=z-1$, or all the following hold:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\roman*)},leftmargin=2.4em]
\item Its only possible root on the unit circle is $1$, with multiplicity
at most one.
\item At least one of $\mu_{<},\mu_{>}$ is nonconstant.
\item Each nonconstant member of $\{\mu_{<},\mu_{>}\}$ is
positive-admissible.
\end{enumerate}
These realizations can be chosen globally bi-Lipschitz and analytic away
from one fixed point.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose a nonidentity realization has a fixed point. By
Proposition~\ref{nrr:sc:prop:fixedinterval}, each nonfixed component is a ray
with a finite fixed endpoint $c$. Every orbit on it tends to $c$ in one
time direction: otherwise its finite limit would be another fixed point
inside the ray. Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:limits} shows that such an orbit has
only roots strictly on one side of the unit circle, together with a possible
simple constant mode. Consequently (i) holds globally.
For increments, the constant mode disappears. The factors $\mu_{<}$
and $\mu_{>}$ are separately least common multiples of positive
increment-orbit spectra, and hence are admissible by
Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:lcm-positive}. Nonidentity gives (ii).

Conversely construct, for each nonconstant factor, a half-line map
with that exact minimal polynomial and finite endpoint zero, using
Section~\ref{nrr:sc:sec:classification}. Reflection or reversal of the
coordinate moves its domain to either desired half-line. If both factors
occur, put one on each side of zero. If only one occurs, use it on both
sides. The combined map $\Phi$ fixes zero, is globally bi-Lipschitz, is
analytic away from zero, and has minimal polynomial $\mu_{<}\mu_{>}$.
There is no root at one in this product.

If $\mu$ includes a simple $z-1$, translate the map:
$f(x)=1+\Phi(x-1)$. Any annihilator of $f$, evaluated at its fixed point
$1$, must vanish at one. Subtracting this constant contribution shows
that it must also annihilate $\Phi$. Conversely the product of those two
coprime factors annihilates $f$. Its minimal polynomial is therefore
$(z-1)\mu_{<}\mu_{>}$, as required.
\end{proof}

Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:fixedpoint} is genuinely stronger than the general
classification. For example $(z-\tfrac12)(z-2)^2$ is admissible globally,
but its exterior factor $(z-2)^2$ is not. Every realization is therefore
fixed-point-free. In contrast, $(z-\tfrac12)(z-2)$ has both fixed-point
and fixed-point-free realizations. A spectrum does not in general
determine the dynamical type of every solution.

\section{Explicit examples and arbitrarily high nonreal multiplicity}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:examples}

\subsection{Even multiplicities on two extreme circles}

Set, for every $n\in\Z$,
\begin{align}
 v_n&=2^n(2-n)+4^n(2+n),\label{nrr:sc:eq:vexample}\\
 u_n&=2^n(4-n)+4^n\left(\frac n3+\frac29\right).
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:uexample}
\end{align}
Direct subtraction gives $u_{n+1}-u_n=v_n$.
The value $v_n$ is positive: for $|n|\le2$ both summands in
\eqref{nrr:sc:eq:vexample} are nonnegative and not both zero; for $n>2$ the
positive $4^n(n+2)$ exceeds $2^n(n-2)$; and for $n<-2$ the corresponding
inequality is reversed between the two bases. In fact the same argument
works for all real $n$.

The sequence $u_n$ increases from $0$ to $+\infty$.
Let $H$ be its strictly increasing polygonal interpolation,
$H(n+s)=(1-s)u_n+su_{n+1}$ for $0\le s\le1$.
Define $F(H(t))=H(t+1)$ on the positive half-line and extend oddly through
zero. Each orbit at phase $s$ is a linear combination of two shifted
copies of $u_n$, so
\begin{equation}
 \bigl((z-2)^2(z-4)^2\bigr)[F]=0.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:evenexample}
\end{equation}
The exact determinant
\begin{equation}
 \det(u_{i+j})_{0\le i,j\le3}=\frac{16384}{9}\ne0
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:evenhankel}
\end{equation}
certifies minimal degree four, hence the displayed minimal polynomial.
The slopes on consecutive intervals are $v_{n+1}/v_n$. They have
positive finite limits $2$ and $4$ at the two ends; positivity on the
remaining finite set gives global bi-Lipschitz bounds.
This rational polygonal example is supplemental to the analytic
realization theorem, not a substitute for it.

\subsection{An everywhere analytic annular example with rational orbit data}

Let
\begin{equation}
 h(t)=2^t-2^{-t}+\frac1{10}\sin\frac{\pi t}{2},
 \qquad f(h(t))=h(t+1).
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:analyticexample}
\end{equation}
Since
\[
 h'(t)=(\log2)(2^t+2^{-t})+\frac\pi{20}\cos\frac{\pi t}{2}
 \ge2\log2-\frac\pi{20}>0,
\]
the coordinate is an analytic bijection $\R\to\R$ with nonvanishing
derivative. The last strict inequality follows, for example, from
$\log2>1/2$ and $\pi<4$.
The resulting $f$ is analytic and fixed-point-free, and its minimal
polynomial is
\begin{equation}
 \mu_f(z)=(z-\tfrac12)(z-2)(z^2+1)
 =z^4-\tfrac52z^3+2z^2-\tfrac52z+1.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:analyticpoly}
\end{equation}
All terms of $h(n)$ are rational. The first four are
$0,8/5,15/4,311/40$, and
\begin{equation}
 \det(h(i+j))_{0\le i,j\le3}=\frac{225}{256}\ne0.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:analytichankel}
\end{equation}
Thus even the unit-circle nonreal pair survives without any root at one
in the characteristic polynomial. The two real roots lie on opposite
sides of the unit circle and supply the two ends of the coordinate.
(Compare Part~I's analytic quartic, Theorem~\ref{thm:quartic}, where the
pair $\pm i$ needs the double root at one that
Theorem~\ref{thm:classification} requires on a single circle.)

\subsection{No multiplicity penalty inside the annulus}

\begin{theorem}[Uniformly bi-Lipschitz high-order nonreal modes]
\label{nrr:sc:thm:uniform}
For every integer $m\ge1$, there is an everywhere real-analytic,
fixed-point-free increasing homeomorphism $f_m$ with
\begin{equation}
 \mu_{f_m}(z)=(z-\tfrac12)(z-2)(z^2+1)^m,
 \qquad
 \frac16\le f_m'(x)\le6\quad(x\in\R).
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:uniformexample}
\end{equation}
In particular, a common global bi-Lipschitz bound places no upper bound
on the multiplicity of an interior nonreal characteristic root.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Write $a=\log2$, $k=m-1$, and $\varepsilon=a^k/(2k!)$.
Set
\begin{equation}
 W(t)=2^t+2^{-t},\qquad
 g_m(t)=W(t)+\varepsilon t^k\cos\frac{\pi t}{2}.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:uniformg}
\end{equation}
The exponential-series inequality
$e^{a|t|}\ge(a|t|)^k/k!$ implies
\[
 \varepsilon|t|^k\le\tfrac12 e^{a|t|}\le\tfrac12W(t).
\]
Hence $W/2\le g_m\le3W/2$.
Take the canonical real antiderivative from \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:primitive},
with no added constant. It is an increasing analytic bijection
$h_m:\R\to\R$. Its exact shift polynomial is the one in
\eqref{nrr:sc:eq:uniformexample}: integration preserves degree $k$ in the
nonzero frequencies $\pm\pi i/2$ and introduces no zero frequency.

Define $f_m=h_m\circ(t\mapsto t+1)\circ h_m^{-1}$.
Because $\tfrac12\le W(t+1)/W(t)\le2$,
\[
 \frac16\le\frac{g_m(t+1)}{g_m(t)}\le6.
\]
Equation \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:Fprime} proves the claimed derivative bounds.
\end{proof}

The proof does not infer minimality from the orbit at $h_m(0)$.
For certain parities an integrated oscillatory component can vanish at
that sampling phase. The shift-space argument proves exact minimality
for the map, and a generic phase supplies a full scalar-orbit certificate.

\subsection{A complete order-32 to order-23 reduction}

Consider the factored polynomial
\begin{equation}
 P=(z-1)^3(z-\tfrac12)^2(z-2)^4(z+2)^7
       (z^2+1)^5(z^2+9)^3.
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:largeP}
\end{equation}
After removing one copy at one, the least and greatest positive roots
are $1/2$ and $2$, of multiplicities $2$ and $4$.
The roots $\pm i$ are interior and keep multiplicity $5$.
The boundary root $-2$ is capped at $4$, and $\pm3i$ disappear.
Restoring the root at one gives
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\core(P)=(z-1)^3(z-\tfrac12)^2(z-2)^4(z+2)^4(z^2+1)^5.}
 \label{nrr:sc:eq:largecore}
\end{equation}
The degree falls from $32$ to $23$. Every increasing solution satisfies
the reduced equation, and a single globally analytic bi-Lipschitz
solution has exactly that degree-$23$ minimal polynomial.
Therefore no further universal factor or multiplicity can be removed.

\section{Reproducibility and verification boundaries}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:verification}

\subsection{Exact code and the scope of its input}

Part~II ships a standard-library Python implementation,
\nolinkurl{code/02-spectral-core-spectral_core.py} (delivered as
\texttt{code/spectral\_core.py}). It accepts a factor-level spectrum of
Gaussian-rational roots, with rational real and imaginary parts and
integer multiplicities. Conjugation symmetry is validated. Squared
moduli are rational, so every comparison is exact.

The implementation does \emph{not} claim to factor an arbitrary input
polynomial over the algebraic numbers. The theorem applies to all real
coefficient polynomials; the shipped implementation has the narrower,
explicit input representation above. For rational or real-algebraic
coefficients, exact root isolation and comparisons of algebraic squared
moduli would extend the front end without changing the core algorithm.

The manuscript's example below imports the module under its delivered name
\texttt{spectral\_core}. Under the shipped names that import fails; run it in
a copy of \texttt{code/} with the delivered names restored, as the report's
\texttt{README.md} describes, with that directory on Python's module search
path (for example, \texttt{PYTHONPATH=code python}).

\begin{lstlisting}
from fractions import Fraction
from spectral_core import Root, increasing_core

one = Root(1)
two = Root(2)
P = {one: 1, two: 2}
assert increasing_core(P) == {one: 1, two: 1}
\end{lstlisting}

The module also implements tests for exact realizability, fixed-point
realizability, fixed-point-free realizability, and affine rigidity.
It rejects zero roots, invalid multiplicities, and asymmetric conjugate
spectra rather than silently applying the theorem outside its hypotheses.

\subsection{Recorded exact checks}

The verifier (\nolinkurl{code/02-spectral-core-verify.py}, delivered as
\texttt{code/verify.py}; the file of that name here is Part~I's verifier)
produced the following results, recorded in
\nolinkurl{data/02-spectral-core-exact_checks.json} and
\nolinkurl{data/02-spectral-core-verification.txt}.
\begin{center}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}Xr@{}}
\toprule
Check & Count or exact result\\
\midrule
Spectra in two finite multiplicity grids & $1354$\\
Divisors examined against the admissibility criterion & $97281$\\
Rational recurrence windows for each example & $-80\le n\le80$\\
Exact increment/monotonicity assertions & $402$\\
Hankel determinant for \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:uexample} & $16384/9$\\
Hankel determinant for \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:analyticexample} & $225/256$\\
Large-core degree reduction & $32\to23$\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

For each finite input spectrum, the verifier computes the core formula
and independently enumerates all divisors satisfying the theorem's
admissibility test. It verifies equality with their least common multiple,
core idempotence, divisibility, and exact realizability of every nontrivial
core. This checks the finite combinatorics and implementation of the
formula, not the analytic realization proof. (Merge note: the enumeration is
independent of the core formula but uses the same
\texttt{positive\_admissible} routine as the rest of the module; the
comparison with Part~I's independently stated circular theorem is the one in
Remark~\ref{nrr:sc:rem:circular}.)

For each explicit orbit, rational arithmetic verifies the recurrence
through the stated window and computes a nonzero Hankel determinant.
The recurrence holds for all integers because of the symbolic
exponential-polynomial formulas, not because of the finite window.
The determinant certifies that no lower-order recurrence can annihilate
that orbit; the all-real constructions connect those orbits to actual maps.

The worked example of Section~\ref{nrr:sc:sec:examples} is also a script,
\nolinkurl{code/02-spectral-core-example.py} (delivered as
\texttt{code/example.py}); its recorded output is
\nolinkurl{data/02-spectral-core-example.txt}. In the write phase
(29 September 2026, Python 3.14.4) both scripts were rerun in a copy with the
delivered names restored: both passed, and their outputs equal the shipped
records apart from Windows line endings.

\subsection{Rebuilding}

The report's \texttt{README.md} gives the rerun commands for both parts
and the build command for this article. No bibliography processor is
needed; Part~II adds no figure files. The JSON output gives full exact
matrices, polynomial coefficients in ascending order, and initial values.
The short verification log is also included. The complete universal theorem
is not machine-checked by these scripts, and no Lean certification is
implied.

\section{Further research questions and a formalization program}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:research}

\begin{enumerate}[label=\textbf{Q\arabic*.},leftmargin=3.2em,itemsep=0.8em]
\item \textbf{Decreasing spectra and failure of single-witness sharpness.}
Determine the exact minimal spectra and universal relation ideal for
decreasing homeomorphisms. Simply replacing ``positive root'' by
``negative root'' is insufficient: odd-time order reversal and even-time
monotonicity must hold simultaneously. Draga's theorem \cite{D2016}
shows that certain two-negative-root equations reduce to a disjunction
of lower-order equations. In such a class, the least common annihilator
can require factors that are not simultaneously realized by any one map.
The increasing case's single-witness theorem may therefore have no
direct decreasing counterpart.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch~42: answered in Part~III for continuous maps
of $\R$ with $P(0)\ne0$. The minimal spectra are those of
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:main}, the universal relation ideal is generated by
$\dcore(P)$ (Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:core}), and single-witness sharpness does
fail as predicted: exactly two witnesses are needed precisely when both a
contracting and an expanding side core survive
(Corollary~\ref{nrr:dc:cor:two-branches}), which includes Draga's
disjunction. Proper intervals and $P(0)=0$ remain open.]

\item \textbf{Smoothness at a fixed point with several radii.}
Classify spectra realizable by globally $C^k$, $C^{k,\alpha}$, or
real-analytic maps having a fixed point. The present construction is
analytic off the point, and Part~I (Section~\ref{sec:regularity}) establishes
some single-circle regularity obstructions. Several radii introduce
competing power scales and possible resonances. An exact threshold theorem
would refine Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:fixedpoint}, rather than merely improve
a construction.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch~42: for \emph{decreasing} maps, which always
have a fixed point, Part~III gives a finite threshold
(Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:finite-threshold}) above which every non-involutive
solution is analytic with the spectra of
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:smooth-occurrence}; the threshold is sufficient, not
shown optimal. The increasing question stays open.]

\item \textbf{The least possible bi-Lipschitz distortion.}
For an admissible $\mu$, minimize a symmetric constant $L$ in
$L^{-1}|x-y|\le|f(x)-f(y)|\le L|x-y|$ over realizations with minimal
polynomial $\mu$. Extreme recurrence growth gives natural lower bounds,
while Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:uniform} shows that large interior
multiplicities need not force large distortion. Determine whether the
infimum depends only on extreme radii, and when it is attained.

\item \textbf{All solutions, not only their possible spectra.}
Describe the moduli of increasing solutions of a fixed equation.
The realization theorem selects convenient coordinates but is not a
parametrization of all solutions. One possible route is a positive cone
of recurrence initial data together with an order-compatible choice of
a fundamental interval and a gluing law.

\item \textbf{Convexity, concavity, and stronger order restrictions.}
Find the analogue of $\core(P)$ for globally convex or concave increasing
solutions. Convexity controls differences between nearby orbits, not just
signs of individual increments. It may yield stronger multiplicity or
oscillation restrictions than the positive-recurrence criterion.

\item \textbf{Proper intervals and one-sided iteration.}
Classify self-maps of bounded intervals, half-lines, and proper open
intervals without surjectivity. The proof here uses bilateral orbits in
an essential way. A one-sided recurrence need not have the negative-time
parity obstruction, and a finite endpoint changes which modes can occur.

\item \textbf{A certified algebraic-coefficient front end.}
Implement exact factorization and algebraic modulus comparison for
$P\in\mathbb{Q}[z]$, returning $\core(P)$ together with root-isolation
certificates. Equal-modulus decisions, rather than approximate root
clustering, are essential at the sharp boundary. The factor-level code
shipped with Part~II is a testable starting point, not that completed front
end.

\item \textbf{Stability of the core under coefficient perturbation.}
Determine the strata on which the degree and multiplicity pattern of
$\core(P)$ are constant, and describe their jumps. Equal-modulus and
positive-real-root collisions are natural boundary loci. A useful output
would distinguish stable eliminations from eliminations destroyed by an
arbitrarily small perturbation.

\item \textbf{Constructive positive envelopes and rational certificates.}
The sufficiency proof uses an existentially large $\Lambda$ and a small
perturbation parameter. Produce explicit certified bounds in terms of
root separations, degrees, and coefficient heights. This would turn an
existence construction into a predictable numerical and symbolic
realization algorithm.

\item \textbf{Lean formalization in separable stages.}
A practical sequence is: the annihilator ideal and difference-spectrum
identity; bilateral recurrence decomposition; finite trigonometric
returns and the positive-mean lemma; the necessary spectral test; the
finite root-level core formula; then the analytic positive-envelope and
conjugacy construction. The algebraic reduction and the finite
classification can be audited independently before the analytic
realization library is complete. (As of 29 September 2026 none of these
stages has been started in ProveIt.)
\end{enumerate}

These are proposed projects, not claims of unresolved status throughout
all literature. The decreasing and regularity questions require a renewed
bibliographic check before being presented as newly formulated problems.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch~42: Part~III's literature check for the
decreasing question is recorded in Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:audit}; it too is
not exhaustive.]

\section{Conclusion of Part~II}
\label{nrr:sc:sec:conclusion}

Removing one $1$-factor converts the increasing iterative-equation problem
into a problem about positive bilateral recurrences. At two different
ends, different exponential envelopes can dominate. That permits even
extreme multiplicities and arbitrary interior multiplicities, exactly
where the circular theorem no longer supplies the answer.

The resulting classification is both necessary and sufficient, and its
sufficiency is global: every permitted spectrum is realized by a
bi-Lipschitz increasing homeomorphism. The universal core then identifies
the exact amount of order reduction available from an arbitrary input
polynomial, with one realization witnessing all retained multiplicities.
The fixed-point theorems separate two dynamical regimes, and the analytic
family shows that high nonreal multiplicity is compatible with uniformly
controlled metric distortion.

The result closes the several-circle gap in Part~I within the increasing
whole-line setting. It does not assert a classification of decreasing maps,
noninjective zero-constant equations, or every individual solution. Its
mathematical claims are the stated and proved theorems; historical priority
and independent formal verification remain separate questions.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch~42: decreasing maps are classified in
Part~III.]

\section{A finite Hankel certificate for minimality}
\label{nrr:sc:app:hankel}

Suppose a scalar sequence $u_n$ is annihilated by a monic degree-$d$
polynomial. If a polynomial of degree $e<d$ also annihilated it, then
there would be a nontrivial linear relation among the first $e+1$ columns
of the Hankel matrix $(u_{i+j})_{0\le i,j\le d-1}$: evaluate that
recurrence at $i=0,\ldots,d-1$. Hence a nonzero determinant proves
minimal order $d$. This does not require numerical conditioning estimates.
(Part~I uses the same argument for \eqref{eq:hankelF}
and \eqref{eq:quarticdet}.)

For the analytic example, the exact matrix is
\[
 \begin{pmatrix}
 0&8/5&15/4&311/40\\
 8/5&15/4&311/40&255/16\\
 15/4&311/40&255/16&5131/160\\
 311/40&255/16&5131/160&4095/64
 \end{pmatrix},
\]
with determinant $225/256$.
Its recurrence polynomial is \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:analyticpoly}. The stored JSON
contains the analogous exact matrix for the even-multiplicity example.

\section{Source and novelty audit of Part~II}
\label{nrr:sc:app:audit}

\paragraph{Repository source.}
The inspected source is Part~I, at
\nolinkurl{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/log-concavity-and-unimodality/}
\nolinkurl{nonreal-roots-in-iterative-equations/article.tex} in ProveIt
(\url{https://github.com/VladimirReshetnikov/ProveIt}), inspected on
28 September 2026. The GitHub content API returned blob SHA
\begin{center}\small\texttt{ae6ea0aa0566316ba9ed06b1750c6e73275059da}.\end{center}
The retrieved conclusion explicitly leaves several-circle spectra
unclassified. The manuscript does not reuse the circular counterexamples
as its new result, and it does not claim that the repository's unrefereed
report is a formal certification.

\paragraph{External sources checked.}
The primary preprints \cite{DM2016,D2016} were read for the formulation and
scope of the order-reduction problem. Page~13 of \cite{DM2016}, including
Problem~6.1, was also visually inspected. Petrov's original question and
its displayed comments \cite{Petrov2016}, including I.~Bogdanov's, were
checked directly. (The manuscript cites arXiv:1503.00570 without a version
and arXiv:1604.01287v2; Part~I's bibliography entries, used here, give the
same works.) Searches for broader iterative-spectrum classifications
returned a mixture of relevant and irrelevant results; no exhaustive
citation-database review was completed. Thus Part~II establishes a concrete
extension of Part~I, not a certified first-in-literature claim.

\paragraph{Trust boundary.}
All universal statements in Part~II rest on the written proofs.
The rational scripts check explicit examples and finite consistency of
the root-level rule. No specialized theorem from the repository or cited
iterative-equation papers is used as an unproved premise; the argument uses
standard real analysis and finite-dimensional linear algebra. No independent
referee or proof assistant has checked the complete development. The
manuscript's author read the repository only; no files were committed or
modified there. The manuscript's own status note and source record are
shipped verbatim as \nolinkurl{02-spectral-core-STATUS.md} and
\nolinkurl{02-spectral-core-source_audit.md}.

\phantomsection\part*{Part III.\quad Orientation-reversing spectra: the decreasing spectral core}
\addcontentsline{toc}{part}{Part III.\texorpdfstring{\quad}{ } Orientation-reversing spectra: the decreasing spectral core}

\noindent\emph{Part~III was added on 29 September 2026 in batch~42 of
ProveIt's incoming-report intake, from one manuscript. It answers the boundary
that Parts~I and~II both leave open, ``We have not classified decreasing
homeomorphisms, for which consecutive iterates reverse order''
(Section~\ref{sec:conclusion}), and Part~II's first research question
(Section~\ref{nrr:sc:sec:research}, Q1), for continuous maps of $\R$ and
equations with nonzero constant coefficient:
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:main} classifies the minimal iterative polynomials of
decreasing homeomorphisms, Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:core} gives the exact
universal reduction $\dcore(P)$, and Corollary~\ref{nrr:dc:cor:two-branches}
shows that it needs one or two witnesses, exactly as Q1 anticipated. It adds a
finite-smoothness rigidity theorem (Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:finite-threshold})
with no counterpart in Parts~I--II. Zero constant coefficient and proper
interval domains remain open. Parts~I and~II are unchanged except for the title
page and dated pointers to this addition.
Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:intro} records the provenance and fixes the notation
of the addition.}

\section{Provenance, conventions and the main results}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:intro}

\subsection{One manuscript, one addition}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:source}

Part~III is batch-42 manuscript~02, an AI-assisted research draft prepared
for Vladimir Reshetnikov, dated 29 September 2026 and titled \emph{The
Decreasing Spectral Core: Complete minimal spectra, finite-smoothness
rigidity, and global polynomial conjugacy} (27 US Letter pages as
delivered). It arrived as
the archive \texttt{ProveIt\_Orientation\_Reversing\_Spectra.zip} (inner
directory \texttt{ProveIt\_Orientation\_Reversing\_Spectra/}) in ProveIt
commit \texttt{8315d24e3} and was placed in \texttt{3609d0473}. It is pinned
to ProveIt commit \texttt{afb2d1227} (full hash in
Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:audit}). At that commit this report contained Part~I
only, as the Git blob \texttt{ae6ea0aa} of \texttt{article.tex} (the blob that
pins Part~II; Section~\ref{nrr:sc:sec:source}), so
the manuscript's statements about ``the ProveIt report'' refer to the text of
Part~I printed above. It read Part~II not in the repository but as the
batch-41 manuscript itself, a copy in Vladimir's file library
(\nolinkurl{article(20260929-042518).tex}), and does not assert that this copy
belongs to the pinned commit; Part~II prints that manuscript. Its verifier,
Makefile, recorded output and status note are shipped beside Parts~I--II with
the prefix \nolinkurl{03-decreasing-core-}; its manuscript, PDF, delivery
README and checksum ledger (\texttt{CHECKSUMS.sha256}, 8/8 verified) are not
shipped and survive in the arrival commit.

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{The manuscript's abstract.} For a real polynomial
$P(z)=\sum a_jz^j$, write $P[f]=\sum a_j f^{\circ j}$, with composition in
place of pointwise powers. We classify all minimal iterative polynomials of
decreasing homeomorphisms of $\R$. Apart from involutions, the nonunit roots
must all lie strictly inside or all strictly outside the unit circle. On each
extreme modulus circle, its negative real root must have maximal
multiplicity; on a single circle that multiplicity must also be odd. These
conditions, with optional simple factors at $1$ and $-1$, are sufficient,
with globally bi-Lipschitz realizations analytic away from at most two
points.

The classification gives an explicit root-by-root formula for the smallest
polynomial annihilating every decreasing solution of $P[f]=0$. Two solutions
always suffice to witness its sharpness, and two are necessary precisely when
both contracting and expanding spectral branches survive.

A second theorem is substantially more rigid. Every non-involutive
$C^\infty$ decreasing solution is globally of the form $f(c+H(t))=c+H(at)$,
where $a<0$, $a\ne-1$, and $H$ is a polynomial with $H(0)=0$, $H'(0)=1$, and
$H'>0$ on $\R$. In fact, an explicit finite $C^N$ hypothesis suffices.
Consequently these solutions are automatically real analytic and algebraic,
and their minimal roots are distinct real integer powers of one negative
multiplier. We derive the exact smooth spectral core, exhibit unbounded
smooth witness complexity, supply self-contained proofs and exact finite
checks, and identify further regularity and formalization problems.
\end{quote}

The manuscript contributes, in the order printed here: its statement of the
target and the two main theorems (this section); a regularity complement to
Part~I's homeomorphism lemma and a two-periodic limit lemma
(Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:recurrences}); the two-cycle interval and the global
separation of contracting and expanding spectra
(Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:geometry}); its use of Part~II's positive-recurrence
theorem (Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:positive}); the classification of decreasing
minimal spectra (Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:classification}); the universal
decreasing core, its two sharp branches, the witness count and an affine
rigidity test (Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:core}); $C^1$ hyperbolicity, an
explicit smoothness threshold and the polynomial-coordinate theorem
(Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:smooth}); smooth spectra, the smooth core and
unbounded smooth witness counts (Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:smooth-core});
examples (Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:examples}); its finite checks and a
formalization route (Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:verification}); ten research
questions (Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:research}); its conclusion
(Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:conclusion}); and its source, dependency and claim
audit (Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:audit}). Every result, proof, example, remark,
limitation and question of the manuscript is printed.

\paragraph{Where the merge had to choose.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\alph*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item The manuscript reproves, so as not to assume Parts~I--II, the
homeomorphism lemma, the annihilator ideal and cyclic composition space, the
two-sided recurrence decomposition, orbitwise least common multiples, the
simultaneous returns and positive means of finite trigonometric sequences,
and, from Part~II, positive admissibility with its necessity proposition and
least-common-multiple lemma, the two-ended positive envelope and the positive
analytic interpolation theorem. Its proofs are the same arguments as those
printed in Parts~I and~II. They are printed once, there, and credited where
Part~III uses them (Sections~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:imported}
and~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:positive}). What the manuscript adds to them is printed:
the regularity of the inverse (Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:homeomorphism}), the
trigonometric limit statement (Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:trig}(iii)) and the
two-periodic limit lemma (Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:limits}), which extends
Part~II's Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:limits}.
\item Remarks~\ref{nrr:dc:rem:reflected-core} and~\ref{nrr:dc:rem:petrov}
were added in the merge. The first expresses the decreasing core through
Part~II's positive-recurrence core $\pcore$ and records a finite check of that
identity made in the write phase; the second combines Part~II's and
Part~III's rigidity theorems into an answer to Petrov's linearity question for
equations with nonzero constant coefficient.
\item Symbols were renamed to avoid collisions with Parts~I--II and within the
manuscript (Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:notation}); no normalization changed.
The manuscript's $\mathcal C_{\downarrow}$ and $\mathcal C_\infty$ are kept.
Its references to ``the ProveIt report'' and to ``the increasing companion''
now read ``Part~I'' and ``Part~II'', and its file names are replaced by the
shipped names.
\item The manuscript's appendix (its source audit) is printed as the last
section of Part~III, so that Part~I's appendices keep their letters. Its
unnumbered verification table stays unnumbered.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Status (manuscript 02 of batch 42).}
This is an AI-assisted, unrefereed research draft, not a Lean- or
Rocq-verified development, and no declaration in ProveIt formalizes any
statement of Parts~I--III. The main classifications and the finite-smoothness
theorem are proved below, rather than assumed from the repository; no theorem
of an unverified repository manuscript is used as an unproved premise. Their
extension beyond the inspected repository reports is explicit; a complete
historical-priority claim is not made. Classical recurrence methods and the
previously known two-branch phenomenon (Draga's Theorem~3 and Remark~4
\cite{D2016}) are credited. The domain is the whole real line and $P(0)\ne0$:
Part~III does not classify maps of arbitrary intervals, equations with zero
constant coefficient, nonhomogeneous equations, higher-dimensional systems, or
every individual continuous solution (it does classify every individual
non-involutive solution in the stated sufficiently smooth class). The
finite-smoothness threshold is sufficient; its optimality is not claimed. The
finite computations check the algebraic rules on finite atlases; they are not
proofs of the all-real theorems.

\subsection{Notation across the three parts}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:notation}

Part~I's and Part~II's symbols keep their meanings in their parts.

not listed ($P$, $Q$, $\mu_f$, $E$, $\Ann$, $\Fix$, $W$, $G$, $\Lambda$ as in
Part~II's envelope lemma, and $\pcore$) agree with Parts~I--II.
For a monic polynomial $A$ and a root $\lambda$, $m_A(\lambda)$ is the
multiplicity of $\lambda$ in $A$ (Part~II's notation; the manuscript writes
$\operatorname{mult}_A(\lambda)$), and $\roots(A)$ is the set of distinct
complex roots.

\subsection{The research target and the contribution}

Part~I studies the obstruction to eliminating nonreal characteristic roots
and classifies increasing minimal spectra supported on a single modulus
circle. Its scope does not include a complete decreasing classification.
Part~II (the manuscript's ``increasing companion'') treats arbitrary-radius
increasing spectra and explicitly excludes decreasing solutions. Part~III
addresses that remaining orientation, then goes beyond spectral occurrence to
a global classification under smoothness assumptions.

The distinction is not a change of a sign in the increasing theorem.
A decreasing homeomorphism has a unique fixed point, but its square may
have an interval of fixed points. Every exterior orbit approaches a
finite two-cycle in one time direction. This forces a global separation
between contraction and expansion and accounts for two different universal
reductions. After sufficient differentiability is imposed, the two-cycle
interval collapses and all non-polynomial spectral behavior disappears.

The external background is the characteristic-root and order-reduction
program, including Yang--Zhang \cite{YZ2004}, Draga--Morawiec \cite{DM2016},
and Draga \cite{D2016}. The use of bilateral orbit recurrences is classical.
In particular, Draga's Theorem~3 eliminates one of two negative extreme
roots straddling $-1$, and his Remark~4 already explains why the result
can be a disjunction of two lower-order equations. We do not claim that
phenomenon or its quadratic example as new. Our target is the exact
arbitrary-multiplicity classification, its sharp reduction formula,
and the separate finite-smoothness rigidity theorem.

\subsection{Conventions and main statements}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:main}

Throughout Part~III, $P\in\R[z]$ is nonzero and $P(0)\ne0$. Constant nonzero
polynomials are permitted as input equations, although they have no
solutions. Iterates are denoted by $f^{\circ j}$, with $f^{\circ0}=\id$;
negative iterates use the inverse. A map is \emph{decreasing} here if it
is strictly decreasing. Its \emph{minimal iterative polynomial} $\mu_f$
is the monic generator of
\[
 \Ann(f)=\{Q\in\R[z]:Q[f]=0\}.
\]
The generator exists whenever $f$ satisfies a nonzero polynomial iterative
equation. All multiplicities are complex-root multiplicities, with
conjugate roots counted separately in polynomial degrees.

For a polynomial $R$ with no root on the unit circle, say that $R$ is
\emph{negative-admissible} when, on its smallest and largest modulus
circles, the negative real root has multiplicity at least as large as
that of every other root on the same circle. If there is just one
modulus circle, require this negative-root multiplicity to be odd.
There is no restriction on multiplicities on strictly intermediate circles.

\begin{theorem}[Continuous spectral classification, preview]
\label{nrr:dc:thm:main}
A monic nonconstant real polynomial $\mu$ with $\mu(0)\ne0$ is the minimal
polynomial of a decreasing homeomorphism of $\R$ if and only if either
\[
 \mu=z+1\quad\hbox{or}\quad\mu=(z-1)(z+1),
\]
or
\begin{equation}
 \mu=(z-1)^{\varepsilon_+}(z+1)^{\varepsilon_-}R,
 \qquad \varepsilon_+,\varepsilon_-\in\{0,1\},
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:main-form}
\end{equation}
where $R$ is nonconstant, negative-admissible, and all its roots have
modulus less than $1$, or all have modulus greater than $1$.
Every permitted $\mu$ has a globally bi-Lipschitz realization, real
analytic outside at most two points.
\end{theorem}

\begin{theorem}[Smooth rigidity, preview]
\label{nrr:dc:thm:smooth}
Let $f:\R\to\R$ be a $C^\infty$ decreasing solution of $P[f]=0$.
Either $f^{\circ2}=\id$, or there are unique $c\in\R$, $a<0$ with
$a\ne-1$, and $H\in\R[t]$ such that
\begin{equation}
 H(0)=0,\qquad H'(0)=1,\qquad H'(t)>0\ (t\in\R),\qquad
 f(c+H(t))=c+H(at).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:poly-conjugacy}
\end{equation}
Here $c$ is the fixed point and $a=f'(c)$. If
$H(t)=\sum_{k\in K}h_kt^k$ with every $h_k\ne0$, then
\begin{equation}
 \mu_f(z)=(z-1)^{\mathbf1_{c\ne0}}\prod_{k\in K}(z-a^k),
 \qquad 1\in K,\quad \max K\ \hbox{odd}.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:smooth-spectrum}
\end{equation}
Moreover, a computable finite differentiability threshold depending only
on the root moduli of $P$ can replace $C^\infty$.
\end{theorem}

The full proofs appear in Sections~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:classification} and
\ref{nrr:dc:sec:smooth}. The threshold is stated explicitly in
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:finite-threshold}. Sections~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:core}
and~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:smooth-core} turn these theorems into exact universal
reductions.

\subsection{What is inherited and what is developed here}

The positive-recurrence criterion used in Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:positive}
is the mechanism of Part~II; the manuscript reproves it because the decreasing
construction needs its continuous positive interpolation, not merely a
necessary condition. Neither its reuse nor the orientation-reversed cubic
example is the principal claim. The substantive continuation is the complete
decreasing classification, including the unit-root and two-cycle geometry,
the maximal reduction branches with arbitrary multiplicities, and the global
polynomial-coordinate theorem under an explicit finite regularity assumption.
No theorem from an unverified repository manuscript is used as an unproved
premise.

\section{Linear algebra behind a nonlinear equation}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:recurrences}

\subsection{What is taken from Parts~I and~II}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:imported}

The manuscript opens by reproving facts that Part~I proves by the same
arguments; they are used here in Part~I's form.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\arabic*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item Every continuous solution $f:\R\to\R$ of $P[f]=0$ is a strictly
monotone homeomorphism (Lemma~\ref{lem:homeo}).
\item $\Ann(f)$ is an ideal, generated by $\mu_f$, because
$(zQ)[f]=Q[f]\circ f$ is \emph{right} composition
(Lemma~\ref{lem:ideal}, \eqref{eq:rightcomp}). One must not replace it by left
composition and distribute $f$ over a sum; that invalid maneuver would destroy
the linear-algebra argument.
\item If $A(0)\ne0$, every complex bilateral sequence annihilated by $A(E)$,
where $Eu(n)=u(n+1)$, has a unique expansion
$u(n)=\sum_{\lambda\in\roots(A)}p_\lambda(n)\lambda^n$ with
$\deg p_\lambda<m_A(\lambda)$, and its minimal recurrence polynomial is
$\prod_{p_\lambda\ne0}(z-\lambda)^{1+\deg p_\lambda}$; real sequences have
conjugate coefficient polynomials at conjugate roots
(Lemma~\ref{lem:recurrence}, \eqref{eq:expoly}).
\item The scalar orbit $u_x(n)=f^{\circ n}(x)$ is such a sequence. A
polynomial annihilates all orbits if and only if it annihilates $f$, by
evaluation at $n=0$. Hence $\mu_f$ is the least common multiple of the orbit
minimal polynomials, and only finitely many orbits are needed
(Lemma~\ref{lem:lcm}).
\end{enumerate}
As in Part~II, let $E$ act on real-valued functions by $Ev=v\circ f$. The
vector space
\[
 V_f=\operatorname{span}_{\R}\{\id,f,f^{\circ2},\ldots\}
\]
is finite dimensional when $P[f]=0$. Right composition is linear on this
space, even though $f$ need not be a linear map of $\R$. It is invertible by
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:inverse} below, and $\id$ is a cyclic vector. The minimal
polynomial of $E|_{V_f}$ equals $\mu_f$, and $\mu_f(0)\ne0$.

The manuscript's version of the homeomorphism lemma adds regularity of the
inverse.

\begin{lemma}
\label{nrr:dc:lem:homeomorphism}
Every continuous solution $f:\R\to\R$ of $P[f]=0$ is a strictly monotone
homeomorphism. If $f$ is $C^k$, $1\le k\le\infty$, then its inverse is
$C^k$ and $f'(x)\ne0$ everywhere. The analogous assertion holds for real
analytic $f$.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The first sentence is Lemma~\ref{lem:homeo}; the manuscript gives the same
proof. Write $P(z)=\sum_{j=0}^d a_jz^j$, $a_0a_d\ne0$.
Substitution of $x=f^{-1}(y)$ gives
\begin{equation}
 f^{-1}(y)=-\frac1{a_0}\sum_{j=1}^d a_j f^{\circ(j-1)}(y).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:inverse}
\end{equation}
This proves the regularity assertion. Differentiating
$f^{-1}\circ f=\id$ proves that $f'$ is nowhere zero.
The constant-polynomial case has no solution and is vacuous.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Trigonometric returns and two-periodic limits}

\begin{lemma}[Elementary trigonometric facts]
\label{nrr:dc:lem:trig}
Let $T(n)=\sum_{\zeta\in S}c_\zeta\zeta^n$, with distinct $|\zeta|=1$.
There are integers $N_j\to+\infty$ such that $\zeta^{N_j}\to1$ for all
$\zeta\in S$. Consequently:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),itemsep=1pt]
\item if $T$ is real and $\liminf_{n\to\infty}T(n)\ge0$, then
$T(n)\ge0$ for every integer $n$;
\item a nonzero nonnegative real $T$ has a strictly positive coefficient
at frequency $1$;
\item if $T(n)$ has a limit as $n\to\infty$, then $T$ is constant.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
The returns are Lemma~\ref{lem:returns}, and (i) and (ii) are
Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:trig} (that is, Lemma~\ref{lem:returns} with the proof
of Theorem~\ref{thm:positive}, and Lemma~\ref{lem:mean}). For (iii), fix
$\ell$; then $T(N_j+\ell)\to T(\ell)$, and the left side tends to the limit,
so $T(\ell)$ equals the limit for every $\ell$. (Equivalently, $T$ minus its
limit is a unit-circle exponential polynomial tending to zero, hence zero by
Corollary~\ref{cor:vanishing}.) The manuscript proves all three by the same
compactness and Ces\`aro-mean arguments; the same return argument works in
negative time.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}[Boundedness, convergence, and spectral size]
\label{nrr:dc:lem:limits}
The following hold for a nonzero sequence of the form \eqref{eq:expoly}.
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),itemsep=2pt]
\item If it is bounded for all $n\in\Z$, all active roots are simple
and have modulus $1$.
\item If it has finite limits along the even and odd subsequences as
$n\to+\infty$, its active roots have modulus less than $1$, except
possibly simple roots $1$ and $-1$. Their contribution is exactly the
limiting two-periodic sequence. For $n\to-\infty$, replace ``less''
by ``greater''.
\item If every active root has modulus at least $\omega>0$,
then $u(n)=O(\rho^n)$ with $0<\rho<\omega$ is impossible as
$n\to+\infty$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Let $r$ be the largest active modulus and $d$ the largest polynomial
degree on that circle. Then
\[
 u(n)=r^n n^d\bigl(T(n)+o(1)\bigr),
\]
where $T$ is a nonzero finite trigonometric sequence. Such a $T$ is
bounded away from zero along a subsequence: its positive mean square
precludes convergence to zero. This rules out exponential growth and
then polynomial growth when boundedness is required. Applying the same
argument to $u(-n)$ proves (i), and proves the growth exclusions in (ii).
The remaining unit-circle component has convergent even and odd
subsequences. Group its frequencies after squaring;
Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:trig}(iii) shows that only $\lambda^2=1$ can remain.
Considering both parities rules out cancellation between $\lambda$ and
$-\lambda$ with $\lambda^2\ne1$. This proves (ii). The same leading-term
argument proves (iii).
\end{proof}

Part~II's Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:limits} is the case of (ii) in which the whole
sequence has one limit; then only a simple root $1$ can remain on the unit
circle. The two-periodic version is what decreasing maps need.

\section{Two-cycles force a global spectral separation}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:geometry}

\begin{theorem}[The two-cycle interval]
\label{nrr:dc:thm:interval}
Let $f$ be a decreasing solution of $P[f]=0$, and put $g=f^{\circ2}$.
The map $f$ has a unique fixed point $c$. Either $g=\id$, or
\begin{equation}
 \Fix(g)=[\alpha,\beta],\qquad
 -\infty<\alpha\le c\le\beta<\infty,
 \qquad f(\alpha)=\beta,
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:interval}
\end{equation}
with $f(\beta)=\alpha$. On $(\beta,\infty)$, either $g(x)>x$ for every
$x$, or $g(x)<x$ for every $x$. In exactly one common time direction,
every exterior orbit tends to the endpoint two-cycle.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The continuous function $f(x)-x$ is strictly decreasing from $+\infty$
to $-\infty$, so it has exactly one zero $c$.
Suppose $p<q$ are fixed by $g$ and $p<x<q$. All even iterates of $x$
remain in $[p,q]$, in both time directions, and all odd iterates remain
in $[f(q),f(p)]$. Thus the whole bilateral orbit is bounded.
By Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:limits}(i), it is a finite trigonometric sequence.
The even subsequence $g^{\circ n}(x)$ is monotone in $n$, because $g$
is increasing, and is bounded. It therefore converges, and
Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:trig}(iii), applied to the squared frequencies, shows
that it is constant. Hence $g(x)=x$.

This proves that $\Fix(g)$ is a nonempty closed interval. It is invariant
under $f$. If it were unbounded above, its image under the decreasing
homeomorphism $f$ would be unbounded below, and conversely. Thus a
proper such interval is bounded on both sides, proving
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:interval}. On its right complement the continuous function
$g(x)-x$ has no zero, hence a fixed strict sign. If that sign is positive,
$g^{\circ n}(x)\to\beta$ as $n\to-\infty$, and tends to $+\infty$
as $n\to+\infty$; any other finite limit would be a further fixed point.
For the negative sign, reverse time. Odd iterates tend to $\alpha$
at the finite end. The left ray is the image of the right ray under
$f$, so its time direction is the same.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Unit roots and one-sided nonunit spectrum]
\label{nrr:dc:cor:separation}
For a non-involutive decreasing solution, all nonunit roots of $\mu_f$
lie on one side of the unit circle: all have modulus less than $1$,
or all have modulus greater than $1$. The only possible unit roots
are simple $1$ and simple $-1$. Moreover,
\begin{equation}
 -1\in\roots(\mu_f)\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\alpha<\beta.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:minus-one}
\end{equation}
If $\alpha=\beta=c$, the root $1$ occurs if and only if $c\ne0$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every exterior orbit has a finite two-periodic limit in the common
time direction from Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:interval}. Apply
Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:limits}(ii). Every interior orbit is exactly
two-periodic. Taking the finite least common multiple of their minimal
polynomials preserves these restrictions and proves the global, rather than
merely orbitwise, separation.

When $\alpha<\beta$, the endpoint orbit itself has a nonzero
$(-1)^n$ component. When the interval is a singleton, every orbit
has constant limit $c$ and no $(-1)^n$ component. In that case the
constant component is exactly $c$, proving the last assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
For a genuine two-cycle interval, the presence of the root $1$ is not
determined just by the fixed point. A nonlinear involution on the
interval may have nonconstant pair averages even when $c=0$.
The equivalence between a simple root $1$ and a nonzero fixed point
is asserted only when the interval is a singleton.
\end{remark}

The periodic cases are now completely separated. If $f^{\circ2}=\id$,
then $\mu_f$ divides $z^2-1$. It cannot be $z-1$, since $f$ is
decreasing. Thus it is either $z+1$, which forces $f=-\id$, or
$z^2-1$. Conversely, reflections $x\mapsto-x$ and $x\mapsto2c-x$,
$c\ne0$, realize these two polynomials.

\begin{corollary}[Bounded-interval version]
\label{nrr:dc:cor:bounded}
A decreasing homeomorphism of a bounded interval that satisfies a
nonzero polynomial iterative equation is an involution.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every bilateral orbit is bounded. The bounded-orbit argument in the
proof of Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:interval} makes each even subsequence
constant. This does not require the interval to be closed.
\end{proof}

\section{Positive bilateral recurrences and their realization}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:positive}

The manuscript's Section~4 reproves Part~II's positive-recurrence theory with
Part~II's statements and arguments; it is used here in Part~II's form, with
one change of letter: the positive exponential polynomial that Part~II calls
$g$ is called $w$ in Part~III, because $g$ denotes $f^{\circ2}$ and a centred
map here.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\arabic*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item \emph{Positive admissibility} is Part~II's
Definition~\ref{nrr:sc:def:admissible}: the positive real root on each
extreme modulus circle has maximal multiplicity on that circle, and it is odd
when there is only one circle.
\item The minimal polynomial of a nonzero nonnegative bilateral recurrence
sequence is positive-admissible, and a least common multiple of finitely many
positive-admissible polynomials is positive-admissible
(Proposition~\ref{nrr:sc:prop:positive-necessary} and
Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:lcm-positive}; the manuscript states them as one
proposition, with the same proofs).
\item The two-ended positive envelope $G$ and weight $W$ of
Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:envelope} (the manuscript's large constant $T$ is
Part~II's $\Lambda$).
\item \emph{Exact positive interpolation}
(Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:positive-realization}): a monic nonconstant real $B$
with $B(0)\ne0$ is positive-admissible if and only if it is the minimal
polynomial of a strictly positive bilateral recurrence sequence, and every
such $B$ is the exact unit-shift minimal polynomial of a positive
real-analytic exponential polynomial $w:\R\to(0,\infty)$ with
$0<m\le w(t+1)/w(t)\le M<\infty$ (Part~II's \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:ratio}),
comparable above and below to the weight $W$ of \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:W}, and
built from the modes \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:mode}.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{remark}[A sampling pitfall]
Exact minimality is a statement about the continuous coordinate, or
about a nonsingular sampling phase. A sine term created by integration
can vanish at every integer without being the zero function. Neither
integer samples alone nor positive numerical samples establish the
continuous minimality or positivity required here. (This is the manuscript's
restatement of the caution printed after
Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:positive-realization}.)
\end{remark}
