\section{Complete classification of decreasing minimal spectra}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:classification}

For a monic polynomial $A$, define its sign reflection by
\begin{equation}
 A^\#(z)=(-1)^{\deg A}A(-z).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:reflection}
\end{equation}
Its roots are the negatives of the roots of $A$, with unchanged
multiplicities. Thus negative-admissibility of $R$ is precisely
positive-admissibility of $R^\#$.

\subsection{Necessity: positive distances from the limiting two-cycle}

\begin{proof}[Necessity in Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:main}]
The involution case was settled after
Corollary~\ref{nrr:dc:cor:separation}. Suppose $f$ is not an involution.
Write the unit part of its minimal polynomial as
$(z-1)^{\varepsilon_+}(z+1)^{\varepsilon_-}$; the exponents are at most one by
that corollary. Its nonunit part $R$ is nonconstant. Indeed, if
there were no nonunit roots, $\mu_f$ would divide $z^2-1$ and $f$
would be an involution. All roots of $R$ lie on the same side of the
unit circle.

Let $x>\beta$, where $[\alpha,\beta]=\Fix(f^{\circ2})$, and set
$b_n=\beta$ for even $n$, $b_n=\alpha$ for odd $n$. Then
\begin{equation}
 v_x(n)=(-1)^n\bigl(f^{\circ n}(x)-b_n\bigr)>0
 \qquad(n\in\Z).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:positive-distances}
\end{equation}
The limiting unit component of the orbit is exactly $b_n$, by
Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:limits}. Hence subtraction removes all its unit roots
and no nonunit root. Multiplication by $(-1)^n$ negates the remaining
roots. Every left exterior orbit is a shift of a right exterior orbit,
and every interior orbit is two-periodic. Consequently the least
common multiple of the minimal polynomials of the $v_x$ is $R^\#$,
and finitely many $x$ suffice to attain it.

Part~II's Proposition~\ref{nrr:sc:prop:positive-necessary} and
Lemma~\ref{nrr:sc:lem:lcm-positive} imply that $R^\#$ is positive-admissible.
Therefore $R$ is negative-admissible. This proves all necessary conditions.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Sufficiency: an odd analytic construction on two rays}

\begin{lemma}[Integrating a one-sided spectrum]
\label{nrr:dc:lem:integrating}
Let $B$ be positive-admissible, with all roots of modulus greater than
$1$, or all of modulus less than $1$. There is a positive real-analytic
bijection $h:\R\to(0,\infty)$, increasing in the former case and
decreasing in the latter, whose exact unit-shift polynomial is $B$.
It satisfies $|h'(t)|=w(t)$ for a positive $w$ with bounded ratios
$w(t+1)/w(t)$ as in \eqref{nrr:sc:eq:ratio}.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Use $w$ from Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:positive-realization} (Part~II's $g$), and
define
\begin{equation}
 h(t)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{t}w(s)\,ds,&\text{all root moduli exceed }1,\\[6pt]
 \displaystyle\int_t^{\infty}w(s)\,ds,&\text{all root moduli are below }1.
 \end{cases}
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:integrated-coordinate}
\end{equation}
The envelopes show integrability at the displayed finite end and
nonintegrability at the other end. Thus $h$ is a bijection as stated,
with derivative nowhere zero.

Every continuous exponent $s$ used in constructing $w$ has nonzero
real part. For a polynomial $p$, a primitive of $e^{st}p(t)$ is Part~II's
\eqref{nrr:sc:eq:primitive}. This preserves its nonzero leading degree. The
primitive vanishes at the convergent end in
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:integrated-coordinate}, so no constant mode is added. The
exact minimal polynomial is still $B$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Sufficiency and regularity in Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:main}]
Let $R$ satisfy the nonunit conditions. Apply
Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:integrating} to $B=R^\#$, and define $F(0)=0$ and
\begin{equation}
 F(h(t))=-h(t+1),\qquad F(-h(t))=h(t+1).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:odd-map}
\end{equation}
The same-direction change of $h(t)$ and $h(t+1)$ makes $F$ strictly
decreasing on each ray, and the signs make it strictly decreasing
across zero. The finite-end limit makes it continuous there, and the
infinite-end limit makes it onto. Its iterates satisfy
\begin{equation}
 F^{\circ n}(\pm h(t))=\pm(-1)^n h(t+n)
 \qquad(n\in\Z).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:odd-orbit}
\end{equation}
For every polynomial $Q$, the equation $Q[F]=0$ on the positive ray
is therefore equivalent to $Q(-E)h=0$. Since the exact shift polynomial
of $h$ is $R^\#$, the exact iterative polynomial of $F$ is $R$.

The inverse function theorem shows analyticity on both open rays, and
\begin{equation}
 |F'(\pm h(t))|=\frac{w(t+1)}{w(t)}.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:slope}
\end{equation}
This is bounded above and away from zero. The mean value theorem on
each ray and continuity at zero extend the same Lipschitz bounds across
zero. Thus $F$ is globally bi-Lipschitz.

To add one factor $z+1$, choose $b>0$, use $F(x)=-x$ on $[-b,b]$,
and replace \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:odd-map} by
\begin{equation}
 F(b+h(t))=-b-h(t+1),\qquad
 F(-b-h(t))=b+h(t+1).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:plateau-general}
\end{equation}
The exterior orbit becomes
$\pm(-1)^n(b+h(t+n))$. Since $R$ has no unit root, its new
$(-1)^n b$ component adds exactly the simple factor $z+1$.
The map is still globally bi-Lipschitz, with bounds incorporating the
slope $1$ on the central interval, and it is analytic away from the
two endpoints.

Finally, to add one factor $z-1$, translate any of these odd maps:
\begin{equation}
 F_c(x)=c+F(x-c),\qquad c\ne0.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:translate}
\end{equation}
For a polynomial $Q$,
$Q[F_c](x)=cQ(1)+Q[F](x-c)$. The second summand is odd as a function
of $x-c$. It can cancel the first everywhere only when both are zero.
Thus $\mu_{F_c}=\lcm(\mu_F,z-1)$, which adds exactly one copy of
$z-1$. The pure involution cases are realized by reflections.
All cases of \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:main-form} are now constructed.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Existence criterion]
\label{nrr:dc:cor:existence}
There is a decreasing continuous solution of $P[f]=0$ if and only if
$P$ has a negative real root.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Every permitted minimal polynomial has a negative real root. Conversely,
if $a<0$ and $P(a)=0$, the linear map $f(x)=ax$ is a solution.
\end{proof}

(Added in the merge.) Together with Part~II's
Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:core}(ii), which gives increasing solutions exactly
when $P$ has a positive real root, this says that $P[f]=0$ has a continuous
solution on $\R$ if and only if $P$ has a real root, every continuous
solution being monotone by Lemma~\ref{lem:homeo}.

\begin{remark}[Not every formal least common multiple is realizable]
The least common multiple of two decreasing minimal spectra need not
itself be a decreasing minimal spectrum. A contracting and an expanding
nonunit spectrum cannot coexist in one map. This is the precise point
at which the increasing and decreasing universal reductions differ.
\end{remark}

\section{The exact universal decreasing core}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:core}

The objective is a monic polynomial $\dcore(P)$ such that, for every
$Q\in\R[z]$,
\begin{equation}
 \left[
 \begin{array}{c}
 P[f]=0\ \Longrightarrow\ Q[f]=0\ \text{for every}\\[-2pt]
 \text{continuous decreasing }f:\R\to\R
 \end{array}\right]
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \dcore(P)\mid Q.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:universal-property}
\end{equation}
When there are no decreasing solutions, this is the vacuous property
and $\dcore(P)=1$. By Corollary~\ref{nrr:dc:cor:existence}, this happens
exactly when $P$ has no negative real root.

\subsection{A closed root-by-root construction}

Assume henceforth that at least one negative root exists. Set
\begin{equation}
 \varepsilon_+=\mathbf1_{P(1)=0},\qquad
 \varepsilon_-=\mathbf1_{P(-1)=0},\qquad
 U(z)=(z-1)^{\varepsilon_+}(z+1)^{\varepsilon_-}.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:unit-core}
\end{equation}
Construct two polynomials $R_<,R_>$, independently, as follows.
For the selected side $S=(0,1)$ or $S=(1,\infty)$, let
\[
 A_S=\{r\in S:P(-r)=0\}
\]
be its set of \emph{negative-anchor radii}.

\paragraph{No negative anchor.}
If $A_S$ is empty, set $R_S=1$.

\paragraph{One negative anchor.}
If $A_S=\{r\}$, let
\begin{equation}
 \kappa=2\left\lfloor\frac{m_P(-r)-1}{2}\right\rfloor+1,
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:odd-cap}
\end{equation}
the largest odd integer not exceeding $m_P(-r)$. Keep exactly the
roots on $|z|=r$, with multiplicities
\begin{equation}
 m_{R_S}(\lambda)=\min\{m_P(\lambda),\kappa\}.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:one-anchor}
\end{equation}
Discard all other roots on this side.

\paragraph{Two or more negative anchors.}
Let $r_- =\min A_S$ and $r_+=\max A_S$. Discard roots outside the
closed annulus $r_-\le|z|\le r_+$. Inside its open part, keep every
multiplicity unchanged. On its boundary circles set
\begin{equation}
 m_{R_S}(\lambda)=
 \begin{cases}
 \min\{m_P(\lambda),m_P(-r_-)\},&|\lambda|=r_-,\\
 \min\{m_P(\lambda),m_P(-r_+)\},&|\lambda|=r_+.
 \end{cases}
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:two-anchors}
\end{equation}
These instructions define $R_<:=R_{(0,1)}$ and
$R_>:=R_{(1,\infty)}$ completely. In particular, nonreal unit roots
are always discarded, and the multiplicities at $\pm1$ are clipped
to one by \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:unit-core}.

\begin{theorem}[Closed universal reduction]
\label{nrr:dc:thm:core}
With the conventions above,
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\ \dcore(P)=U R_<R_>\ }
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:core-formula}
\end{equation}
when decreasing solutions exist, and $\dcore(P)=1$ otherwise.
This polynomial has property \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:universal-property} and
divides $P$ up to its nonzero leading scalar.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Fix one side of the unit circle. Every nonconstant negative-admissible
divisor of $P$ on that side must have negative roots on both of its
extreme circles. If there is one available negative anchor, the divisor
must have just that circle; its negative-root multiplicity is odd and
at most $\kappa$, so \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:one-anchor} is an upper bound for all
its multiplicities. The polynomial defined there itself satisfies the
admissibility conditions.

With at least two available anchors, no root outside
$[r_-,r_+]$ can occur in such a divisor. A root on one of these outer
boundary circles, if used, must be bounded in multiplicity by the
negative root of that circle. Thus \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:two-anchors} bounds all
permitted divisors. In the open annulus there is no universal
multiplicity loss: the proposed $R_S$ includes all roots there and
uses the two outer negative anchors to satisfy its only extreme-circle
conditions. It is itself negative-admissible. Therefore $R_S$ is both
an upper bound for, and a member of, the set of possible nonunit
minimal spectra on the selected side.

Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:main} now implies that every decreasing
minimal polynomial dividing $P$ divides $UR_<R_>$. Conversely,
whenever $R_S\ne1$, that theorem realizes $UR_S$ as an exact minimal
polynomial. If both side polynomials are $1$, existence forces
$\varepsilon_-=1$, and $U$ is realized by an involution. Thus the least common
multiple of attainable minimal polynomials is exactly
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:core-formula}.
A polynomial $Q$ annihilates every solution exactly when it is a
multiple of each attainable minimal polynomial. This is
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:universal-property}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Added in the merge: the decreasing core through Part~II's positive core]
\label{nrr:dc:rem:reflected-core}
Let $P_<$ and $P_>$ be the monic factors of $P$ formed from its roots of
modulus less than and greater than $1$ (Part~II's $\mu_<$, $\mu_>$ applied to
$P$), and let $\pcore$ be Part~II's positive-recurrence core
(Section~\ref{nrr:sc:sec:core}), with $\pcore(1)=1$ and $1^\#=1$. If $P$ has
a negative real root, then
\[
 R_<=\pcore\bigl(P_<^\#\bigr)^\#,\qquad R_>=\pcore\bigl(P_>^\#\bigr)^\#,
 \qquad
 \dcore(P)=(z-1)^{\varepsilon_+}(z+1)^{\varepsilon_-}\,
 \pcore\bigl(P_<^\#\bigr)^\#\,\pcore\bigl(P_>^\#\bigr)^\#.
\]
Indeed the negative anchors on the side $S$ are exactly the positive roots of
$P_S^\#$, with $m_{P_S^\#}(r)=m_P(-r)$, and the three rules above are
\eqref{nrr:sc:eq:onecore} (with the same $\kappa$) and
\eqref{nrr:sc:eq:annularcore} for $B=P_S^\#$, read back through the
reflection. Compare Part~II's $\core(P)=(z-1)^{\delta(P)}\pcore(\scD P)$: the
increasing core applies $\pcore$ once, to all of $\scD P$ across the unit
circle, whereas the decreasing core applies it to the two sides separately,
keeps at most simple roots $\pm1$ and discards the other unit roots. The
two separate applications are why two witnesses may be needed
(Corollary~\ref{nrr:dc:cor:two-branches}). As a finite check made in the write
phase, the closed core of the shipped verifier
(\nolinkurl{code/03-decreasing-core-verify.py}) was compared with the displayed
formula evaluated by Part~II's \texttt{positive\_core}
(\nolinkurl{code/02-spectral-core-spectral_core.py}) on every profile of the
four continuous atlases of Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:verification} that has a
negative real root: $2{,}552$ of the $2{,}693$ profiles, with no disagreement.
The comparison script is not shipped; this description specifies it.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Two sharp branches, and the minimum witness count}

A \emph{witness family} for a universal core consists of solutions of
the original equation whose minimal polynomials have that core as their
least common multiple. Such a family certifies that no further factor
or multiplicity can be deleted universally.

\begin{corollary}[The sharp disjunction]
\label{nrr:dc:cor:two-branches}
If $R_<\ne1$ and $R_>\ne1$, every continuous decreasing solution of
$P[f]=0$ satisfies at least one of
\begin{equation}
 (UR_<)[f]=0\qquad\hbox{or}\qquad (UR_>)[f]=0.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:disjunction}
\end{equation}
Both maximal branch polynomials are attained by globally bi-Lipschitz
solutions. Exactly two witnesses are required for $\dcore(P)$.
If solutions exist and at most one side survives, exactly one witness
suffices. If there are no solutions, the empty family witnesses the
constant core $1$.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The nonunit roots of any one solution lie on a single side of the
unit circle. Its unit roots divide $U$, and its nonunit part divides
the corresponding side core. This proves the disjunction, including
involutions, which satisfy both branch equations when both are present.
The realization theorem supplies both maximal branches. Their least
common multiple is the core. A single witness in the two-sided case
would have a minimal polynomial containing nonunit roots on both sides,
contradicting Corollary~\ref{nrr:dc:cor:separation}. The remaining cases
follow from the same realization construction.
\end{proof}

The two-branch phenomenon in this corollary includes the classical
example discussed by Draga \cite[Remark~4]{D2016}. Its role here is a
benchmark for a fully explicit maximum, not a new discovery of the
existence of alternative reductions. The additional content is that
all roots, all multiplicities, both maximal branches, and the exact
minimum witness count are determined simultaneously.

\subsection{An exact affine-rigidity test}

\begin{theorem}[When every decreasing solution is affine]
\label{nrr:dc:thm:affine}
Assume $P$ has a negative real root, and use \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:unit-core}.
Every continuous decreasing solution of $P[f]=0$ is affine if and only
if one of the following mutually exclusive conditions holds:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),itemsep=2pt]
\item $\varepsilon_-=1$, $\varepsilon_+=0$, and $R_<=R_>=1$;
\item $\varepsilon_-=0$, and each nonconstant one of $R_<,R_>$ is linear.
\end{enumerate}
Under these conditions, every solution is homogeneous linear if and
only if $\varepsilon_+=0$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
In (i), the universal core is $z+1$, so $f=-\id$.
In (ii), any nonconstant branch has the form
$(z-1)^{\varepsilon_+}(z-a)$, $a<0$, $a\ne-1$. There is no two-cycle interval,
since $-1$ is absent. If the fixed point is $c$, every orbit's unit
component is the constant $c$. Centering the map removes it, leaving
only the root $a$ with multiplicity one. Thus
$f(x)=c+a(x-c)$. If $\varepsilon_+=0$, the fixed-point equation forces $c=0$.

Conversely, if $\varepsilon_-=0$ and a side core has degree at least two,
its exact realization cannot be affine: an affine decreasing map with
slope $a\ne-1$ has minimal polynomial $z-a$ or $(z-1)(z-a)$.
If $\varepsilon_-=1$ and a nonunit side survives, its realization with a
nondegenerate two-cycle interval is non-affine. Finally, when
$\varepsilon_-=\varepsilon_+=1$, the polynomial $z^2-1$ divides $P$ and admits
nonlinear decreasing involutions. For instance, conjugating reflection
by the nonodd increasing polynomial
$H(t)=t+t^2/4+t^3$ gives one; its derivative is positive, as checked
in Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:examples}.

If $\varepsilon_+=1$, any negative root $a$ gives nonzero translations of
$x\mapsto ax$ with minimal polynomial dividing $(z-1)(z-a)$, or
translated reflections when $a=-1$. Hence not all solutions are
homogeneous linear. This proves the final assertion.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Added in the merge: Petrov's question with nonzero constant coefficient]
\label{nrr:dc:rem:petrov}
Let $P(0)\ne0$. Every continuous solution of $P[f]=0$ on $\R$ is increasing
or decreasing (Lemma~\ref{lem:homeo}), so Part~II's
Theorem~\ref{nrr:sc:thm:rigidity} and Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:affine} together
decide Petrov's linearity question \cite{Petrov2016} for such $P$. Every
continuous solution is affine if and only if (a)~$P$ has no positive root, or
$\core(P)=z-r$ with $r>0$, or $\core(P)=(z-1)^2$; and (b)~$P$ has no negative
root, or condition (i) or~(ii) of Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:affine} holds.
Every continuous solution is homogeneous linear if and only if (a$'$)~$P$ has
no positive root or $\core(P)=z-r$ with $r>0$; and (b$'$)~$P$ has no negative
root, or (i) or~(ii) holds with $\varepsilon_+=0$. This combination is
immediate and is not claimed as a separate result of either manuscript.
Equations with $P(0)=0$, whose solutions need not be injective, remain open.
\end{remark}

\section{Finite-smoothness rigidity and global polynomial conjugacy}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:smooth}

The bi-Lipschitz realization theorem permits nonreal roots and repeated
roots of arbitrarily large multiplicity. Sufficient differentiability
at the finite end of the orbits eliminates both phenomena. The crucial
point is that this is a global theorem, not just a local linearization.
We prove it directly from the finite-dimensional composition space.

\subsection{One derivative already rules out a two-cycle interval}

\begin{proposition}[$C^1$ hyperbolicity]
\label{nrr:dc:prop:c1}
Let $f$ be a $C^1$ decreasing solution of $P[f]=0$ that is not an
involution. Then
\begin{equation}
 \Fix(f^{\circ2})=\{c\},\qquad a=f'(c)<0,\qquad a\ne-1,
 \qquad P(a)=0.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:hyperbolic}
\end{equation}
Put $\sigma=1$ if $|a|<1$ and $\sigma=-1$ if $|a|>1$.
Then $f^{\circ(\sigma n)}(x)\to c$ for every $x\in\R$ as
$n\to+\infty$. Near $c$, the map $f^{\circ\sigma}$ is a strict
Lipschitz contraction.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
The inverse is $C^1$ and $f'$ is negative everywhere by
Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:homeomorphism}. Suppose the interval
$[\alpha,\beta]=\Fix(f^{\circ2})$ is nondegenerate, and put
$g=f^{\circ2}$. Since $g=\id$ on this interval, differentiability at
$\beta$ gives $g'(\beta)=1$; the inverse derivative is also $1$.
Choose $x>\beta$, and let $\tau\in\{1,-1\}$ be the time direction
in which $g^{\circ(\tau n)}(x)\to\beta$. The positive sequence
\[
 u_n=g^{\circ(\tau n)}(x)-\beta
\]
is a recurrence sequence with limit zero. Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:limits},
applied to the even subsequence of the original orbit, says that all
its active roots have modulus less than $1$. Therefore
$u_n=O(\rho^n)$ for some $\rho<1$. Differentiability at $\beta$,
however, gives $u_{n+1}/u_n\to1$. A positive sequence with this ratio
limit has $u_n^{1/n}\to1$, contradicting the exponential upper bound.
Hence the interval is a singleton.

If $a=-1$, the identical argument at $c$, where $g'(c)=a^2=1$,
gives the same contradiction. Thus $a\ne-1$. Differentiating
$P[f]=0$ at the fixed point gives $P(a)=0$.

The common convergent time direction already exists by
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:interval}. In that direction the successive ratios
of the positive even distances tend to $|a|^{2\tau}$.
They cannot exceed $1$ in the limit, and equality was just ruled out.
Thus $|a|^\tau<1$ and $\tau=\sigma$. Continuity of the derivative
makes $f^{\circ\sigma}$ a local strict contraction. The two-cycle
interval is a singleton, so both parities of every orbit converge
to $c$ in this direction.
\end{proof}

\subsection{An explicit regularity threshold}

For a nonconstant $P$ with $P(0)\ne0$, write
\[
 r_-(P)=\min_{\lambda\in\roots(P)}|\lambda|,
 \qquad r_+(P)=\max_{\lambda\in\roots(P)}|\lambda|.
\]
For each negative real root $a\ne-1$, define
\begin{equation}
 \Theta_P(a)=
 \begin{cases}
 \displaystyle\frac{\log r_-(P)}{\log|a|},&-1<a<0,\\[7pt]
 \displaystyle\frac{\log r_+(P)}{\log|a|},&a<-1.
 \end{cases}
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:threshold-ratio}
\end{equation}
Both quantities, when used, are at least $1$. Let $\Theta(P)$ be their
maximum; set $\Theta(P)=0$ when there is no such negative root. Choose any
integer
\begin{equation}
 N\ge1,\qquad N>\Theta(P).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:N}
\end{equation}
For a nonzero constant $P$, set $\Theta(P)=0$; its solution class is empty.
For example, $N=\lfloor \Theta(P)\rfloor+1$ works. This is a sufficient
threshold, not a claim that the least sufficient regularity has been
found in every case. Roots that can already be removed by
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:core} may be removed before computing it, to improve
the bound.

\begin{lemma}[Finite jets detect the whole composition space]
\label{nrr:dc:lem:jet}
Let $f$ be a non-involutive $C^N$ decreasing solution, with $N$ as in
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:N}. Center it at its fixed point:
\[
 g(x)=f(c+x)-c,
 \qquad V=\operatorname{span}_{\R}\{\id,g,g^{\circ2},\ldots\}.
\]
The map taking a function in $V$ to its Taylor coefficients of orders
$1,\ldots,N$ at $0$ is injective.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
Evaluation at the fixed point gives $cP(1)=0$, so $P[g]=0$.
Every function in $V$ vanishes at $0$, and right composition
$E_g\colon v\mapsto v\circ g$ is invertible on $V$. Its minimal polynomial
divides $P$. Let $a=g'(0)$ and $\sigma$ be as in
Proposition~\ref{nrr:dc:prop:c1}; set
\begin{equation}
 b=|a|^\sigma<1,
 \qquad \omega=\min_{\lambda\in\roots(P)}|\lambda|^\sigma
 =\begin{cases}r_-(P),&\sigma=1,\\1/r_+(P),&\sigma=-1.\end{cases}
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:kappa}
\end{equation}
Condition \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:N} gives $b^N<\omega$. Choose $q$ with
$b<q<1$ and $q^N<\omega$.

Suppose $v\in V$ has zero derivatives through order $N$ at $0$.
Taylor's theorem gives $v(y)=O(|y|^N)$ near zero. Every orbit
$g^{\circ(\sigma n)}(x)$ eventually enters a neighborhood on which
$g^{\circ\sigma}$ has Lipschitz constant at most $q$. Hence, for each
fixed $x$,
\[
 |g^{\circ(\sigma n)}(x)|\le C_xq^n,
 \qquad v(g^{\circ(\sigma n)}(x))=O(q^{Nn}).
\]
The latter is a recurrence sequence whose possible roots are
$\lambda^\sigma$, with $\lambda$ a root of $P$. All have modulus
at least $\omega$. By Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:limits}(iii), the sequence must
be zero. In particular $v(x)=0$. Since $x$ was arbitrary, $v=0$.
\end{proof}

The proof is the finite-regularity replacement for an analytic identity
theorem. A nonzero element of the composition space cannot vanish too
rapidly at the fixed point: exponential contraction would make its
orbit decay faster than any permitted recurrence mode.

\begin{theorem}[Finite-smoothness polynomial-coordinate theorem]
\label{nrr:dc:thm:finite-threshold}
Let $N$ satisfy \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:N}. Every $C^N$ decreasing solution of
$P[f]=0$ is either an involution or has the unique global representation
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:poly-conjugacy}. In the latter case it is real analytic,
real algebraic, and globally bi-Lipschitz, with exact minimal polynomial
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:smooth-spectrum}. In particular,
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:smooth} holds for $C^\infty$ solutions.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Suppose the solution is not an involution and retain the notation of
Lemma~\ref{nrr:dc:lem:jet}. The order-of-vanishing filtration on $V$ is
separated by the first $N$ derivatives. Each successive graded piece
has dimension at most one: its first nonzero Taylor coefficient is a
scalar. Thus $V$ has a basis of distinct vanishing orders
\[
 K\subseteq\{1,\ldots,N\},\qquad 1\in K.
\]
Right composition by $g(x)=ax+o(x)$ preserves this filtration and acts
on the order-$k$ graded piece by $a^k$. The matrix of $E_g$ is triangular
with diagonal entries $a^k$, $k\in K$. These entries are distinct, since
$|a|\ne1$. Therefore $E_g$ is diagonalizable over $\R$.
An eigenfunction with eigenvalue $a^k$ has vanishing order exactly $k$,
as comparison of its leading Taylor coefficient shows.

Choose the eigenfunction $\psi$ with eigenvalue $a$, normalized by
$\psi'(0)=1$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \psi(0)=0,\qquad \psi\circ g=a\psi.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:schroeder}
\end{equation}
Its derivative is positive near zero. Differentiating the iterated
identity at an arbitrary point gives
\begin{equation}
 \psi'(g^{\circ n}(x))(g^{\circ n})'(x)=a^n\psi'(x)
 \qquad(n\in\Z).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:propagate-derivative}
\end{equation}
Take $n=\sigma j$ with $j$ large enough to enter that neighborhood.
Both $(g^{\circ n})'(x)$ and $a^n$ have sign $(-1)^n$, and neither
vanishes. Therefore $\psi'(x)>0$ everywhere.
Its range is an interval. Equation \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:schroeder}, in the
opposite time direction, produces arbitrarily large positive and
negative values from any nonzero value of $\psi$. Its range is
therefore all of $\R$. Thus $\psi$ is a global $C^N$ diffeomorphism.
Let $H=\psi^{-1}$; then $H(0)=0$, $H'(0)=1$, and $H'>0$.

For an eigenfunction $v_k$ with eigenvalue $a^k$, set
$w_k(t)=v_k(H(t))$. It obeys
\[
 w_k(at)=a^k w_k(t),\qquad
 w_k(t)=\gamma_k t^k+o(t^k)\quad(t\to0),\qquad\gamma_k\ne0.
\]
For fixed $t$, use $a^{\sigma j}t\to0$ to obtain
\[
 w_k(t)=a^{-k\sigma j}w_k(a^{\sigma j}t)
       \longrightarrow\gamma_k t^k.
\]
The left side is independent of $j$, so $w_k(t)=\gamma_k t^k$
for every real $t$. Expand $\id$ in the eigenbasis of $E_g$ and compose
with $H$. The result is a polynomial identity
\begin{equation}
 H(t)=\sum_{k\in K}h_kt^k.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:H-polynomial}
\end{equation}
Every $h_k$ is nonzero: if the cyclic vector $\id$ missed an eigenspace,
all its iterates would miss it, contradicting the definition of $V$.
Since $H$ is an increasing polynomial bijection, its degree is odd
and its leading coefficient is positive. Equation \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:schroeder}
now yields \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:poly-conjugacy} globally.

The fixed point $c$ and multiplier $a$ are intrinsic. For uniqueness
of $H$, if $H_1,H_2$ are two normalized coordinates, then
$L=H_1^{-1}\circ H_2$ commutes with $t\mapsto at$ and
$L(t)=t+o(t)$ at zero. The same scaling argument gives $L(t)=t$
everywhere. Hence $H_1=H_2$.

In the $t$ coordinate, the scalar iterates are
\begin{equation}
 f^{\circ n}(c+H(t))=c+\sum_{k\in K}h_k a^{kn}t^k.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:smooth-iterates}
\end{equation}
Distinct $a^k$ and the independent functions $t^k$ give the exact
minimal polynomial \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:smooth-spectrum}; the constant mode
is present precisely when $c\ne0$. Since $H'>0$, the inverse
function theorem makes $H^{-1}$ real analytic at every real point.
Thus $f$ is real analytic. Eliminating $t$ from
$x=c+H(t)$ and $y=c+H(at)$ shows that $f$ is real algebraic.
Finally,
\[
 f'(c+H(t))=a\frac{H'(at)}{H'(t)}.
\]
Its absolute value is positive and continuous, and tends to
$|a|^{\deg H}$ as $t\to\pm\infty$. It therefore has positive lower
and finite upper bounds, proving global bi-Lipschitz regularity.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Automatic analyticity with the exact exception]
\label{nrr:dc:cor:automatic}
If $P$ is not divisible by $z^2-1$, every decreasing $C^N$ solution,
with $N$ as in \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:N}, is real analytic. If $z^2-1$ divides
$P$, nonanalytic $C^\infty$ involutions are genuine exceptions.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
The theorem handles non-involutions. An involution has minimal
polynomial $z+1$ or $z^2-1$; without the second divisor only $-\id$
can occur.
For the exception, take a nonzero smooth bump $\eta$ supported in
$(1,2)$, small enough that $\psi(x)=x+\eta(x)$ has positive derivative.
Then $f=\psi^{-1}\circ(-\id)\circ\psi$ is a smooth decreasing
involution. It equals $-x$ outside a compact set but is not $-x$
everywhere, so the real-analytic identity theorem shows that it is
not globally real analytic.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Spectral deletion under sufficient regularity]
\label{nrr:dc:cor:deletion}
Every nonreal root and every repeated root can be deleted from a
universal annihilator for decreasing $C^\infty$ solutions, and also
for decreasing $C^N$ solutions at the threshold \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:N}.
In particular all their minimal polynomials are squarefree and real-rooted.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
For non-involutions use \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:smooth-spectrum}; for involutions
use $\mu_f\mid z^2-1$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Algebraic does not mean rational}

\begin{corollary}[Rational solutions are affine]
\label{nrr:dc:cor:rational}
A decreasing rational function defined on all of $\R$ that satisfies
$P[f]=0$ is affine.
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
Its rational iterates span a finite-dimensional space. A common
denominator for a fixed finite basis shows that their degrees as
rational maps are uniformly bounded. If $d$ is the degree of $f$
as a rational map of the complex projective line, its $n$th iterate
has degree $d^n$. For completeness, degree multiplicativity follows
by homogenizing coprime numerator--denominator pairs: substitution of
a pair of degree $e$ into a pair of degree $d$ yields degree $de$
and no common projective zero, hence no cancellation. Boundedness
therefore forces $d=1$.
A real degree-one rational map with a nonconstant denominator has a
real pole. Since $f$ is defined on all of $\R$, its denominator must
be constant. Thus $f$ is affine.
\end{proof}

The polynomial-coordinate theorem supplies many nonlinear algebraic
solutions, but this corollary prevents them from simplifying to
nonlinear rational functions. The degree of $H$ bounds the algebraic
degree of $f$ over $\R(x)$; that bound need not be asserted to be sharp
in every specialization.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch~54: the bound is attained: for every
coordinate of Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:finite-threshold} the degree of $f$ over
$\R(x)$ is exactly $\deg H$ (Part~IV, Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:normalized}).]

\section{Exact smooth spectra and their universal core}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:smooth-core}

\subsection{Which integer-power spectra actually occur?}

\begin{theorem}[Smooth spectral occurrence]
\label{nrr:dc:thm:smooth-occurrence}
Apart from $z+1$ and $z^2-1$, the polynomials occurring as decreasing
$C^\infty$ minimal spectra are exactly
\begin{equation}
 (z-1)^{\varepsilon_+}\prod_{k\in K}(z-a^k),
 \qquad \varepsilon_+\in\{0,1\},\quad a<0,\ a\ne-1,
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:smooth-occurrence}
\end{equation}
where $K$ is a finite set of positive integers containing $1$ and
having odd maximum. Each is realized by a globally real-analytic,
globally bi-Lipschitz map. Thus the $C^\infty$ and real-analytic classes
have exactly the same possible minimal polynomials.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
Necessity follows from Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:finite-threshold} applied to
$P=\mu_f$. For sufficiency, let $M=\max K$. If $M=1$, take $H(t)=t$.
For odd $M\ge3$, start with
\[
 H_0(t)=t+t^M,\qquad H_0'(t)=1+Mt^{M-1}>0.
\]
For every $1<k<M$ in $K$, the function
$k|t|^{k-1}/(1+M|t|^{M-1})$ is bounded on $\R$. Add sufficiently
small nonzero multiples of these $t^k$ to $H_0$. The derivative stays
positive, the support becomes exactly $K$, and the normalization
$H'(0)=1$ is unchanged. Define
$f(c+H(t))=c+H(at)$, taking $c=0$ when $\varepsilon_+=0$ and $c=1$ when
$\varepsilon_+=1$. The exact formula \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:smooth-iterates} proves
the claimed minimum. The two involution spectra have analytic reflection
realizations.
\end{proof}

The odd-maximum condition is indispensable. For example, the support
$\{1,2\}$ cannot be the support of an increasing polynomial bijection
of $\R$, while $\{1,2,3\}$ can. The roots $a^k$ are not arbitrary
real roots of the correct signs: the same multiplier $a$ must account
for every one of them by an integer exponent.

\subsection{The exact smooth reduction formula}

Let $\varepsilon_+=\mathbf1_{P(1)=0}$. For each negative root $a\ne-1$ of
$P$, form the finite set
\begin{equation}
 K_P(a)=\{k\ge1:P(a^k)=0\}.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:KPa}
\end{equation}
It is finite because the powers of $a$ are distinct, and it contains
$1$. Let $M_P(a)$ be its largest odd member, and set
\begin{equation}
 J_P(a)=K_P(a)\cap\{1,\ldots,M_P(a)\},\qquad
 B_a(z)=(z-1)^{\varepsilon_+}\prod_{k\in J_P(a)}(z-a^k).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:Ba}
\end{equation}
If $P(-1)=0$, also form the involution branch
\begin{equation}
 B_{\rm inv}(z)=(z-1)^{\varepsilon_+}(z+1).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:Binv}
\end{equation}
Let $\mathcal B(P)$ consist of all the polynomials in
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:Ba} and, when present, \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:Binv}.

\begin{theorem}[Universal smooth core]
\label{nrr:dc:thm:smooth-core}
The polynomial
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{\ \smoothcore(P)=\lcm_{B\in\mathcal B(P)}B\ }
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:smoothcore}
\end{equation}
with the empty least common multiple interpreted as $1$, satisfies
\begin{equation}
 \bigl[P[f]=0\Rightarrow Q[f]=0
       \text{ for all decreasing }C^\infty\text{ maps }f\bigr]
 \quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \smoothcore(P)\mid Q.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:smooth-universal}
\end{equation}
The same polynomial works for globally real-analytic solutions and
for $C^N$ solutions whenever $N>\Theta(P)$.
Every branch $B\in\mathcal B(P)$ is itself attained as an exact
real-analytic minimal polynomial, and every solution in any of these
regularity classes has minimum dividing at least one branch.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
For a non-involutive smooth solution, its multiplier $a$ is a negative
root of $P$. Its coordinate support $K$ lies in $K_P(a)$, contains
$1$, and has odd maximum. Therefore $K\subseteq J_P(a)$, and its
minimal polynomial divides $B_a$. Conversely, $J_P(a)$ itself has
odd maximum, so Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:smooth-occurrence} realizes the
whole branch $B_a$. Involutions are exactly accounted for by $B_{\rm inv}$.
Taking the least common multiple proves the universal property and
its sharpness.
The finite-smoothness theorem says that the $C^N$ class introduces
no additional non-involutive spectra. Any additional nonanalytic
involutions still have minimum dividing $B_{\rm inv}$. Since all
maximal branches have analytic realizations, the core is unchanged.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Exact computation versus floating-point root plots]
Formulas \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:KPa}--\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:Ba} use equality of real
numbers, not a tolerance test on approximate logarithms. For a polynomial with
rational or algebraic coefficients, root isolation and exact algebraic
arithmetic can decide the finitely many tests. A finite exponent bound
comes from the extreme moduli of $P$: for $|a|>1$, only
$k\le\log r_+(P)/\log|a|$ can occur; for $|a|<1$, only
$k\le\log r_-(P)/\log|a|$ can occur. Certified overestimates suffice.
The formulas do not promise a particular bit-complexity for the root
isolation step.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Smooth witness complexity is unbounded}

Because \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:smoothcore} is squarefree, the least number of
smooth witnesses is the minimum number of branch root sets needed to cover
$\roots(\smoothcore(P))$. This is an exact finite covering formulation:
every solution is contained in one branch, and every branch can be
realized. Branches contained in larger branches may first be discarded.
No general complexity classification of this special covering problem
is needed for the following sharp contrast with
Corollary~\ref{nrr:dc:cor:two-branches}.

\begin{theorem}[One continuous witness versus arbitrarily many smooth witnesses]
\label{nrr:dc:thm:witness-growth}
For every positive integer $m$, there is a polynomial $P_m$ whose
continuous decreasing core is attained by one globally bi-Lipschitz
$C^1$ solution, but whose smooth core requires exactly $m$ witnesses.
Indeed, let
\begin{equation}
 r_j=2+\frac{j}{m+1}\quad(1\le j\le m),\qquad
 P_m(z)=\prod_{j=1}^m(z+r_j).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:clustered}
\end{equation}
Its only decreasing $C^2$ solutions are $f(x)=-r_jx$, $1\le j\le m$.
For $m\ge2$, a single exact-core $C^1$ realization is not $C^2$.
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
All roots of $P_m$ are negative, distinct, and outside the unit circle,
so the continuous core is $P_m$ itself. Since $2<r_j<3$, no positive
integer power of $r_j$ of exponent at least two belongs to the interval
$(2,3)$. Hence $K_{P_m}(-r_j)=\{1\}$, and each smooth branch is just
$z+r_j$. Also
\[
 \Theta(P_m)=\frac{\log r_m}{\log r_1}<\frac{\log3}{\log2}<2.
\]
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:finite-threshold} therefore applies already at $C^2$.
There is no unit root, so its only $C^2$ solutions are the stated
homogeneous linear maps. Their distinct singleton spectra show that
exactly $m$ witnesses are required.

For the $C^1$ realization, set $h(t)=\sum_{j=1}^m r_j^t$ and use
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:odd-map}. This positive increasing coordinate has exact
shift spectrum $\{r_1,\ldots,r_m\}$, and the resulting $F$ has minimum
$P_m$. Its derivative on the positive ray is
\[
 F'(h(t))=-\frac{\sum_j(\log r_j)r_j^{t+1}}
                    {\sum_j(\log r_j)r_j^t},
\]
a negative weighted average of the $r_j$. As $t\to-\infty$, both
$F(h(t))/h(t)$ and $F'(h(t))$ tend to $-r_1$. Odd extension makes
$F$ a global $C^1$ diffeomorphism.
If $m\ge2$, put $\gamma=\log r_2/\log r_1\in(1,2)$.
Writing $x=h(t)$ at the finite end gives
\[
 F(x)+r_1x
 =-\sum_{j=2}^m(r_j-r_1)r_j^t
 \sim -(r_2-r_1)x^\gamma\qquad(x\downarrow0).
\]
This is incompatible with the $O(x^2)$ remainder of a $C^2$ function
with value $0$ and derivative $-r_1$ at zero. Thus the realization
is not $C^2$.
\end{proof}

The witness-count difference is not due to root multiplicities,
nonreal roots, opposite sides of the unit circle, or a nonzero fixed
point: none of those occurs in \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:clustered}. It is caused
solely by the common-multiplier constraint imposed by smoothness.

\section{Examples, sharp boundaries, and exact certificates}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:examples}

\subsection{A classical benchmark: contraction or expansion, not both}

Take
\[
 P(z)=(z+2)(z+\tfrac12).
\]
The two continuous branch cores are $z+2$ and $z+\tfrac12$, so the
only decreasing solutions are $-2x$ and $-x/2$. Their least common
multiple is $P$, but no single solution has that minimal polynomial.
This is the quadratic example behind Draga's Remark~4
\cite{D2016}, not a new counterexample. It illustrates why a sharp
universal annihilator need not itself be a minimal polynomial of one
solution.

\subsection{A nondegenerate two-cycle interval}

For $b>0$ and $r>0$, $r\ne1$, define
\begin{equation}
 F(x)=
 \begin{cases}
 -x,&|x|\le b,\\
 -rx+(r-1)b\sgn(x),&|x|>b.
 \end{cases}
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:piecewise-plateau}
\end{equation}
It is odd, decreasing, and globally bi-Lipschitz. Its square fixes
exactly $[-b,b]$. For $x>b$,
\[
 F^{\circ n}(x)=(-1)^n\bigl(b+r^n(x-b)\bigr),
\]
so its exact minimal polynomial is $(z+1)(z+r)$. At the endpoints,
the magnitudes of the one-sided derivatives are $1$ and $r$.
Thus the map is not $C^1$, in agreement with
Proposition~\ref{nrr:dc:prop:c1}. The verifier checks the identity for
$b=1$, $r=2$ at 401 rational points, while the displayed orbit formula
proves it for all real inputs.

\subsection{Nonreal roots really survive in the decreasing class}

Let
\begin{equation}
 h(t)=2^t\left(1+\frac1{100}\cos\frac{2\pi t}{3}\right),
 \qquad F(h(t))=-h(t+1),\qquad F(-x)=-F(x).
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:cubic-example}
\end{equation}
The derivative of $h$ is positive: the constant term in its bracket
is $\log2$, and the sum of the absolute perturbation amplitudes is
at most $(\log2+2\pi/3)/100<\log2$.
The limits at the two ends make $h$ an increasing bijection onto
$(0,\infty)$. Its exact shift polynomial is $z^3-8$, with three
nonzero spectral components. Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \mu_F=z^3+8=(z+2)(z^2-2z+4),\qquad F^{\circ3}(x)=-8x.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:cubic-minimum}
\end{equation}
Its orbit beginning at $h(0)$ has exact first values
\[
 \frac{101}{100},\quad-\frac{199}{100},\quad
 \frac{199}{50},\quad-\frac{202}{25},\quad
 \frac{398}{25},\quad-\frac{796}{25}.
\]
The $3\times3$ Hankel determinant of its first five values is
$-27/625\ne0$, independently certifying that the orbit has no
recurrence of order below three (the argument of
Section~\ref{nrr:sc:app:hankel}). The map is globally bi-Lipschitz
and analytic off zero, but is not differentiable at zero: the quotient
$F(h(t))/h(t)$ is a nonconstant three-periodic function of $t$, so it
has no limit as $t\to-\infty$. Consistently, $\Theta(z^3+8)=1$ gives a
$C^2$ obstruction from the finite-smoothness theorem as well.

This construction is the orientation-reversed counterpart of Part~I's
increasing cubic mechanism (Theorem~\ref{thm:cubic}, with minimal polynomial
$z^3-8$). Its value here is as a transparent sharpness example for the new
classification, not as the principal novelty of Part~III.

\subsection{Multiplicity is constrained at the extremes, not inside}

For every positive integer $m$, the polynomial
\begin{equation}
 R_m(z)=(z+2)(z^2+9)^m(z+4)
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:interior-multiplicity}
\end{equation}
is negative-admissible. Its negative extreme roots have multiplicity
one, while the interior nonreal roots $\pm3i$ have arbitrary
multiplicity $m$. Thus $R_m$ is realized by a globally bi-Lipschitz
decreasing map. No sufficiently smooth solution has this exact
minimum. In fact $\Theta(R_m)=\log4/\log2=2$, so $C^3$ already rules it
out, regardless of $m$.

At two distinct extreme radii, even multiplicities are permitted:
\[
 (z+2)^2(z+4)^2
\]
is realizable, whereas $(z+2)^2$ is not a minimal decreasing spectrum.
For the former, an explicit positive envelope is
\[
 w(t)=(1-t)2^t+(1+t)4^t>0.
\]
On $[-1,1]$ both summands are nonnegative. Outside that interval,
the ratio of a negative summand to the positive one is less than
$1/2$. Integrating from $-\infty$ gives
\[
 h(t)=2^t\left(\frac{1-t}{\log2}+\frac1{(\log2)^2}\right)
 +4^t\left(\frac{1+t}{\log4}-\frac1{(\log4)^2}\right),
\]
and \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:odd-map} realizes the claimed multiplicities exactly.
The parity condition belongs to a \emph{single} circle, not independently
to each of two extreme circles. (Compare Part~II's increasing example
$(z-2)^2(z-4)^2$, Section~\ref{nrr:sc:sec:examples}.)

\subsection{A nonlinear analytic map with a positive characteristic root}

Let
\begin{equation}
 H(t)=t+\tfrac14t^2+t^3,
 \qquad H'(t)=3\left(t+\tfrac1{12}\right)^2+\tfrac{47}{48}>0.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:H-example}
\end{equation}
Define $f(H(t))=H(-2t)$. Then $f$ is decreasing, globally analytic,
and nonlinear, with
\begin{equation}
 \mu_f=(z+2)(z-4)(z+8)=z^3+6z^2-24z-64.
 \label{nrr:dc:eq:smooth-cubic}
\end{equation}
Thus smooth rigidity does not mean that every root must be negative.
The positive root $4=(-2)^2$ is an allowed even power of the common
negative multiplier. Its orbit at $H(1)=9/4$ begins
\[
 \tfrac94,\quad -9,\quad72,\quad-504,\quad4176.
\]
The corresponding $3\times3$ Hankel determinant is $46656\ne0$.
The exact polynomial identity
\[
 H((-2)^3t)+6H((-2)^2t)-24H(-2t)-64H(t)=0
\]
is checked coefficient by coefficient by the shipped verifier.
A nonzero translation of the fixed point adds exactly the simple
factor $z-1$.

Using the same nonodd $H$ with multiplier $-1$ gives a nonlinear
analytic involution. It has minimum $z^2-1$, not $z+1$:
if it were $-\id$, the defining identity would force $H$ to be odd.
This is why the involutive exception must be retained in the smooth theorem.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{A sharp $C^1$ versus $C^2$ boundary}{A sharp C1 versus C2 boundary}}

An especially simple non-polynomial coordinate is
\[
 h(t)=2^t+3^t,\qquad F(h(t))=-h(t+1),\qquad F(-x)=-F(x).
\]
Its exact minimum is $(z+2)(z+3)$. Its derivative on the positive
ray lies between $-3$ and $-2$ and tends to $-2$ at zero, so the
odd extension is $C^1$. Write $y=2^t$ and
$\gamma=\log3/\log2\in(1,2)$. Then
\[
 x=y+y^\gamma,\qquad F(x)=-2x-y^\gamma
       =-2x-x^\gamma+o(x^\gamma)\quad(x\downarrow0).
\]
It is not $C^2$. Conversely $\Theta((z+2)(z+3))=\log3/\log2<2$,
so every decreasing $C^2$ solution is polynomial-coordinate and the
smooth-core formula reduces it to $-2x$ or $-3x$.
This verifies the sharpness of the sufficient threshold in this
particular equation; it does not prove that the bound is optimal for
all input polynomials. (Part~I's Section~\ref{sec:regularity} records the
corresponding $C^1$ versus $C^{1,\alpha}$ boundary for increasing maps on one
circle.)

\section{Verification, effective use, and a formalization route}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:verification}

\subsection{What the exact computations check}

The verifier \nolinkurl{code/03-decreasing-core-verify.py} (delivered as
\texttt{code/verify.py}; the file of that name here is Part~I's verifier)
uses only Python's standard library and exact rational arithmetic. It
represents a root profile by its radius, its type (negative real, other real,
conjugate pair, or unit root), and its multiplicity. Two deliberately separate
procedures are compared: a direct admissibility predicate and the closed
universal-core construction of Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:core}. For each input
profile, every divisor profile is enumerated; the least common multiple of all
admissible divisors must equal the proposed closed core. The attained
branch and one-versus-two-witness criteria are checked as well.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
\toprule
Atlas & Input profiles & Divisor tests\\
\midrule
Squarefree, two sides and unit roots & 1,024 & 59,049\\
Single-circle multiplicities & 1,125 & 121,500\\
Interior and boundary multiplicities & 288 & 12,600\\
Two-sided multiplicities & 256 & 10,000\\
\midrule
Total across the four atlases & 2,693 & 203,149\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Every test passed. A separate exact-rational smooth-spectrum atlas checks
1,024 input root sets and 59,049 candidate divisors against the
integer-power and odd-maximum formula of
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:smooth-core}; all of those checks passed as well. The
table above counts only the continuous-core checks. These counts are test
instances across the four atlases, not a claim that all profiles across
different atlases are distinct. The script also checks the two cubic Hankel
determinants, the polynomial-coordinate identity, the rational plateau sample,
and an eight-slope instance of the witness family
\eqref{nrr:dc:eq:clustered}. Recorded output is supplied in both
\nolinkurl{data/03-decreasing-core-verification.json} and
\nolinkurl{data/03-decreasing-core-verification.txt} (delivered as
\texttt{results/verification.json} and \texttt{results/verification.txt};
the two files are byte-identical, and both hold the JSON record). (Merge note:
in the write phase, 29 September 2026, Python~3.14.4, the verifier was rerun
in a scratch copy with its delivered name restored, in about 2~seconds; it
passed, and its JSON output and its printed output equal the shipped records
apart from Windows line endings. It also passes under \texttt{python -O}, as
its checks raise explicit exceptions rather than use \texttt{assert}.)

These are finite consistency checks. In particular, the profile
predicate encodes the theorem's stated conditions; exhaustive
agreement with it does not establish their equivalence to existence
of a real map. The all-real construction, the spectral necessity,
and the finite-smoothness theorem rest on the proofs in Part~III,
not on numerical experimentation or exhaustive sampling.

\subsection{A practical decision workflow}

For a polynomial with certified algebraic roots, the following workflow
produces exact answers.
First, test for a negative real root; without one, the decreasing
solution class is empty. Next construct $R_<,R_>,U$ using exact
modulus comparisons and multiplicities. This gives the continuous
core, its maximum one or two branches, and the affine-rigidity test.
To restrict regularity, compute a certified upper bound for $\Theta(P)$
and choose an integer strictly above it. The finite-smoothness theorem
then reduces the spectral problem to the power tests $P(a^k)=0$.
These tests generate the branches $B_a$ and the involution branch,
and their least common multiple gives the smooth core.

The construction of a bi-Lipschitz witness is effective in principle:
choose the finite exponential modes, enlarge $\Lambda$ until the envelope's
two tail inequalities hold, and choose a positive perturbation smaller
than a certified domination bound. The proofs establish termination of
these searches, but the supplied checker does not implement a general
certified transcendental inequality engine. An implementation of that
engine would be a separate deliverable, not something hidden behind
the exact finite test counts.

\subsection{A possible Lean development}

A formal proof should begin with the right-composition module, not
with an attempt to normalize nested nonlinear expressions. The
natural algebraic layer consists of a finite-dimensional cyclic space,
an invertible composition operator, orbit evaluation maps, and the
least-common-multiple description of the minimal polynomial.
The recurrence layer can then establish the exponential-polynomial
basis and its growth lemmas. The order-topological layer proves the
two-cycle interval and the common finite-end direction.

The continuous occurrence theorem additionally needs the positive
envelope, domination of finitely many exponential-polynomial modes,
and the analytic inverse function theorem on rays. The finite-smoothness
result takes a different route after the recurrence layer: local
contraction, Taylor remainder bounds, finite-jet injectivity,
diagonalization by distinct vanishing orders, and global propagation
of an eigenfunction coordinate.

A useful formalization milestone would be the finite-smoothness
polynomial-coordinate theorem conditional only on an abstract
recurrence growth lemma. A second milestone would discharge that
lemma. A third would formalize the root-profile algorithms and show
that their returned polynomials have the universal properties. This
separation makes the trusted assumptions visible. No Lean source is
included in the delivered package, and no existing repository formalization
is claimed to certify these results. (As of 29 September 2026 none of these
milestones, nor any stage of Part~II's plan, has been started in ProveIt.)

\section{Further research questions}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:research}

The following problems are proposed continuations, not assertions that
no related special case has ever been studied. They distinguish
questions left open by Part~III from statements already proved here.

\subsection{Optimal finite differentiability}

Determine the least integer $N_{\rm opt}(P)$ such that every decreasing
$C^{N_{\rm opt}(P)}$ solution is either an involution or polynomial-coordinate.
The bound $N>\Theta(P)$ is explicit but can overcount roots that are
incompatible with a particular multiplier. A stronger algorithm should
combine the continuous branch reduction, the derivative multiplier,
and exact integer-power resonances before assigning a threshold.
The equation $f(x)+2x=0$ is already rigid at
$C^0$, whereas the two-root examples in Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:examples}
show that $C^1$ genuinely fails where $C^2$ succeeds.

\subsection{Complete intermediate-regularity spectra}

Classify minimal spectra for each fixed $C^k$ class below the automatic
analyticity threshold, and for H\"older or Zygmund regularity. The
coordinate terms suggest a mixture of fractional powers, logarithmic
powers from repeated roots, and oscillations in $\log|x-c|$ from
nonreal roots. A complete theorem must handle cancellations at the
same asymptotic degree and the matching of the two sides of the fixed
point; an inspection of one mode is not enough.

\subsection{All continuous solutions, rather than only their spectra}

The continuous theorem classifies which minimal polynomials occur
and constructs a witness, but it does not parametrize every continuous
solution with a given minimum. Develop a complete parameter space for
such solutions, including the possible involution on the central
interval and compatible data on an exterior fundamental interval.
The recurrence equation makes that data highly constrained, while
monotonicity imposes an infinite family of inequalities.

\subsection{Optimal distortion and quantitative realizations}

For an admissible $\mu$, minimize a natural bi-Lipschitz distortion
such as $\operatorname{Lip}(f)\operatorname{Lip}(f^{-1})$ over its
realizations. Determine which spectral features force a positive gap
from the best affine distortion, and whether the infimum is attained.
The existing construction gives finite bounds but is not optimized;
small spectral coefficients can preserve exact minimality while
making a mode difficult to detect numerically.

\subsection{Certified construction from algebraic input}

Turn the existence construction into a certificate-producing program.
Input should be a polynomial with algebraic coefficients and isolated
roots; output should include explicit envelope parameters, exact
certified domination bounds, a coordinate expression, and a proof
object for the Lipschitz constants. The symbolic spectral test is
finite and elementary after root data are supplied, but certification
of the global exponential inequalities is a separate analytic task.

\subsection{The special covering problem for smooth witnesses}

Determine the complexity of the minimum branch-cover problem from
Section~\ref{nrr:dc:sec:smooth-core}. The root sets are not arbitrary:
they arise from integer powers of negative anchors and an odd-maximum
cutoff. This arithmetic structure might allow a simpler algorithm
than general set cover. Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:witness-growth} proves that
no uniform bound on the answer exists, but says nothing about the
complexity of computing that answer.

\subsection{Algebraic degree and exceptional polynomial coordinates}
\label{nrr:ed:q-degree}

For $f=H\circ(a\id)\circ H^{-1}$, determine the exact degree of
$f$ over $\R(x)$ from $H$ and $a$. The upper bound $\deg H$ can
have special reductions when the coordinate has compositional
structure. Classify these reductions, and characterize when two
polynomial-coordinate descriptions yield algebraically related maps.
The normalized coordinate of a fixed non-involutive map is already
unique; the unresolved issue concerns algebraic degree and families,
not that uniqueness theorem.
[Added 29 September 2026, batch~54: answered in Part~IV for polynomial
coordinates and every nonzero multiplier. $\C(H(t),H(at))=\C(W(t))$, where $W$
is the maximal normalized right compositional factor of $H$ with
$W(at)=a^{\deg W}W(t)$, so the degree is $\deg H/\deg W$
(Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:common}). If $a$ is not a root of unity the degree is
$\deg H/g$, $g$ the gcd of the positive support exponents of $H$
(Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:gcd}), hence exactly $\deg H$ when $H'(0)\ne0$:
compositional structure causes no reduction
(Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:normalized}). When $H'(0)\ne0$, reductions occur
only at torsion multipliers, where a terminating exact algorithm computes the
degree (Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:torsion},
Proposition~\ref{nrr:ed:prop:algorithm}); for involutions of odd coordinate
degree $n$ exactly the divisors of $n$ occur (Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:divisors}).
The family question is answered for one fixed coordinate
(Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:observations}); two unrelated coordinates remain
open (Section~\ref{nrr:ed:q-coordinates}).]

\subsection{Higher-dimensional ordered systems}

Investigate analogues for maps preserving or reversing a proper cone
in $\R^d$ and satisfying a finite linear relation among iterates.
The one-dimensional proof relies on a finite two-cycle limit and a
one-dimensional graded jet at each order. Both features change in
higher dimensions: bounded recurrent motion need not collapse, and
resonant jets have multidimensional eigenspaces. Identify hypotheses
under which a finite-dimensional composition space still forces an
algebraic global conjugacy.

\subsection{Nonhomogeneous equations and stability}

Extend the classification where possible to
$\sum_{j=1}^d a_jf^{\circ j}(x)=F(x)$ with prescribed non-linear
$F$, and to approximate equations on invariant domains. Existing
existence and stability programs, including \cite{LZ2013,XHWX2026},
operate in this broader setting. An appropriate first target is a
forcing term lying in a finite-dimensional space preserved by right
composition, so that a replacement for the cyclic recurrence space
survives. Small residual in an equation should not be confused with
small error in exact minimal spectral support.

\subsection{Independent audit and formal verification}

The highest-priority mathematical audit is the passage from a family
of alternating orbits to a single global spectral branch, followed by
the finite-jet injectivity and global-coordinate argument. The
highest-priority literature audit is a full-text comparison with the
older characteristic-solution and differentiability papers, not just
their abstracts. The highest-priority formal task is a verified
recurrence growth lemma feeding both classification theorems. These
three activities address correctness, priority, and machine-checking
respectively; none substitutes for the others.

\section{Conclusion of Part~III}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:conclusion}

Orientation reversal imposes a structure absent from the unrestricted
increasing problem: a finite two-cycle interval, a common convergent
time direction, and a prohibition on mixing contracting and expanding
nonunit spectra. Combined with the positive bilateral recurrence
criterion, this yields a complete occurrence theorem and a closed
universal reduction with at most two sharp branches.

Sufficient differentiability changes the answer more radically.
The two-cycle interval disappears, the fixed point is hyperbolic,
and a finite jet detects the entire composition space. Its eigenfunctions
then become integer powers of one global coordinate, forcing that
coordinate's inverse to be a polynomial. This proves automatic
analyticity and algebraicity for non-involutions at an explicit finite
regularity threshold. The smooth core is therefore governed by exact
integer-power compatibility, rather than merely by extreme-circle
multiplicity conditions. The unbounded witness-count example separates
these two mechanisms even for simple negative real roots on one side
of the unit circle.

\section{Source, dependency, and claim audit of Part~III}
\label{nrr:dc:sec:audit}

\paragraph{Repository and companion provenance.}
The repository anchor was inspected at commit
\begin{center}\small
\texttt{afb2d1227d8bc5df3beffc1463e958db3544960b}.
\end{center}
The relevant report is under
\begin{quote}\small\raggedright
\nolinkurl{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/log-concavity-and-unimodality/nonreal-roots-in-iterative-equations/}
\end{quote}
(the manuscript cites it as the GitHub tree of that path at that commit,
\url{https://github.com/VladimirReshetnikov/ProveIt/tree/afb2d1227d8bc5df3beffc1463e958db3544960b/SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/log-concavity-and-unimodality/nonreal-roots-in-iterative-equations}).
Its README, status information, and indexed source excerpts identify
a circular increasing classification and explicitly limited scope; at that
commit it held Part~I only. The separate increasing companion, printed here
as Part~II, was retrieved from the user's prior-file library as the LaTeX
source \nolinkurl{article(20260929-042518).tex}, including the
positive-recurrence construction and its scope statement. The manuscript
does not represent it as an independently refereed publication or as a file
verified to be part of the pinned repository commit.

\paragraph{Extent of the external literature check.}
The relevant full arXiv text of Draga--Morawiec \cite{DM2016}, including
Problem~6.1 on preprint page~13, and Draga's follow-up \cite{D2016},
including Theorem~3 and Remarks~4--6, were inspected. The former asks
about nonreal maximal-modulus roots tied with a real root. The latter
already proves a particular negative-root disjunction. Neither the
recurrence method nor the existence of that disjunction is a priority
claim of Part~III.

For Yang--Zhang \cite{YZ2004} and Li--Zhang \cite{LZ2013}, the
publisher's abstract and bibliographic record were inspected, not the
subscription full text. The former describes multiple characteristic
roots and linear-difference constructions; the latter concerns
existence and uniqueness of decreasing solutions in a leading-coefficient
problem. A recent paper of Xia--Huo--Wang--Xia \cite{XHWX2026}, published
in April 2026, was also checked at abstract level. It treats sufficient
conditions for existence, uniqueness, and stability in a nonhomogeneous
half-line setting, not a stated complete homogeneous spectral
classification in its abstract.

The broader search surfaced older differentiability and linear-dependence
papers whose full contents were not obtained. Therefore Part~III
claims a self-contained proved extension of the inspected ProveIt
program, not an exhaustive determination of first publication.
The source list is a record of relevant checked context, not a complete
bibliography of iterative functional equations.

\paragraph{Logical dependencies and limitations.}
The main continuous theorem depends on elementary recurrence
decomposition, finite trigonometric averages, one-dimensional order
dynamics, exponential domination, and the inverse function theorem.
The finite-smoothness theorem additionally uses Taylor remainder bounds
and elementary finite-dimensional diagonalization. The universal-core
formulas are consequences of these occurrence theorems. The exact
computations are supplements and are not logical premises of any
all-real theorem.

The domain is the whole real line and $P(0)\ne0$. Part~III does not
classify arbitrary maps on arbitrary intervals, equations with a zero
constant coefficient, nonhomogeneous equations, higher-dimensional
systems, or every individual continuous solution. It does classify
every individual non-involutive solution in the stated sufficiently
smooth class. The suggested future questions are not claimed solved.
All new proofs remain open to independent mathematical review.
The manuscript's own status note is shipped verbatim as
\nolinkurl{03-decreasing-core-STATUS.md}.

\section{The question and the scope of the answer}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:scope}

\subsection{One manuscript, one addition}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:source}

Part~IV is batch-54 manuscript~01, an AI-assisted research draft prepared
for Vladimir Reshetnikov, dated 29 September 2026 and titled \emph{Two
Observations Determine the Coordinate: Exact algebraic degree, torsion
exceptions, and nonradical solutions of order-two iterative equations}
(25 US Letter pages as delivered). It arrived as the archive
\texttt{ProveIt\_Exact\_Algebraic\_Degree.zip} (inner directory
\texttt{ProveIt\_Exact\_Algebraic\_Degree/}) in ProveIt commit
\texttt{392fff082} and was placed in \texttt{4d922d5b2}. It is pinned to
ProveIt commit \texttt{11e1e9001} (full hash in
Section~\ref{nrr:ed:app:provenance}). At that commit this report already held
Parts~I--III, as the Git blob \texttt{b52eba81} of \texttt{article.tex}, and
that blob is unchanged up to the placement commit; so the manuscript's
``repository'', ``repository question'' and ``source report'' mean the text
of Parts~I--III printed above. It also read Part~III's source manuscript from
Vladimir's file library (as \nolinkurl{article(20260929-151305).tex}). The
``Section~11.7'' it cites from that file is printed here as Part~III's
Section~35.7; the question's text was checked in the write phase against the
batch-42 arrival of that manuscript (commit \texttt{8315d24e3}) and agrees.
The manuscript's verifier, Makefile, requirements file, recorded output and
provenance note are shipped beside Parts~I--III with the prefix
\nolinkurl{04-exact-degree-}; its manuscript, PDF, delivery README and
checksum ledger (\texttt{CHECKSUMS.sha256}, 9/9 verified at placement) are
not shipped and survive in the arrival commit.

\begin{quote}\small
\textbf{The manuscript's abstract.} The ProveIt report on decreasing
polynomial iterative equations leaves open the exact algebraic degree of a
solution written as $f=H\circ(a\id)\circ H^{-1}$. We determine it by
computing the intermediate field $\C(H(t),H(at))$. If $a$ has infinite
multiplicative order, this field is $\C(t^g)$, where $g$ is the greatest
common divisor of the positive exponents occurring in $H$. Thus every
normalized polynomial coordinate, with $H'(0)\ne0$, gives algebraic degree
exactly $\deg H$. Nontrivial compositional structure of $H$ does not cause
exceptions in this hyperbolic setting.

For a root-of-unity multiplier, we give an exact maximal-right-factor
criterion, a finite coefficient algorithm, and a complete list of degree
drops for real polynomial-coordinate involutions of a prescribed coordinate
degree. Two observations also recover a primitive coordinate rationally, so
all nonzero hyperbolic iterates have the same algebraic degree and the same
normal closure.

The distinction between iterative order and algebraic complexity is
substantial. An explicit order-two family has algebraic degree $n$ and
symmetric monodromy $S_n$ for every odd $n\ge3$. More strongly, the single
equation $f\circ f=\id$ admits, for every odd $n\ge5$, globally analytic
algebraic solutions of degree $n$ that are not expressible by radicals and
have bi-Lipschitz distortion arbitrarily close to one. Their coordinate
polynomials can have rational coefficients. We prove these statements, give
exact distortion formulas for the perturbation family, and supply
independently cross-checked finite computations.
\end{quote}

The manuscript contributes, in the order printed here: the question, the two
notions of degree and the principal conclusions (this section); the dilation
common-field theorem (Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:field}); the support-gcd
formula, the exact hyperbolic degree and the degree of all iterates
(Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:infinite}); the torsion criterion and the exact
degrees of polynomial-coordinate involutions
(Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:torsion}); the graph curve, its resultant, a finite
right-factor algorithm and rational recovery
(Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:algorithms}); normal closures and the Morse
monodromy criterion (Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:monodromy}); the binomial
family of nonradical order-two solutions
(Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:hyperbolic-family}); nonradical involutions near
reflection (Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:reflection}); its finite checks,
limitations and arithmetic interpretation
(Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:verification}); ten research questions
(Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:questions}); its conclusion
(Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:conclusion}); its provenance, dependency and claim
audit (Section~\ref{nrr:ed:app:provenance}); and its reproduction guide
(Section~\ref{nrr:ed:app:reproduce}). Every result, proof, example, remark,
limitation and question of the manuscript is printed.

\paragraph{Where the merge had to choose.}
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textnormal{(\alph*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item Nothing in the manuscript repeats a result of Parts~I--III. Part~III
proves that sufficiently smooth non-involutive decreasing solutions have the
polynomial-coordinate form \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:poly-conjugacy} and records the
upper bound $\deg H$; Part~IV starts from an explicit polynomial coordinate
and uses neither Part~III's classification nor any other theorem of
Parts~I--III as a premise. So every proof is printed in full, and nothing
is credited across parts except that context.
\item The manuscript cites Draga--Morawiec by arXiv:1503.00570 without a
version; Part~I's entry for the same paper \cite{DM2016} is used. Its entry
for ``the ProveIt report'' (a GitHub tree URL at the pin) is replaced by
references to Parts~I--III, and the URL is kept in
Section~\ref{nrr:ed:app:provenance}. Its two further works,
Zieve--M\"uller \cite{ZM2008} and Kreso--Tichy \cite{KT2016}, were added to
the bibliography after their arXiv records (titles, authors, dates) were
checked in the write phase.
\item Two sentences are corrected by bracketed merge notes rather than
silently: the manuscript places its target question in ``Part~III,
Section~34'' (it is Section~35.7), and its Section~\ref{nrr:ed:sec:verification}
says the exact-degree question is ``fully answered'', which for torsion
multipliers means an exact algorithm and the involution classification, not a
closed formula.
\item Its file names are replaced by the shipped names, and its reproduction
guide (Section~\ref{nrr:ed:app:reproduce}) keeps the delivered commands and
adds the scratch-copy command that works with them here. Its two appendices
are printed as the last sections of Part~IV, so that Part~I's appendices keep
their letters; its unnumbered verification table stays unnumbered.
\item No symbol was renamed and no normalization changed. The manuscript's
symbols collide with those of Parts~I--III in several places;
Table~\ref{nrr:ed:tab:notation} lists them.
\item Two dated notes were added to Part~III (after
Corollary~\ref{nrr:dc:cor:rational} and after Research question~35.7), and a
paragraph and a subtitle line to the title page.
\end{enumerate}

\paragraph{Status (manuscript 01 of batch 54).}
AI-assisted, unrefereed, and not formalized in Lean or Rocq; no declaration in
ProveIt formalizes any statement of Parts~I--IV. The main results are proved
here from explicitly stated classical algebraic and covering-space facts
(polynomial L\"uroth theory, uniqueness of a normalized right factor of fixed
degree, and the Morse--monodromy criterion, all credited as classical). The
claimed advance is relative to the inspected repository question; an
exhaustive historical-priority claim is not made. The radical obstruction
concerns algebraic functions over $\C(x)$; it does not assert nonradical
values at particular numerical inputs. Part~IV does not classify all
continuous solutions of an iterative equation, all smooth involutions, all
real algebraic diffeomorphisms, or field relations between two unrelated
polynomial coordinates. Its finite computations check implementations and
listed examples; they are not proofs of the universal statements.

\subsection{Notation across the four parts}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:notation}

Symbols of Parts~I--III keep their meanings in their parts.
Table~\ref{nrr:ed:tab:notation} fixes the meanings used in Part~IV. Letters
not listed ($\mu_f$, $\id$, $f^{\circ j}$, $\operatorname{Lip}$, $P[f]$)
agree with Parts~I--III. In particular, iterative order ($\deg\mu_f$) and
algebraic degree ($\algdeg f$) are different numbers: the maps of
Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:binomial} have $\deg\mu_f=2$ and $\algdeg f=n$.
