\subsection{A specific repository question}

For a polynomial $P(z)=\sum_{j=0}^m p_jz^j$, a \emph{polynomial iterative equation}
is an identity
\begin{equation}
 P[f](x):=\sum_{j=0}^m p_j f^{\circ j}(x)=0,
 \qquad f^{\circ0}=\id.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:iterative}
\end{equation}
The powers in this expression are iterates, not pointwise powers. The
characteristic-root and order-reduction approach to such equations is an established
subject; Draga and Morawiec \cite{DM2016} provide relevant background.

Part~III of this report develops a classification of sufficiently smooth decreasing
solutions in terms of polynomial coordinates. Its conclusion, for the
non-involutive maps covered by that theorem, has the form
\begin{equation}
 f(c+H(t))=c+H(at),\qquad
 a<0,\quad a\ne-1,\quad H(0)=0,\quad H'(0)=1,\quad H'>0\text{ on }\R.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:repo-coordinate}
\end{equation}
[Merge note: this is \eqref{nrr:dc:eq:poly-conjugacy} of Part~III's
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:smooth}, proved as
Theorem~\ref{nrr:dc:thm:finite-threshold}.]
The original manuscript's Section 11.7 asks for the exact degree of $f$ over
$\R(x)$, possible reductions caused by composition in $H$, and relations between
coordinate families. In the merged repository this is among the research questions
in Part III, Section 34. [Merge note: this is Research question~35.7 of
Part~III (Section~\ref{nrr:ed:q-degree}), not Section~34, which is
Part~III's verification section.] The original question and the upper-bound
discussion (after Corollary~\ref{nrr:dc:cor:rational}) were both inspected;
provenance is recorded in Section~\ref{nrr:ed:app:provenance}.

We settle the exact-degree part and the common-coordinate family part. In the
hyperbolic normalized setting of \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:repo-coordinate}, the proposed possibility
of a compositional degree drop does not occur. Such drops are genuine at torsion
multipliers, notably at $a=-1$, and also without the nonzero-linear-term hypothesis.
We classify these mechanisms rather than silently excluding them.

The proofs below do \emph{not} assume the correctness of the repository's global
smoothness classification. They begin with an explicit polynomial coordinate.
Therefore they apply to every map satisfying \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:repo-coordinate}, independently
of the other claims in that report. We neither reprove nor extend its differentiability
threshold in this article.

\subsection{Two different degrees}

Fix a nonconstant $H\in\C[t]$ of degree $n$ and $a\in\C^\times$. Set
\begin{equation}
 \field=\C(H(t),H(at))\subseteq\C(t),\qquad
 d(H,a)=[\C(t):\field],\qquad
 r(H,a)=[\field:\C(H(t))].
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:fields}
\end{equation}
The tower law gives $d(H,a)r(H,a)=n$.

When $H\in\R[t]$, $H(0)=0$, and $H'>0$ on $\R$, it is a global real-analytic
diffeomorphism of $\R$. Define
\begin{equation}
 f_a(x)=H\bigl(aH^{-1}(x)\bigr),\qquad a\in\R^\times.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:fa}
\end{equation}
Its algebraic degree is $\algdeg(f_a)=r(H,a)$, interpreted either through its
real analytic germ or through the complex function field of its algebraic graph.
The real and complex degrees agree in this setting, as proved below.

By contrast, the \emph{minimal iterative polynomial} $\mu_f$ is the monic
least-degree nonzero polynomial satisfying \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:iterative}. Our examples have
$\deg\mu_f=2$ and $\algdeg(f)=n$ with $n$ arbitrarily large. Nothing identifies
these two notions of degree.

\subsection{The principal conclusions}

The main field theorem will show that $\field=\C(W(t))$, where $W$ is the unique
monic polynomial with $W(0)=0$ that has maximal degree among right compositional
factors of $H$ satisfying
\begin{equation}
 W(at)=a^{\deg W}W(t).
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:eigen-intro}
\end{equation}
This has three particularly useful consequences.

First, if $a$ is not a root of unity and
$g=\gcd\{k\ge1:[t^k]H\ne0\}$, then
\begin{equation}
 \field=\C(t^g),\qquad \algdeg(f_a)=\frac{n}{g}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:main-preview}
\end{equation}
In particular, $H'(0)\ne0$ forces $g=1$ and exact degree $n$.

Second, for polynomial-coordinate involutions, $d(H,-1)$ is the largest degree of
an odd right compositional factor of $H$. For any fixed odd coordinate degree $n$,
the possible algebraic degrees are exactly the positive divisors of $n$.

Third, degree-one topological dynamics need not have degree-one algebra. There are
nonradical algebraic involutions of every odd degree $n\ge5$, with symmetric
monodromy $S_n$, whose derivatives are uniformly as close to $-1$ as prescribed.
These solve the \emph{same} iterative equation $f^{\circ2}=\id$.

\subsection{Classical ingredients and the novelty boundary}

Polynomial L\"uroth theory and uniqueness of a normalized right factor of a fixed
degree are classical. Zieve--M\"uller \cite[Lemma 2.2, Corollary 2.9 and
Remark 2.16]{ZM2008} give the pertinent intermediate-field and decomposition
facts. We include an elementary coefficient proof of the latter uniqueness, because
it makes the dilation argument transparent. The implication from a Morse polynomial
to symmetric monodromy is also classical; see Kreso--Tichy \cite{KT2016}.
We give its covering-space proof and check the required hypotheses explicitly.

The article's contribution to the repository is the exact common-field calculation,
its degree and reconstruction consequences, the torsion analysis, and the resulting
complexity examples. It would be misleading to label the classical ingredients, or
an unrestricted historical priority for their consequences, as established novelties.

\section{The dilation common-field theorem}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:field}

\subsection{Polynomial intermediate fields}

\begin{lemma}[Polynomial form of L\"uroth's theorem]
\label{nrr:ed:lem:luroth}
Let $F\in\C[t]$ be nonconstant, and let
$\C(F(t))\subseteq E\subseteq\C(t)$. Then there is a polynomial $W$ with
$E=\C(W(t))$. Every polynomial in $t$ belonging to $E$ is a polynomial in $W$.
Moreover,
\[
 [\C(t):\C(W(t))]=\deg W.
\]
The generator $W$ is unique after requiring it to be monic and to vanish at zero.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Classical L\"uroth's theorem gives $E=\C(w(t))$ for a rational function $w$.
Write $F=A\circ w$ with $A$ rational. The polynomial $F$ has exactly one pole
on $\mathbf P^1$, namely infinity. The preimage under $w$ of every pole of $A$
therefore consists only of infinity. Surjectivity of a nonconstant rational map
shows that $A$ has a single pole, say $p$, and $w^{-1}(p)=\{\infty\}$.
Postcompose $w$ by a M\"obius transformation sending $p$ to infinity. The resulting
$W$ has its only pole at infinity, hence is polynomial. The corresponding outer
function $A$ is polynomial as well.

If a polynomial $G(t)$ equals a rational function $B(W(t))$, then a finite pole of
$B$ would have a finite preimage under the nonconstant polynomial $W$, producing
a finite pole of $G$. Thus $B$ is polynomial. The field-extension degree equals
the degree of the rational map $W:\mathbf P^1\to\mathbf P^1$, hence equals
$\deg W$. This is also obtained from the generic equation $W(T)-W(t)=0$.

Two generators of the same rational function field differ by a M\"obius
transformation. If both are polynomial in $t$, that transformation must be affine.
Their monic leading coefficients and zero constant terms remove this ambiguity.
\end{proof}

\subsection{A rigid normalization}

\begin{lemma}[Uniqueness of a right factor of prescribed degree]
\label{nrr:ed:lem:unique}
Suppose
\[
 F=A\circ U=B\circ V
\]
with nonconstant polynomials over a characteristic-zero field, and suppose $U,V$
are monic of the same degree $d$ with $U(0)=V(0)=0$. Then $U=V$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Put $\deg F=md$ and let $h$ be the leading coefficient of $F$. Since $U$ and $V$
are monic, the outer polynomials have leading coefficient $h$. Terms of
$A(U)-hU^m$ and $B(V)-hV^m$ have degree at most $md-d$. Thus the coefficients
of $F/h$ at degrees
\[
 md-1,\ md-2,\ \ldots,\ md-d+1
\]
are exactly those of $U^m$ and of $V^m$.

Write $U=t^d+u_{d-1}t^{d-1}+\cdots+u_1t$ and similarly for $V$. The coefficient
of $t^{md-j}$ in $U^m$, for $1\le j<d$, is
\[
 m u_{d-j}+\text{a polynomial in }u_{d-1},\ldots,u_{d-j+1}.
\]
Successively comparing these coefficients determines every $u_{d-j}$ uniquely.
Division by $m$ is valid in characteristic zero. The constant terms have already
been fixed at zero, so $U=V$. For $d=1$, both polynomials are simply $t$.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Exact common field under dilation]
\label{nrr:ed:thm:common}
For nonconstant $H\in\C[t]$ and $a\in\C^\times$, there is a unique monic
polynomial $W$, with $W(0)=0$, generating $\field$. Write $d=\deg W$. Then
\begin{equation}
 H=A\circ W,\qquad W(at)=a^dW(t),\qquad
 H(at)=A(a^dW(t))
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:factor-covariance}
\end{equation}
for a polynomial $A$ of degree $n/d$.

Equivalently, $W$ has the largest degree among all monic, zero-constant right
compositional factors $V$ of $H$ satisfying $V(at)=a^{\deg V}V(t)$. In particular,
\begin{equation}
 d(H,a)=\deg W,\qquad r(H,a)=\frac{\deg H}{\deg W}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:degree-theorem}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
By Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:luroth}, choose the normalized polynomial generator $W$ of
$\field$. Since both observations are polynomials in $t$, there are polynomials
$A,B$ with
\[
 H(t)=A(W(t)),\qquad H(at)=B(W(t)).
\]
Replacing $t$ by $t/a$ in the second equality gives
\[
 H(t)=B(W(t/a)).
\]
The polynomial $V(t)=a^dW(t/a)$ is monic of degree $d$ and vanishes at zero.
Absorbing its scalar normalization into the outer polynomial gives two normalized
right factors of $H$, namely $W$ and $V$. Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:unique} implies
$W(t)=a^dW(t/a)$, or $W(at)=a^dW(t)$. Substituting this in $H(at)=A(W(at))$
proves \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:factor-covariance}.

Conversely, suppose $H=C\circ V$ and $V(at)=a^eV(t)$, where $e=\deg V$.
Then both $H(t)$ and $H(at)$ belong to $\C(V(t))$, so
\[
 \field\subseteq\C(V(t))\subseteq\C(t).
\]
The tower law implies $e\le d$. The generator $W$ itself satisfies the criterion,
so it is maximal. Any other normalized maximal candidate has degree $d$ and equals
$W$ by Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:unique}. Finally, the degree formulas follow from
Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:luroth} and the tower law.
\end{proof}

The crucial point is not merely that two polynomials have a common right factor.
The two observations are related by a dilation of the input. Equal-degree
right-factor uniqueness forces their common factor to transform as a single
weight under that dilation. This reduces a functional decomposition problem to a
coefficient restriction.

\section{Infinite-order multipliers and exact hyperbolic degree}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:infinite}

\subsection{A support-gcd formula}

\begin{definition}
For a nonconstant polynomial $H$, define its positive support gcd by
\[
 g(H)=\gcd\{k\in\N:k\ge1,\ [t^k]H(t)\ne0\}.
\]
The constant coefficient is deliberately omitted.
\end{definition}

\begin{theorem}[Infinite-order field formula]
\label{nrr:ed:thm:gcd}
Suppose $a\in\C^\times$ is not a root of unity. Then
\begin{equation}
 \C(H(t),H(at))=\C(t^{g(H)}),\qquad
 d(H,a)=g(H),\qquad r(H,a)=\frac{n}{g(H)}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:gcd-formula}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Let $W$ be the generator from Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:common}, of degree $d$.
If the coefficient of $t^k$ in $W$ is nonzero, its covariance gives $a^k=a^d$.
Infinite multiplicative order forces $k=d$. Since $W$ is monic, $W=t^d$.
Consequently $H$ is a polynomial in $t^d$, so $d$ divides $g=g(H)$.

Conversely, $H(t)$ and $H(at)$ are both polynomials in $t^g$. Hence
$\field\subseteq\C(t^g)$, and the tower law gives $d\ge g$. Together with
$d\mid g$, this yields $d=g$. The field and remaining degree formulas follow.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[The normalized hyperbolic answer]
\label{nrr:ed:cor:normalized}
Let $H\in\R[t]$ satisfy $H(0)=0$ and $H'>0$ on $\R$, and let
$a\in\R\setminus\{0,1,-1\}$. Then
\begin{equation}
 \C(H(t),H(at))=\C(t),\qquad
 [\R(x,f_a(x)):\R(x)]=[\C(x,f_a(x)):\C(x)]=\deg H.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:normalized-answer}
\end{equation}
The same degree conclusion holds for $x\mapsto c+f_a(x-c)$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
The coefficient $H'(0)$ is nonzero, so $g(H)=1$. A nonzero real number other than
$1$ and $-1$ has infinite multiplicative order. Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:gcd} gives the
complex field equality and degree.

For the real assertion, let $E_\R=\R(H(t),H(at))$. The equality over $\C$
gives a rational expression $t=R(H(t),H(at))$ with complex coefficients.
Conjugating the coefficients gives another such expression. Their average is
fixed by complex conjugation and belongs to the real rational function field
$E_\R$ (equivalently, take the fixed field in $E_\R(i)$). Thus
$E_\R=\R(t)$, and the real tower degree is $\deg H$. Translating the two
observations by constants does not change a field-extension degree.
\end{proof}

This proves that the degree upper bound in the repository question is always
sharp under its normalized hyperbolic hypotheses. It is not necessary to assume
that $H$ is indecomposable.

\begin{example}[Composition is not a hyperbolic exception]
Let $U(t)=t+t^3$ and $H=U\circ U$, so $\deg H=9$, $H'(0)=1$, and $H'>0$.
For $a=-2$, the function $H\circ(-2\id)\circ H^{-1}$ has algebraic degree
\emph{nine}, not three. In contrast, for $a=-1$ the same odd $H$ produces
$-\id$, of degree one. The distinction is the multiplier, not merely the
presence of a decomposition.
\end{example}

\begin{example}[Why the linear term matters]
For $H=t^6+t^4$, one has $g(H)=2$, so an infinite-order multiplier gives degree
$6/2=3$. For $H=t^6+t^3+7$, the degree is $6/3=2$. These statements concern
algebraic branches of the parametrized graph; these $H$ are not the normalized
real diffeomorphisms in Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:normalized}.
\end{example}

\subsection{Every nonzero iterate has the same degree}

\begin{corollary}[Two observations and a whole orbit]
\label{nrr:ed:cor:observations}
Suppose $g(H)=1$ and $a$ has infinite multiplicative order. For distinct integers
$r,s$,
\begin{equation}
 \C(H(a^rt),H(a^st))=\C(t).
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:anytwo}
\end{equation}
For a real polynomial diffeomorphism $H$, every nonzero iterate of $f_a$ has
algebraic degree $\deg H$. Moreover, there is a rational function $R(X,Y)$ such
that, as a function-field identity,
\begin{equation}
 H^{-1}(x)=R(x,f_a(x)),\qquad
 f_b(x)=H\bigl(bR(x,f_a(x))\bigr)\quad(b\in\C^\times).
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:reconstruct}
\end{equation}
The complex expression is interpreted on the corresponding algebraic branch.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Replace $t$ by $a^rt$ and apply Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:gcd} to the ratio $a^{s-r}$,
which still has infinite order. For iterates, $f_a^{\circ j}=f_{a^j}$ for every
integer $j$, and $j\ne0$ preserves infinite order. Finally,
$\C(H(t),H(at))=\C(t)$ means precisely that $t$ is a rational function of those
two observations. Substituting it into $H(bt)$ proves \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:reconstruct}.
\end{proof}

A rational reconstruction need not have a denominator nonzero at every real
point. The assertion is an identity in the function field, valid off its poles;
where the original real functions are analytic, apparent singularities can be
handled through a different rational chart or by continuation. It is not a claim
of a universally nonsingular numerical reconstruction formula.

The consequence for families is exact but limited: all dilations using one fixed
primitive coordinate $H$ lie in the same degree-$n$ extension of $\C(x)$. It does
not classify arbitrary pairs of unrelated coordinate polynomials $H$ and $G$.

\section{Torsion multipliers and real involutions}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:torsion}

\subsection{The complete coefficient restriction}

\begin{theorem}[Torsion criterion]
\label{nrr:ed:thm:torsion}
Let $a$ have finite multiplicative order $q$. The generator $W$ of
Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:common}, of degree $d$, is the maximal-degree normalized right
factor of $H$ whose nonzero exponents are all congruent to $d$ modulo $q$.
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
 W(t)=t^rR(t^q),\qquad 0\le r<q,\qquad r\equiv d\pmod q,
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:torsion-form}
\end{equation}
with $W(0)=0$. When $r=0$, the last condition requires $R(0)=0$.
If $H'(0)\ne0$, then necessarily
\begin{equation}
 d\equiv1\pmod q,\qquad W(t)=tR(t^q),\qquad R(0)\ne0.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:torsion-linear}
\end{equation}
The algebraic observation degree remains exactly $n/d$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The covariance $W(at)=a^dW(t)$ is equivalent, coefficient by coefficient, to
$a^k=a^d$ for every exponent in the support of $W$. For an element of order $q$,
this is exactly $k\equiv d\pmod q$. This proves \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:torsion-form} and the
maximal-factor characterization. If $H=A\circ W$ and $H'(0)\ne0$, the chain rule
gives $A'(0)W'(0)\ne0$, since $W(0)=0$. Thus exponent one occurs in $W$, giving
\eqref{nrr:ed:eq:torsion-linear}. The degree formula is Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:common}.
\end{proof}

For $a=1$, every right factor satisfies the covariance, and the maximal one is
the normalized copy of $H$ itself. The observation degree is one, as it must be.
For $a=i$, the polynomial $H=t+t^5$ also generates a degree-one observation:
$H(it)=iH(t)$. If instead $H=(s+s^2)\circ(t+t^5)$, the maximal generator is
$t+t^5$ and the observation degree is two. These examples show why the
infinite-order qualification cannot be dropped.

\subsection{Odd right factors are exactly the reflection exceptions}

\begin{corollary}[Exact degree of a polynomial-coordinate involution]
\label{nrr:ed:cor:odd-factor}
Let $H\in\R[t]$ satisfy $H(0)=0$ and $H'>0$ on $\R$, and define
\[
 \iota_H=H\circ(-\id)\circ H^{-1}.
\]
Let $d$ be the largest degree of a monic odd polynomial $W$ with $H=A\circ W$.
Then
\begin{equation}
 \algdeg(\iota_H)=\frac{\deg H}{d}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:involution-degree}
\end{equation}
In particular, $\iota_H=-\id$ if and only if $H$ is odd.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:torsion} with $q=2$. Since $H'(0)\ne0$, the generator has
odd degree and contains only odd powers. Conversely every odd right factor has
$W(-t)=-W(t)$ and qualifies. Real coefficients follow either from the real form
of Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:luroth} or from uniqueness under coefficient conjugation.
The identity $\iota_H=-\id$ is equivalent to $H(-t)=-H(t)$.
\end{proof}

If $H=A\circ W$ with $W$ odd and $H'>0$, then $W'$ never vanishes. With the
monic normalization, $W$ is an increasing odd-degree real polynomial and is a
real diffeomorphism. The identity $W\circ(-\id)=(-\id)\circ W$ cancels that
coordinate layer:
\[
 \iota_H=A\circ(-\id)\circ A^{-1}.
\]
This explains geometrically why an odd inner factor lowers the algebraic degree.
It also shows why analogous cancellation is absent for a nonlinear normalized
factor under an infinite-order dilation.

\begin{lemma}[An adjacent coefficient forbids an odd inner factor]
\label{nrr:ed:lem:adjacent}
Let $U$ have odd degree $m$ and a nonzero coefficient of $t^{m-1}$. Then $U$
has no odd right compositional factor of degree greater than one.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose $U=A\circ V$ with $V$ odd of degree $e>1$. Then $e\ge3$, $e\mid m$,
and the coefficient of $t^{e-1}$ in $V$ is zero. The leading power
$V^{m/e}$ therefore has zero coefficient at $t^{m-1}$. Every lower outer power
has degree at most $m-e<m-1$. Hence $[t^{m-1}]U=0$, a contradiction.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[All degree drops at a fixed real coordinate degree]
\label{nrr:ed:thm:divisors}
Fix an odd positive integer $n$. Among all $H\in\R[t]$ of degree $n$ with
$H(0)=0$, $H'(0)=1$, and $H'>0$ on $\R$, the possible algebraic degrees of
$\iota_H$ are exactly the positive divisors of $n$. Every such degree is
realized with rational coefficients in $H$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Necessity follows from Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:odd-factor}: the algebraic degree is
$n/d$, where $d\mid n$.

For sufficiency, write $n=md$. All three integers are odd. Define
\[
 W_d(t)=
 \begin{cases}t,&d=1,\\ t+t^d,&d>1,\end{cases}
 \qquad
 U_m(s)=
 \begin{cases}s,&m=1,\\
 s+s^m+\dfrac{s^{m-1}}{2(m-1)},&m>1.
 \end{cases}
\]
Let $H=U_m\circ W_d$. Both component polynomials have derivative one at zero.
The derivative of $W_d$ is positive. For $m>1$, put $r=|s|$. The potentially
negative contribution to $U_m'$ has magnitude at most $r^{m-2}/2$. If $r\le1$,
then $U_m'\ge1-1/2>0$. If $r\ge1$, then
$mr^{m-1}-r^{m-2}/2>0$. Thus $H'>0$ everywhere.

When $m>1$, Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:adjacent} and Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:odd-factor} imply
$\C(U_m(s),U_m(-s))=\C(s)$. The same equality is immediate when $m=1$.
Substituting $s=W_d(t)$, and using oddness of $W_d$, gives
\[
 \C(H(t),H(-t))=\C(W_d(t)).
\]
The observation degree is therefore $n/d=m$, as required. All coefficients are
rational by construction.
\end{proof}

\section{Algebraic curves, resultants, and exact computation}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:algorithms}

\subsection{The graph and its normalization}

\begin{theorem}[The graph equation and its resultant multiplicity]
\label{nrr:ed:thm:curve}
Let $W,A,d$ be as in Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:common}, and put $r=n/d$. There is a unique
irreducible polynomial $F_{H,a}(X,Y)\in\C[X,Y]$, monic in $Y$, such that
\[
 F_{H,a}(H(t),H(at))=0.
\]
It has degree $r$ in each variable and total degree $r$. The normalization of
its projective curve is $\mathbf P^1$, with birational parametrization
\begin{equation}
 X=A(s),\qquad Y=A(a^ds),\qquad s=W(t).
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:normalization}
\end{equation}
If $h_n$ is the leading coefficient of $H$, then, with the usual resultant
convention,
\begin{equation}
 \Res_t(H(t)-X,H(at)-Y)=(-1)^n h_n^n F_{H,a}(X,Y)^d.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:resultant-power}
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The kernel of the substitution map
$\C[X,Y]\to\C[t]$, $(X,Y)\mapsto(H(t),H(at))$, is a height-one prime ideal,
so it is generated by one irreducible polynomial. Its degree in $Y$ is
$[\field:\C(H(t))]=r$. The element $t$ is integral over $\C[H(t)]$ after
dividing the leading coefficient out of $H(T)-H(t)$. Hence $H(at)$ is integral
over this ring. The monic minimal polynomial of $H(at)$ over $\C(H(t))$ has
coefficients in the integrally closed ring $\C[H(t)]$, allowing the stated
monic normalization of $F_{H,a}$. Interchanging the observations gives degree
$r$ in $X$ as well.

Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:common} says that the function field of the curve is exactly
$\C(s)$, with the two functions in \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:normalization}. They have degree
$r$ as polynomials in $s$. Thus the parametrization is birational. It extends
to a morphism from $\mathbf P^1$ to the projective plane: its two leading
coefficients cannot vanish simultaneously at infinity. A generic line pulls
back to a polynomial equation of degree $r$ with $r$ distinct finite solutions.
Birationality therefore gives total curve degree $r$. The smooth projective
normalization of its function field $\C(s)$ is $\mathbf P^1$.

For the resultant, take a generic $X$. The polynomial $H(T)-X$ has $n$ distinct
roots $t_1,\ldots,t_n$. The $r$ conjugate values of $H(at)$ over $\C(X)$ each
occur exactly $d$ times among $H(at_1),\ldots,H(at_n)$, by the separable field
tower of degrees $d$ and $r$. Therefore
\[
 \prod_{j=1}^n\bigl(Y-H(at_j)\bigr)=F_{H,a}(X,Y)^d.
\]
The root-product formula for the resultant multiplies
$\prod_j(H(at_j)-Y)$ by $h_n^n$, proving \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:resultant-power} for generic
$X$ and therefore as a polynomial identity.
\end{proof}

A rational normalization is not a rational inverse to the projection
$(X,Y)\mapsto X$. That projection has degree $r$, which can be arbitrarily
large and can have nonsolvable monodromy. In particular, genus zero does not
supply a radical formula for $Y$ as a function of $X$.

\subsection{A finite right-factor algorithm}

The proof of Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:unique} is constructive. Let
$H=h_nt^n+\cdots$, fix a divisor $d\mid n$, and put $m=n/d$. Begin with $W=t^d$.
For $j=1,\ldots,d-1$, set
\begin{equation}
 [t^{d-j}]W=
 \frac{[t^{n-j}](H/h_n)-[t^{n-j}]W_{\mathrm{previous}}^m}{m}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:candidate}
\end{equation}
Only already determined coefficients occur on the right. This yields the unique
possible monic zero-constant right factor of degree $d$. It need not actually
be a right factor.

To test that remaining property, subtract successive leading multiples of powers
of $W$ from $H$. At each stage a nonzero remainder must have degree divisible
by $d$; otherwise $H$ cannot belong to the polynomial ring $\C[W]$. Since every
subtraction lowers the degree, the process terminates. A zero remainder supplies
an outer polynomial $A$ with $H=A(W)$.

\begin{proposition}[Exact finite computation of the degree]
\label{nrr:ed:prop:algorithm}
For input in an exactly computable characteristic-zero coefficient field, the
following algorithm returns $d(H,a)$ and its normalized generator $W$:
examine the divisors $d\mid n$ in decreasing order; construct the candidate by
\eqref{nrr:ed:eq:candidate}; test $H\in k[W]$ and $W(at)=a^dW(t)$; return the first
candidate passing both tests. The algorithm terminates and is correct.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The candidate of any actual right factor is forced by Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:unique}.
Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:common} proves that the largest successful degree is exactly
$d(H,a)$. The candidate $W=t$ for $d=1$ always succeeds, proving termination.
All tests involve finitely many coefficient operations and exact equalities.
\end{proof}

Rational and algebraic-number inputs are appropriate for an implementation.
Arbitrary complex coefficients without an exact representation or equality oracle
are not. The algorithm does not require an independent root-of-unity test: the
covariance is checked directly. For a rational multiplier other than $0,1,-1$,
the support-gcd formula is a much cheaper shortcut. No bit-complexity bound or
numerical stability guarantee is asserted for the general decomposition algorithm.

There is also a Laurent-series description of the same candidate. Take the formal
$m$th root of $H/h_n$ at infinity with leading term $t^d$, retain its polynomial
part, and subtract its constant term. The resulting polynomial is exactly the
candidate in \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:candidate}; coefficient comparison proves this description
without choosing analytic branches.

\subsection{Rational recovery by elimination}

When $d=1$, the common root of
\[
 H(T)-X=0,\qquad H(aT)-Y=0
\]
is generically unique \emph{on the graph curve}. Over its function field, the gcd
of these polynomials in $T$ is linear. A nonzero linear subresultant therefore
recovers $T$ as a rational function of $X,Y$. One must work modulo the graph
relation or evaluate at its generic point: over the field $\C(X,Y)$ with
independent $X,Y$, the two polynomials generally have gcd one.

\begin{example}[An explicit non-binomial reconstruction]
\label{nrr:ed:ex:recovery}
Let $H=t+t^2+t^3$ and $a=2$. Then $H'=1+2t+3t^2>0$ and
\begin{equation}
 t=\frac{16X-Y}{16X-2Y+14},\qquad X=H(t),\quad Y=H(2t).
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:cubic-recovery}
\end{equation}
Indeed, after substitution, the denominator is $14+12t+8t^2$, and the numerator
is $t$ times that polynomial. The denominator is positive for real $t$, so this
particular chart has no real singularities. The penultimate nonzero subresultant is
\[
 2\bigl((16X-2Y+14)T-(16X-Y)\bigr).
\]
The resultant itself is the irreducible cubic
\begin{align*}
 &512X^3-192X^2Y+24XY^2-Y^3\\
 &\hspace{1em}{}+256X^2-120XY+10Y^2+56X-28Y.
\end{align*}
It differs by a minus sign from the monic-in-$Y$ normalization of the graph equation.
\end{example}

\section{Normal closures and monodromy}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:monodromy}

\subsection{Transfer through the recovered coordinate}

\begin{theorem}[Normal-closure transfer]
\label{nrr:ed:thm:galois}
Let $H=A\circ W$ be the normalized factorization in Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:common}.
The normal closure of the graph-function extension
\[
 \C(X,Y)/\C(X),\qquad F_{H,a}(X,Y)=0,
\]
is the normal closure of $\C(s)/\C(A(s))$. Its permutation Galois group is
therefore that of $A(S)-X$ on its $r=n/d$ roots.

In particular, when $d=1$, it is the Galois group of $H(T)-X$. For an
infinite-order multiplier with $g(H)=1$, all nonzero iterates have the same
normal closure and the same permutation Galois group.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:common} identifies the graph field with $\C(s)$, where
$X=A(s)$ and $Y=A(a^ds)$. These are equal extensions over the common base
$\C(X)$, not merely extensions of the same degree. Their normal closures in a
fixed algebraic closure are consequently equal. Embeddings of $\C(s)$ over
$\C(X)$ correspond to the roots of $A(S)-X$. The iterate assertion follows
from Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:observations}.
\end{proof}

The theorem transfers nonsolvability, not just the count of algebraic branches.
If the relevant normal-closure group is nonsolvable, no branch of $Y$ is
expressible by radicals over $\C(X)$. A radical expression for one branch would
put its simple extension inside a radical extension; its normal closure would
then have solvable Galois group in characteristic zero with the roots of unity
available. This would contradict the nonsolvable group of the same extension.

This is a statement about an algebraic \emph{function}. It does not say that its
value at every particular algebraic or real input is nonradical. For example, all
our normalized maps take the radical value zero at zero.

\subsection{A classical symmetric-monodromy criterion}

\begin{definition}
A complex polynomial is called \emph{Morse} here if all of its critical points
are simple and their critical values are pairwise distinct.
\end{definition}

\begin{lemma}[Morse polynomials have full symmetric monodromy]
\label{nrr:ed:lem:morse}
If $A\in\C[t]$ is Morse of degree $r\ge2$, then $A(T)-X$ has Galois group $S_r$
over $\C(X)$ in its natural action on the roots.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Remove the finite critical values from the target plane and all their preimages
from the source plane. The polynomial becomes an unbranched covering of degree
$r$. Its source is a complex plane with finitely many points removed and is
connected, so the monodromy action on a fiber is transitive.

At a simple critical point $u$, local coordinates give
$A(t)-A(u)=c(t-u)^2+\text{higher terms}$ with $c\ne0$. A small loop around $A(u)$
therefore exchanges exactly the two local inverse branches. Since no other
critical point has the same critical value, its monodromy permutation is a
single transposition. Such small loops generate the fundamental group of the
punctured target plane, so the monodromy group is generated by transpositions.

Associate an edge to each generating transposition on the $r$ sheets. The orbits
of the generated group are exactly the connected components of this graph.
Transitivity makes it connected; transpositions along a connected graph generate
all of $S_r$. The usual correspondence between algebraic-function monodromy and
the Galois group of the normal closure gives the asserted Galois group.
\end{proof}

The covering-space and monodromy correspondence used here are classical; the
criterion is included to make clear precisely what must be checked in the examples,
not as a claimed new theorem. A reference and historical context appear in
Kreso--Tichy \cite{KT2016}.

\section{Explicit nonradical hyperbolic solutions of order two}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:hyperbolic-family}

\subsection{A closed algebraic equation in every odd degree}

\begin{theorem}[Binomial-coordinate family]
\label{nrr:ed:thm:binomial}
Let $n\ge3$ be odd, let $a\in\R\setminus\{0,1,-1\}$, and put
\begin{equation}
 H_n(t)=t+t^n,\qquad f_{n,a}=H_n\circ(a\id)\circ H_n^{-1},\qquad
 \delta=a-a^n.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-def}
\end{equation}
Then $f_{n,a}$ is a global real-analytic diffeomorphism, with
\begin{equation}
 \mu_{f_{n,a}}(z)=(z-a)(z-a^n),\qquad \algdeg(f_{n,a})=n.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-degrees}
\end{equation}
Its monic ordinary algebraic equation is
\begin{equation}
 \Phi_{n,a}(X,Y)=(Y-a^nX)^n+\delta^{n-1}(Y-aX)=0.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-curve}
\end{equation}
This polynomial is irreducible over $\C(X)$ and has Galois group $S_n$. Thus,
for odd $n\ge5$, $f_{n,a}$ is not expressible by radicals over $\C(x)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Since $n$ is odd,
$H_n'(t)=1+nt^{n-1}>0$ on $\R$, and the odd-degree polynomial is onto. Thus it
has a real-analytic inverse and defines the stated diffeomorphism.

At a coordinate point $x=H_n(t)$, its iterates satisfy
\begin{equation}
 f_{n,a}^{\circ j}(H_n(t))=a^jt+a^{nj}t^n.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-orbits}
\end{equation}
For any polynomial $P$, this gives
\[
 P[f_{n,a}](H_n(t))=P(a)t+P(a^n)t^n.
\]
The monomials $t,t^n$ are linearly independent, and $a\ne a^n$. Therefore the
annihilating ideal is exactly the set of polynomials vanishing at both $a$ and
$a^n$, proving the minimal iterative polynomial assertion.

The first two observations give an unusually simple inverse-coordinate formula:
\begin{equation}
 Y=a^nX+\delta t,\qquad t=\frac{Y-a^nX}{\delta}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-recovery}
\end{equation}
Substitution in $t^n+t-X=0$ proves \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-curve}. Since $H_n'(0)=1$,
Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:normalized} gives degree $n$. The displayed polynomial is
monic of that degree in $Y$, hence is the irreducible minimal polynomial.

It remains to establish its Galois group. The critical points of $H_n$ are the
$n-1$ distinct nonzero roots of $nt^{n-1}=-1$. At such a point $u$,
\[
 H_n(u)=\frac{n-1}{n}u.
\]
Their critical values are therefore distinct. Thus $H_n$ is Morse and has
monodromy $S_n$ by Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:morse}. Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:galois} transfers the
same group to \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-curve}. For $n\ge5$, the symmetric group is
nonsolvable, giving the radical obstruction.
\end{proof}

The source polynomial has discriminant
\begin{equation}
 \Disc_T(T^n+T-X)
 =(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\bigl(n^nX^{n-1}+(n-1)^{n-1}\bigr)
 \qquad(n\text{ odd}).
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-discriminant}
\end{equation}
One way to verify the formula is to use
$\Disc(p)=(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\Res(p,p')$ for monic $p$, evaluate the resultant at
the roots of $p'=nT^{n-1}+1$, and use
$p(u)=(n-1)u/n-X$. Since $n-1$ is even, the factor in parentheses has no real
zero. This agrees with the absence of real critical values for the real inverse.

\begin{example}[A decreasing quintic that satisfies a quadratic iterative equation]
For $n=5$ and $a=-2$, one has $\delta=30$ and
\begin{equation}
 f^{\circ2}(x)+34f(x)+64x=0,
 \qquad
 (f(x)+32x)^5+30^4(f(x)+2x)=0.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:quintic-example}
\end{equation}
The inverse coordinate is $t=(f(x)+32x)/30$. The ordinary algebraic equation has
exact degree five and Galois group $S_5$, although the minimal iterative equation
has order two. For every real $x$ the algebraic equation displayed here has exactly
one real root $f(x)$, because it is an affine change of $t^5+t-x=0$.
This does \emph{not} say that the iterative equation alone has a unique solution;
it also has the affine solutions $-2x$ and $-32x$.
\end{example}

\subsection{Exact distortion and a rational near-reflection sequence}

\begin{proposition}[Distortion of the binomial family]
\label{nrr:ed:prop:hyperbolic-distortion}
Put $b=|a|$. Then
\begin{equation}
 |f_{n,a}'(H_n(t))|
 =b\frac{1+nb^{n-1}t^{n-1}}{1+nt^{n-1}},
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:derivative-hyperbolic}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
 \Lip(f_{n,a})=\max(b,b^n),\quad
 \Lip(f_{n,a}^{-1})=\frac{1}{\min(b,b^n)},\quad
 \Lip(f_{n,a})\Lip(f_{n,a}^{-1})=\max(b,b^{-1})^{n-1}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:distortion-hyperbolic}
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Differentiate the conjugacy. Since $n-1$ is even, $t^{n-1}$ ranges over
$[0,\infty)$. The ratio in \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:derivative-hyperbolic} lies between its
values at zero and at infinity, namely $b$ and $b^n$, and attains or approaches
both endpoints. The mean value theorem identifies the Lipschitz constant of a
$C^1$ map with the supremum of the absolute derivative. Apply the same argument
to the inverse derivative to obtain the formulas.
\end{proof}

For an explicit rational-coefficient sequence, take odd $n\ge5$ and
$a_n=-(1+n^{-2})$. Its distortion is
\[
 (1+n^{-2})^{n-1}<e^{1/n}\longrightarrow1.
\]
Moreover,
\[
 \sup_{x\in\R}|f_{n,a_n}'(x)+1|
 =(1+n^{-2})^n-1\longrightarrow0.
\]
Each member still has algebraic degree $n$ and Galois group $S_n$. The coefficients
of its order-two iterative polynomial are rational, and these polynomials tend
to $(z+1)^2$. The next section strengthens the phenomenon by keeping the iterative
polynomial exactly fixed.

\section{One fixed equation: nonradical involutions near reflection}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:reflection}

\subsection{An exact distortion calculation}

For odd $n\ge3$ and a real parameter $\eps$, define
\begin{equation}
 H_{n,\eps}(t)=t+t^n+\eps t^{n-1}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:perturbed-coordinate}
\end{equation}
Whenever $H_{n,\eps}'>0$ on $\R$, write
$\iota_{n,\eps}=H_{n,\eps}\circ(-\id)\circ H_{n,\eps}^{-1}$.
The even term breaks the odd symmetry that would otherwise reduce this involution
to $-\id$.

\begin{proposition}[A closed symmetric equation and rational recovery]
\label{nrr:ed:prop:reflection-curve}
For $\eps\ne0$, the observations $X=H_{n,\eps}(t)$ and $Y=H_{n,\eps}(-t)$
generate $\C(t)$ and have the irreducible monic algebraic equation
\begin{equation}
 G_{n,\eps}(X,Y)
 =(X+Y)(X+Y+2\eps)^{n-1}-2\eps^n(X-Y)^{n-1}=0.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-curve}
\end{equation}
The coordinate is recovered by
\begin{equation}
 t=\frac{\eps(X-Y)}{X+Y+2\eps}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-recovery}
\end{equation}
If $\eps$ is real and $H_{n,\eps}'>0$ on $\R$, the real zero set of $G_{n,\eps}$
is the graph of $\iota_{n,\eps}$ together with the isolated point
$(-\eps,-\eps)$. The denominator in \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-recovery} never
vanishes on the actual real graph.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Adding and subtracting the two observations gives
\[
 X+Y=2\eps t^{n-1},\qquad X-Y=2t(1+t^{n-1}).
\]
These identities prove both \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-recovery} and
\eqref{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-curve}. The rational recovery gives the field equality,
so the algebraic observation degree is $n$. Since $G_{n,\eps}$ is monic of
degree $n$ in $Y$, it is the irreducible graph equation.

On the real parametrized graph the denominator equals
$2\eps(1+t^{n-1})$, which is nonzero because $n-1$ is even. Conversely, a real
zero $(X,Y)$ with nonzero denominator determines a real $t$ by
\eqref{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-recovery}. Equation~\eqref{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-curve} then gives
$t^{n-1}=(X+Y)/(2\eps)$, and substitution in the preceding sum and difference
identities yields $H_{n,\eps}(t)=X$, $H_{n,\eps}(-t)=Y$.

If the denominator is zero, the graph equation forces $X-Y=0$, so the only
remaining point is $(-\eps,-\eps)$. It does not belong to the real graph:
the decreasing involution has its unique fixed point at zero. It is isolated
in the real zero set, since a sequence of other real zeros tending to it would,
by continuity of the graph, make it a graph point as well.
\end{proof}

At $\eps=0$, the coordinate $t+t^n$ remains Morse, but the two-observation field
collapses to $\C(H_n(t))$ and the involution becomes $-\id$.
The polynomial $G_{n,\eps}$ specializes to $(X+Y)^n$, while the irreducible graph
equation becomes $X+Y=0$. Thus the degree collapse at zero is a loss of information
in the observations, not a collision of critical values of the inverse-coordinate
cover. It also illustrates why a real algebraic zero set should not automatically
be identified with a single selected real branch.

\Needspace{15\baselineskip}
\begin{proposition}[Sharp derivative and distortion formulas]
\label{nrr:ed:prop:reflection-distortion}
Let
\begin{equation}
 \theta=|\eps|\left(\frac{n-2}{n}\right)^{(n-2)/(n-1)}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:theta}
\end{equation}
Then $H_{n,\eps}'>0$ on $\R$ if and only if $\theta<1$. Under this condition,
put $\iota_{n,\eps}=H_{n,\eps}\circ(-\id)\circ H_{n,\eps}^{-1}$ and
$L=(1+\theta)/(1-\theta)$. Then
\begin{align}
 \Lip(\iota_{n,\eps})&=\Lip(\iota_{n,\eps}^{-1})=L,
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-lip}\\
 \Lip(\iota_{n,\eps})\Lip(\iota_{n,\eps}^{-1})&=L^2,
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-distortion}\\
 \sup_{x\in\R}|\iota_{n,\eps}'(x)+1|&=\frac{2\theta}{1-\theta}.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-derivative}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
The case $\eps=0$ is immediate; assume $\eps\ne0$. Write
\[
 H_{n,\eps}'(t)=D(t)+E(t),\qquad
 D(t)=1+nt^{n-1}>0,\qquad E(t)=(n-1)\eps t^{n-2}.
\]
Here $D$ is even and $E$ is odd. For $s=|t|\ge0$,
\[
 \frac{|E(t)|}{D(t)}=
 \frac{(n-1)|\eps|s^{n-2}}{1+ns^{n-1}}.
\]
The ratio vanishes at zero and tends to zero at infinity. Away from zero, the
sign of its derivative is the sign of
\[
 (n-2)-ns^{n-1}.
\]
Thus its maximum occurs at $s^{n-1}=(n-2)/n$ and is exactly $\theta$. Because
$E/D$ takes both signs, $D+E>0$ everywhere is equivalent to $\theta<1$.

Differentiating the defining involution gives
\[
 \iota_{n,\eps}'(H_{n,\eps}(t))
 =-\frac{D(t)-E(t)}{D(t)+E(t)}.
\]
The quantity $E/D$ ranges over $[-\theta,\theta]$, attaining both endpoints.
Consequently the absolute derivative ranges over $[L^{-1},L]$. The Lipschitz
constant is $L$, and the inverse equals the involution itself. Finally,
$L-1\ge1-L^{-1}$, so the largest deviation of the derivative from $-1$ is
$L-1=2\theta/(1-\theta)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Only finitely many parameters fail the monodromy test}

\begin{lemma}[An exact finite exceptional set]
\label{nrr:ed:lem:exceptional}
For each fixed odd $n\ge3$, the polynomial $H_{n,\eps}$ is Morse for all but
finitely many complex parameters $\eps$. The exceptional set does not contain
zero. It is contained in the zero set of the explicitly computable nonzero
polynomial
\begin{equation}
 E_n(\eps)=\Disc_X\!\left(\Disc_t(H_{n,\eps}(t)-X)\right)\in\Z[\eps].
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:exceptional-polynomial}
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Let $D_n(\eps,X)=\Disc_t(H_{n,\eps}(t)-X)$. Its degree in $X$ is $n-1$, with
nonzero constant leading coefficient, independent of $\eps$. Up to a nonzero
constant, it is the product of $X-H_{n,\eps}(u)$ over the roots $u$ of
$H_{n,\eps}'$, counted with their multiplicities. Therefore a squarefree
$D_n(\eps,X)$ implies that every critical point is simple and every critical
value is different from the others.

At $\eps=0$, formula~\eqref{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-discriminant} shows that $D_n(0,X)$ is
squarefree: its constant term is nonzero, and its derivative can vanish at a
root only if that root is zero. Hence $E_n(0)\ne0$. The polynomial $E_n$ is
nonzero and has only finitely many roots. Outside those roots $D_n$ is squarefree,
which gives the asserted Morse condition.
\end{proof}

\begin{theorem}[Nonradical involutions of unbounded degree at fixed iterative order]
\label{nrr:ed:thm:fixed-involution}
For every odd integer $n\ge5$ and every $\eta>0$, there exists a nonzero rational
parameter $\eps$ such that $H=H_{n,\eps}$ defines a global real-analytic
involution $\iota=H\circ(-\id)\circ H^{-1}$ satisfying all of the following:
\begin{equation}
 \mu_\iota(z)=z^2-1,\qquad \algdeg(\iota)=n,\qquad
 \Gal(\iota/\C(x))\cong S_n,
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:fixed-complexity}
\end{equation}
where the Galois group means that of the normal closure in its natural
$n$-sheeted action. In addition,
\begin{equation}
 \Lip(\iota)\Lip(\iota^{-1})<1+\eta,\qquad
 \sup_{x\in\R}|\iota'(x)+1|<\eta.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:near-isometry}
\end{equation}
In particular, $\iota$ is not expressible by radicals over $\C(x)$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
Choose a sufficiently small nonzero rational $\eps$ outside the finite exceptional
set of Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:exceptional}. Such parameters exist in every punctured
neighborhood of zero. Since $\theta\to0$ with $\eps$, Proposition~\ref{nrr:ed:prop:reflection-distortion}
ensures $H'>0$ and both strict bounds in \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:near-isometry}.

By construction, $\iota^{\circ2}=\id$. The polynomial $H$ is not odd because
$\eps\ne0$, so $\iota\ne-\id$. A monic linear iterative annihilator would have
the form $z-c$, making $\iota(x)=cx$. Since $\iota$ is decreasing and an
involution, this would force $c=-1$, which has just been excluded. Therefore
its minimal iterative polynomial is exactly $z^2-1$.

The nonzero coefficient of $t^{n-1}$ in $H$ rules out every nonlinear odd right
factor by Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:adjacent}. Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:odd-factor} then gives
$\C(H(t),H(-t))=\C(t)$ and exact algebraic degree $n$. The selected parameter
makes $H$ Morse, so Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:morse} and Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:galois} give $S_n$.
Its nonsolvability for $n\ge5$ proves the radical obstruction.
\end{proof}

This gives a negative answer to any proposed bound on algebraic degree in terms
of iterative order and a distortion upper bound strictly larger than one, even
when the iterative polynomial itself is fixed. It is also stronger than a
statement involving merely convergence of polynomial coefficients: all these
functions solve exactly $f\circ f=x$.

The derivative bound gives a global normalized estimate as well. Since
$\iota(0)=0$, the mean value theorem implies
\begin{equation}
 |\iota(x)+x|\le
 \frac{2\theta}{1-\theta}|x|\qquad(x\in\R).
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:global-near-reflection}
\end{equation}
Thus a sequence of such nonradical functions can converge to reflection in
$C^1$ on every compact interval, with a uniform derivative estimate on the entire
line. No convergence of all higher derivatives is asserted.

\subsection{A terminating rational certificate search}

The existential parameter choice is effective. Fix $n$ and choose an integer
$M_0\ge2$ large enough for the desired conservative distortion bounds. Test
\begin{equation}
 \eps_M=\frac{1}{(n-1)M},\qquad M=M_0,M_0+1,\ldots.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:epsilon-search}
\end{equation}
For each $M$, compute
$D_n(\eps_M,X)$ exactly and test
\begin{equation}
 \gcd\bigl(D_n(\eps_M,X),\partial_XD_n(\eps_M,X)\bigr)=1.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:disc-gcd}
\end{equation}
Only finitely many values fail, so this search terminates. It is unnecessary to
construct the full bivariate exceptional polynomial $E_n$.

Since $\theta<|\eps_M|<1/M$, a passing parameter has the rational certificates
\begin{equation}
 \Lip(\iota)\le\frac{M+1}{M-1},\qquad
 \Lip(\iota)\Lip(\iota^{-1})\le\left(\frac{M+1}{M-1}\right)^2.
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:coarse-cert}
\end{equation}
The sharper bound obtained by replacing $1/M$ with $|\eps_M|$ is also valid.
The exact value is given by \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:theta}--\eqref{nrr:ed:eq:reflection-distortion}.

With $M=100$, the exact test passes for $n=3,5,7,9,11$, using respectively
$\eps=1/200$, $1/400$, $1/600$, $1/800$, $1/1000$. The common conservative distortion bound
is $10201/9801$. These computations certify the listed examples; the theorem for
every odd degree rests on the finite-exception argument, not on this finite list.
For $n=5$, the sharper rational distortion bound is $160801/159201$.

\subsection{The apparent symmetry paradox}

There is no contradiction between involutivity and a nonsolvable normal closure.
The map $t\mapsto-t$ induces an automorphism of the rational normalization and
exchanges the two functions $X=H(t)$ and $Y=H(-t)$. It does not fix $X$, so it is
not an automorphism over the base field $\C(X)$.

The irreducible graph equation can be chosen symmetric in $X,Y$. Indeed, swapping
$X,Y$ preserves its irreducible curve. Its defining polynomial is therefore
carried to a scalar multiple of itself; the scalar is $1$ or $-1$. The second
possibility would make it divisible by $X-Y$, forcing the curve to be the
identity graph, which a decreasing involution is not. Symmetry of this algebraic
relation is perfectly compatible with an $n$-sheeted projection of monodromy $S_n$.

\section{Verification, limitations, and implications}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:verification}

\subsection{Independent exact checks}

The accompanying \nolinkurl{code/04-exact-degree-verify.py} (delivered as
\texttt{code/verify.py}; the file of that name here is Part~I's verifier) implements the right-factor algorithm
using SymPy and exact rational or Gaussian-rational arithmetic. Its principal
cross-check is not a second encoding of the same support criterion. Independently,
it computes the degree in $T$ of
\begin{equation}
 \gcd\bigl(H(T)-H(t),\ H(aT)-H(at)\bigr).
 \label{nrr:ed:eq:fiber-gcd}
\end{equation}
Over the generic parameter $t$, this degree is the number of points in a common
fiber of the two observations, namely $d(H,a)$. Polynomial gcd computation is
thus compared directly with the predicted maximal right-factor degree.

The actual completed tests are summarized below. All passed with Python 3.13.5
and SymPy 1.14.0. The random tests use the fixed seed 20260929; they are exactly
reproducible and use no floating-point root approximations.

\begin{center}
\small
\begin{tabularx}{\textwidth}{@{}Xr@{}}
\toprule
Test family & Cases \\
\midrule
Every nonempty positive support in $\{1,\ldots,6\}$, coefficients one;
 five multipliers $2,-2,1/2,-1,1$ & 315 \\
Thirty seeded monic rational polynomials of degree $2$--$9$;
 three multipliers $-2,-1,1/2$ & 90 \\
Structured decomposition, support-gcd, reflection, and fourth-root cases & 8 \\
Every divisor realization for odd coordinate degrees $1$ through $15$ & 18 \\
Binomial family identities and discriminants, odd degrees $3$ through $15$ & 7 \\
Explicit cubic rational-coordinate recovery & 1 \\
Resultant multiplicity for $H=t^6+t^3$, $a=2$ & 1 \\
Near-reflection resultant identities in degrees $3$ and $5$ & 2 \\
Critical-value and explicit graph-equation certificates for the near-reflection family & 5 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabularx}
\end{center}

The first row comprises 63 distinct support polynomials, each with five
multipliers. The second row is a seeded list of 30 generated polynomials rather
than a claim of exhaustive enumeration of all polynomials in those degrees.
Some structured examples can overlap other test families; the table counts test
instances, not disjoint mathematical objects.

The binomial-family checks substitute the coordinate into the ordinary algebraic
equation, verify the order-two iterate identity, check the generic common-fiber
gcd, and compare exact discriminants with \eqref{nrr:ed:eq:binomial-discriminant}.
The resultant test verifies that the resultant is the third power of an
irreducible quadratic graph equation in the nonprimitive example $H=t^6+t^3$.
The near-reflection checks compute and record the critical-value discriminants
and test their squarefreeness exactly. They also verify the symmetric graph equation
and rational recovery by substitution; in degrees three and five, the resultant
is independently compared with the negative of $G_{n,\eps}$.

Full output is in \nolinkurl{data/04-exact-degree-verification.json} and
\nolinkurl{data/04-exact-degree-verification.txt} (delivered as
\texttt{results/verification.json} and \texttt{results/verification.txt}).
(Merge note: in the write phase, 29 September 2026, the verifier was rerun in a
scratch copy with its delivered name restored, under Python~3.13.5 and
SymPy~1.14.0, in about 11~seconds; all checks passed, and both outputs equal
the shipped records apart from Windows line endings.) These are finite implementation checks and
example certificates. They do not prove the universal field theorem, the
all-degree realization theorem, or the existence theorem for every small
distortion tolerance; those assertions rest on the proofs in the article.

\subsection{What has and has not been resolved}

The exact-degree question is fully answered for polynomial coordinates and
nonzero scalar multipliers. [Merge note: for torsion multipliers the answer
is the terminating exact algorithm of Proposition~\ref{nrr:ed:prop:algorithm},
with a complete classification for involutions
(Corollary~\ref{nrr:ed:cor:odd-factor}, Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:divisors});
no closed formula is claimed there.] The answer is explicit in the infinite-order case,
and a finite maximal-right-factor computation in the torsion case. For the
normalized hyperbolic maps in the repository question, it reduces to the single
formula $\algdeg(f)=\deg H$, with no exceptional decompositions.

We also answer the family question when all maps use one fixed coordinate:
two distinct hyperbolic orbit observations generate the whole coordinate field,
and every other dilation is rationally expressible from them. We do not claim
to classify field intersections or composita arising from two unrelated
coordinate polynomials.

The fixed-involution examples separate algebraic and dynamical complexity in a
particularly sharp way. They have the same exact iterative polynomial, uniformly
controlled distortion, and arbitrarily large nonsolvable algebraic degree. This
rules out a complexity bound based only on those data. It does not rule out
bounds using additional restrictions, such as coefficient heights, a prescribed
non-torsion multiplier, a bound on coordinate degree, or a prescribed ramification
configuration.

No result here is a classification of all continuous solutions of an iterative
equation, all smooth involutions, or all possible real algebraic diffeomorphisms.
The domain is a polynomial-coordinate family. The assertion that every sufficiently
smooth non-involutive decreasing solution belongs to that family is part of the
source report, not a theorem reproved here. No positive-characteristic analogue,
multivariate analogue, or general rational-coordinate analogue is assumed.

\subsection{Arithmetic interpretation}

For the rational-coefficient examples, the monic graph equations have rational
coefficients. Their geometric Galois group over $\C(X)$ is $S_n$, so the
arithmetic group over $\Q(X)$ is also $S_n$: it acts on the same $n$ roots,
contains the geometric group, and is contained in $S_n$. Thus the obstruction
is not introduced by allowing arbitrary real coefficients.

Individual rational specializations $X=x_0$ can have smaller Galois groups and
can even make the selected value rational. The present argument does not
classify those exceptional inputs. Separating generic functional nonsolvability
from arithmetic specialization is essential for an accurate interpretation.

\section{Further research questions}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:questions}

These questions continue the results proved here. They are stated as research
targets, not as claims that every related special case is absent from the literature.

\subsection{Effective complexity of two-observation recovery}

The existence of a rational $R(X,Y)$ recovering $t$ is exact, and subresultants
give an algorithm. Determine optimal bounds for the numerator and denominator
degrees, coefficient heights, and bit complexity in terms of $\deg H$ and the
input size. The generic subresultant bound need not be sharp for a dilated pair.
A useful goal is a canonical reduced reconstruction certificate and a proof
that it is minimal under a natural bidegree ordering.

\subsection{Real charts and conditioning}

The function-field reconstruction can have denominators that vanish on the real
graph even though the real inverse coordinate is smooth. Characterize when one
rational chart suffices globally, as in Example~\ref{nrr:ed:ex:recovery}; otherwise bound
the number of charts needed. Then compare algebraic degree with the condition
number of the coordinate-recovery map. The binomial identity shows that high
degree and easy two-observation recovery can coexist, but its denominator
$\delta=a-a^n$ becomes small near a resonant multiplier.

\subsection{A fast torsion decomposition algorithm}

The divisor-by-divisor algorithm is finite but not optimized. Can the residue-class
support restriction $W=t^rR(t^q)$ be imposed during candidate construction to reduce
coefficient growth or avoid most divisor tests? Determine the bit complexity for
rational and algebraic-number input, including a compact certificate for the
maximality of the returned factor. This is a more precise target than a generic
polynomial decomposition implementation.

\subsection{Monodromy at exceptional perturbation parameters}

The discriminant method proves that the near-reflection family has only finitely
many non-Morse parameters in each degree. Determine those parameters, the collision
patterns of their critical values, and their resulting monodromy groups. The exact
positivity range $\theta<1$ is already known from Proposition~\ref{nrr:ed:prop:reflection-distortion};
it is natural to ask which monodromy degenerations can occur inside that range.
A uniform description would turn the terminating rational search into a direct
closed parameter choice.

\subsection{Arithmetic specialization and radical values}

For $\Phi_{n,a}$ and for the near-reflection graph equations, characterize rational
inputs where the specialized Galois group is smaller than $S_n$, and determine
which selected real values are solvable by radicals. The generic field result
alone cannot answer this. Exact specialization certificates, rather than numerical
root plots, would connect the present analysis to the repository's radical-solvability
and polynomial-formula programs. [Merge note: these are the collection report
\nolinkurl{galois-theory-and-radicals/specialization-safe-radical-solvers} and
the Lean project \nolinkurl{Algebra/PolynomialFormulas}, whose
\nolinkurl{AbelRuffini.lean} proves that a fixed rational quintic has a root
not expressible by radicals; neither is used in Part~IV.]

\subsection{Optimal regularity thresholds revisited}

The source report's question about the least differentiability forcing a global
polynomial coordinate remains open here. The exact-degree result supplies a new
observable after that threshold: coordinate degree equals algebraic degree for
hyperbolic normalized maps. Can finite jet data or the minimal iterative spectrum
control this degree before a full coordinate is constructed? Any proposed argument
must keep the involution branch separate, in light of Theorem~\ref{nrr:ed:thm:fixed-involution}.

\subsection{Different coordinates and field intersections}
\label{nrr:ed:q-coordinates}

We have classified all dilations of one coordinate through a common rational
function field. For two polynomials $H,G$, study the fields generated by
$H(aH^{-1}(x))$ and $G(bG^{-1}(x))$ over $\C(x)$. Determine when their normal
closures coincide, intersect nontrivially, or are linearly disjoint. This is the
unresolved part of the repository's broader question about algebraically related
coordinate descriptions; ordinary compositional equivalence is only one mechanism.

\subsection{Rational coordinates and multiple poles}

The polynomial proof exploits a unique pole at infinity and uniqueness of a
normalized right factor of fixed degree. For rational coordinates, a common
intermediate generator can have several poles, and that uniqueness argument no
longer transfers unchanged. Identify the correct replacement for the dilation
common-field theorem in terms of pole configurations and finite symmetries.
Even a complete two-pole or Laurent-polynomial case would be a substantive extension.

\subsection{Higher-dimensional observations}

For a polynomial map $H:\C^m\to\C^m$ and a diagonal linear transformation $A$,
consider the field generated by the components of $H(t)$ and $H(At)$. Determine
hypotheses ensuring recovery of $\C(t_1,\ldots,t_m)$ and quantify the number of
observations needed when two do not suffice. Resonances among coordinate weights
and the failure of one-variable L\"uroth theory are genuine new obstacles, not
minor notational changes.

\subsection{A staged Lean or Rocq formalization}

A practical first milestone is Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:unique}, using polynomial coefficients
and degree arithmetic only. Next formalize the covariance deduction assuming a
polynomial intermediate-field generator, then the support-gcd corollary. A separate
layer can certify the executable right-factor search and the common-fiber gcd tests.
The analytic realization and distortion formulas need only one-variable calculus.

The final monodromy step should be isolated explicitly: formalize the elementary
critical-point calculation and either import a precisely stated Morse-monodromy
theorem or prove the covering-space correspondence. This separation would make
clear which degree results are kernel-checked before the more demanding Galois
and analytic infrastructure is completed. No such formalization is included in
this package. (As of 29 September 2026 none of these stages has been started
in ProveIt.)

\section{Conclusion of Part~IV}
\label{nrr:ed:sec:conclusion}

The exact algebraic degree of a polynomial-coordinate iterative solution is a
common-field problem. Polynomial intermediate fields and normalized right-factor
rigidity force the common coordinate to be a dilation eigenfunction. Infinite-order
dilations leave only monomial common factors, producing the support-gcd formula
and exact degree $\deg H$ whenever the linear term is nonzero. Torsion multipliers
retain residue-class factors, which explain every degree drop and give a complete
odd-divisor realization theorem for polynomial-coordinate involutions.

The same field identity transfers normal closures and recovers an entire dilation
family from two observations. Its consequences go beyond sharpening an upper
bound: order-two iterative equations can have solutions of arbitrarily large
nonradical degree, and even the fixed equation $f\circ f=x$ admits such solutions
arbitrarily close to reflection. Topological simplicity, small distortion, rational
normalization, and low iterative order therefore do not force algebraic simplicity.

\section{Provenance, dependencies, and claim audit of Part~IV}
\label{nrr:ed:app:provenance}

\subsection{Repository snapshot}

The repository was inspected at the commit
\begin{center}
\small\texttt{11e1e900114e7c0cfdcd19fe346ddb4f2852dbc5}.
\end{center}
The relevant directory is
\begin{quote}\small\ttfamily
\path{SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/log-concavity-and-unimodality/nonreal-roots-in-iterative-equations/}.
\end{quote}
Its merged \texttt{article.tex} was identified by the Git blob
\begin{center}\small\ttfamily
b52eba816e4c6a108d740e4e4e0edd5b1f8631ec.
\end{center}
The README records the three-part merger and the original Part III title
\emph{The Decreasing Spectral Core: Complete minimal spectra, finite-smoothness
rigidity, and global polynomial conjugacy}. The original Part III source was
also inspected through the user's prior-file library as
\texttt{article(20260929-151305).tex}. Its Section 11.7 identifies the exact-degree
question; its discussion of rational solutions supplies the context for the
existing degree upper bound. This source is an unrefereed companion, not an
independent confirmation of the repository's claims.
(The manuscript cites the report as the GitHub tree of that path at that commit,
\url{https://github.com/VladimirReshetnikov/ProveIt/tree/11e1e900114e7c0cfdcd19fe346ddb4f2852dbc5/SetTheory/Cardinals/docs/reports/log-concavity-and-unimodality/nonreal-roots-in-iterative-equations};
that bibliography entry is replaced here by references to Parts~I--III. Its
Section~11.7 is Part~III's Section~35.7, and its provenance note is shipped
verbatim as \nolinkurl{04-exact-degree-PROVENANCE.md}.)

\subsection{Dependency boundary}

The common-field theorem uses the classical L\"uroth theorem, elementary facts
about rational maps and their degrees, and the coefficient comparison proved in
Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:unique}. The support-gcd, torsion, and degree results then follow
without any iterative-equation classification theorem. The finite computation
uses these proved criteria but is separately checked against polynomial gcds.

The analytic examples additionally use the real inverse function theorem and the
mean value theorem. Their degree claims follow from the common-field results.
Their Galois claims use the classical complex covering-space/monodromy correspondence
and the Morse criterion, whose local and permutation-group argument is given in
Lemma~\ref{nrr:ed:lem:morse}. The all-degree perturbation construction uses only a nonzero
polynomial discriminant and the finiteness of its exceptional parameter set.

Neither the computations nor the source report's asserted smooth classification
are premises of the central common-field theorem. No Lean or Rocq theorem is
claimed to certify this manuscript. The paper resolves a question explicitly
left open in the inspected repository, not a separately established long-standing
open problem in the published literature.

\subsection{Extent of the literature check}

The relevant intermediate-field and equal-degree decomposition statements in
Zieve--M\"uller \cite{ZM2008}, including the credited coefficient argument,
were inspected in the full preprint. The Morse-monodromy discussion in
Kreso--Tichy \cite{KT2016} was inspected as well. Draga--Morawiec
\cite{DM2016} supplies the iterative-equation context; no priority claim
about order-two constructions is inferred from its abstract or from an absence
of a particular statement there.

The search is not a complete historical survey of polynomial decomposition,
algebraic involutions, or algebraic functional equations. Accordingly, the paper
claims self-contained proofs of the stated extension of the ProveIt question,
not proof that each consequence first appears here. Independent mathematical
review and a broader bibliographic comparison remain appropriate.

\section{Reproduction guide for Part~IV}
\label{nrr:ed:app:reproduce}

The package contains the article source and PDF, the exact checker, its recorded
outputs, a Makefile, dependency information, and a checksum ledger. With the tested
SymPy version installed, run from the package root:
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/verify.py --output-dir results
latexmk -pdf -interaction=nonstopmode article.tex
\end{lstlisting}
(Merge note: those commands apply to the delivered package. Here the article is
Part~IV of this report, the checker is \nolinkurl{code/04-exact-degree-verify.py},
its outputs are \nolinkurl{data/04-exact-degree-verification.json} and
\nolinkurl{data/04-exact-degree-verification.txt}, and the tested SymPy version is
pinned in \nolinkurl{data/04-exact-degree-requirements.txt}; the manuscript's PDF
and checksum ledger are not shipped. Run in this report's directory, the first
command would call Part~I's verifier \texttt{code/verify.py}, which rejects the
option \texttt{-{}-output-dir} with a usage error; the delivered Makefile, shipped
as \nolinkurl{code/04-exact-degree-Makefile}, runs that command in its
\texttt{verify} target and builds or cleans \texttt{article.*} in place in its
other targets. In the delivered layout the default output directory
\texttt{results} overwrites the recorded outputs. Rerun the checker in a scratch
copy instead, for example with \texttt{W} a scratch directory:
\begin{lstlisting}
mkdir -p "$W/04/code"
cp code/04-exact-degree-verify.py "$W/04/code/verify.py"
cd "$W/04"
uv run --no-project --with sympy==1.14.0 python code/verify.py --output-dir "$W/04/rerun"
\end{lstlisting}
The checker uses \texttt{assert}, so do not run it under \texttt{python -O}.
The report's \texttt{README.md} records this rerun.)
The checker does not require network access. The TeX source uses standard packages
including Latin Modern (in the delivered manuscript; this report is set in newtx), AMS mathematics, microtype, hyperref, and listings.
No external image or proprietary font file is required.

For clarity, the main algorithm is summarized independently of the implementation:
\begin{lstlisting}
input: nonconstant H of degree n, nonzero a
for d in divisors(n), in decreasing order:
    m = n/d
    W = t^d
    for j = 1,...,d-1:
        c = (coeff(H/lc(H),n-j) - coeff(W^m,n-j))/m
        W = W + c*t^(d-j)
    if W(a*t) != a^d*W(t): continue
    if exact_composition_division(H,W) succeeds:
        return (W, n/d)
\end{lstlisting}
The returned second component is the algebraic observation degree; the degree
of $W$ is the discarded common-fiber multiplicity. Keeping these two outputs
distinct avoids a frequent inversion of the degree formula.

\appendix
\section{Source and status audit}
\label{app:sources}

The literature search was performed on 20 September 2026. The primary
sources below were used to identify the question and delimit the claims,
not to replace the proofs in this article.

\paragraph{The target.}
Draga--Morawiec \cite{DM2016}, Problem~6.1, is present in the retrieved
HTML and in the rendered page~13 of arXiv:1503.00570v2. The preprint has
14 pages. Its published bibliographic record is
\emph{Aequationes Mathematicae} 90 (2016), 935--950, with DOI
\texttt{10.1007/s00010-016-0420-4}. The source is about preserving the
solution equation after reducing the characteristic polynomial, rather
than merely preserving the existence of at least one solution.

\paragraph{Subsequent context.}
Draga's note \cite{D2016} gives another order-reduction theorem and
mentions the unrestricted nonreal-root issue in its final remarks.
The later paper \emph{Means of iterates} \cite{DM2018} treats a particular
family of iterative mean equations and uses characteristic-root
analysis. A 2021 paper \cite{TLM2021} studies a class of fourth-order
equations under restrictions on the characteristic roots. These sources
show continuing work on the subject; none of the retrieved material
established priority for the counterexample developed here.

\paragraph{Initial lead.}
Petrov's question \cite{Petrov2016} was the initial candidate. Its displayed
answer count was zero in the retrieved page. An unanswered question page
is not, by itself, proof that no published solution exists elsewhere.

\paragraph{Search limitation.}
The search included the authors' names, polynomial-like iterative
equations, nonreal-root elimination, equal-modulus roots, and the numbered
problem. Some returned results were irrelevant and were not used. The
search was not a complete citation-database survey. Thus the report does
not attach a first-discovery claim to the elementary construction or to
the classification theorem. The complete source identifiers and this
status distinction are also recorded in
\texttt{notes/literature\_audit.md}.

\section{Reproduction, selection, and exclusion record}
\label{app:reproduce}

From the archive's top-level directory, the following commands reproduce
the exact certificates and, with the optional plotting dependency, the
numerical illustrations:
\begin{lstlisting}
python code/verify.py
python code/analytic_demo.py --plots
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
pdflatex -interaction=nonstopmode -halt-on-error article.tex
\end{lstlisting}
The supplied figure files allow the PDF to be compiled without running
the plotting script. No bibliography processor is required.

The exact verifier normalizes positive rational numbers by multiplication
or division by eight, not by taking an approximate logarithm. This avoids
wrong branch choices at powers of eight. Its independent conjugacy
implementation also uses rational operations. The recurrence polynomials
in the JSON certificate are stored with coefficients in \emph{ascending}
order, so, for example, $z^3-8$ is represented by
\texttt{[-8, 0, 0, 1]}.

The random area draw was:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
Number of listed areas & 80\\
Generator call & \texttt{secrets.randbelow(80)}\\
Returned zero-based index & 17\\
Selected one-based index & 18\\
Selected area & Functional equations\\
Redraws & 0\\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
An entropy-backed draw is recorded rather than seed-replayed; running a
new draw is not expected to reproduce the same index. The full 80-element
list is in \texttt{data/selection.json}.

The excluded input was the 1,095-line file \texttt{manifest(1).tex}, with
SHA-256 fingerprint
\begin{center}
\small\ttfamily
729fb6e3aef3f30c7c5284c4296681fba72b6d6af68211f178ad0bc3c5d15b41.
\end{center}
The file was reviewed as an exclusion list; this report does not certify
the correctness or priority of its individual mathematical claims.
A suggested new manifest entry is supplied separately so that future
selections can exclude the present target as well.
